Recipe book

Breakfast Pancakes

A booklet of the cakes this journal tags for breakfast.

Breakfast here means the meal the entry itself names: a stack, a dosa, a johnnycake, a hotcake. It is not a claim that nobody eats the same cake at supper.

Print the booklet if you want the recipes in one sheaf. The field-guide page for each cake has the longer history.

United States · No chili heat · about 8 ployes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Acadian Ployes

Mild buckwheat, soft on one side, with a few holes like a thick crepe.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 ployes

History

Ployes are Acadian buckwheat pancakes from the St. John Valley of northern Maine and neighboring New Brunswick. Acadians are the French-speaking people who were expelled from Nova Scotia by the British in 1755 and who rebuilt communities in places that include Louisiana and the Maritimes. In the north, buckwheat ployes stayed a daily bread. They are cooked on one side, not flipped, and eaten with butter, cretons, or maple syrup.

Cultural background

A ploye is closer to a bread than to a dessert pancake. Families keep a mix of buckwheat and wheat, though a pure buckwheat version is made. This recipe uses buckwheat only, with baking powder, so it is gluten-free.

Why it tastes this way

Buckwheat grows in a short, cool season, which is why it became the local grain. A wet batter on a hot griddle sets holes on top, a sign the water is steaming out. Cooking one side keeps the top soft for wrapping or for butter. The Acadian north and the Cajun south share a history of expulsion and do not share this bread. Louisiana’s rice calas are a different descendant.

How it is made

Buckwheat flour, water, salt, and baking powder make a thin batter that is cooked on one side.

Authentic touch. Do not flip them. When the top is set and full of holes, the ploye is done. A flip dries it into a cracker.

How it is used

Butter, maple syrup, or a savory spread. They can be wrapped around a filling like a soft bread.

Field notes

  • Ployes are cooked on one side. Holes on top are the sign they are ready.
  • They belong to the Acadian north, not to the Cajun rice kitchen, though the histories of the people are connected.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup light buckwheat flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/4 cups cold water
  • Butter, to serve

Steps

  1. Stir flour, baking powder, and salt. Whisk in water to a thin batter.
  2. Heat a lightly oiled skillet over medium-high.
  3. Pour a thin round and do not spread it too nervously. Holes should form.
  4. Cook until the top is set and the underside is spotted brown. Do not flip.
  5. Stack and cover with a towel so they stay flexible.
  6. Serve with butter.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A mix with wheat flour is common in Acadian cupboards and is not gluten-free.
  • Cretons, a pork spread, are a traditional partner and add meat.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • Side dish
  • butter
  • maple syrup
  • cretons
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · Medium · about 8 adai

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Adai

A thick, savory pancake of mixed dals and rice, spiced with chile and curry leaf.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 adai

History

Adai is a Tamil pancake of rice and several lentils, coarser and spicier than dosa, often cooked for dinner as much as breakfast. A common accompaniment is jaggery and butter, or avial, a vegetable stew. The batter is ground and may rest a few hours. It is not a thin crisp dosa.

Cultural background

The mix of dals is the point: toor, chana, and moong with a little urad, or a similar house blend. Curry leaves and dried chile go into the grind.

Why it tastes this way

Several lentils make it taste beany, hearty, and more savory than a rice-dominant dosa. Chile and curry leaf are ground in, so the heat is in the cake, not only in a side chutney. A coarser grind keeps it textured. Jaggery on the side is a Tamil contrast, sweet against a spicy pancake, and it surprises people who expect only chutney.

How it is made

Soaked rice and mixed dals are ground coarse with chile and curry leaves and cooked as thick pancakes.

Authentic touch. Grind it coarse, like wet sand, not a smooth dosa cream. Cook it thicker, with oil, until the center is no longer raw. A thin adai dries out and misses the point.

How it is used

With jaggery, or with a vegetable stew, and a spoon of yogurt if you eat dairy.

Field notes

  • Adai is thicker and spicier than dosa, and it uses several dals.
  • Jaggery on the side is a real way to eat it.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup rice
  • 2 tablespoons toor dal
  • 2 tablespoons chana dal
  • 2 tablespoons moong dal
  • 1 tablespoon urad dal
  • 2 dried red chiles
  • 8 curry leaves
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Soak the rice and dals together 4 hours. Drain.
  2. Grind coarse with chiles, curry leaves, salt, and a little water.
  3. Rest 2 hours if you can. It is good the same day.
  4. Spread thick rounds on a hot oiled tawa and cook, turning, until both sides are browned and the center is cooked.
  5. Serve with jaggery or a stew.
  6. Oil the edges so they crisp.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A smoother grind makes it more like dosa and less like adai.
  • Fewer chiles make it milder. The classic is noticeably spiced.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • jaggery
  • avial
  • yogurt
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free

Denmark · No chili heat · about 28 spheres

Flying Pancakes of the World

Denmark · Northern Europe

Æbleskiver

Spherical pancakes, browned in a special pan, dusted with sugar.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 6 · about 28 spheres

History

Æbleskiver are Danish round pancakes cooked in a cast-iron pan with hemispherical wells. The name means apple slices, and some recipes tuck in a bit of apple, though many modern æbleskiver have no apple inside. They are a Christmas and winter treat in Denmark and at Danish American church events. The pan is essential.

Cultural background

Powdered sugar and jam, especially raspberry, are the finish. They are turned with a knitting needle or a skewer so the sphere browns all over.

Why it tastes this way

The well traps steam and batter so the cake becomes a ball instead of a disk. A little cardamom is a common Danish perfume. Sugar on the outside clings to the crisp shell. The apple in the name records an older filling. Leaving the apple out is common now and should be admitted, not hidden.

How it is made

A thick batter is cooked in an æbleskiver pan, turned until round, and sugared.

Authentic touch. Fill the wells two-thirds full. Turn the cakes before the batter on top is completely dry, so the seam seals into a ball. Too late, and you get a hemisphere.

How it is used

Hot, with powdered sugar and jam.

Field notes

  • The name refers to apples, but many æbleskiver today have none inside.
  • Danish American festivals still cook them as heritage food.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1/4 teaspoon cardamom
  • 1 large egg, separated
  • 1 cup milk
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter
  • Powdered sugar, to serve

Steps

  1. Mix the batter with the yolk. Whip the white and fold it in.
  2. Butter an æbleskiver pan over medium heat.
  3. Fill the wells two-thirds full.
  4. Turn the cakes with a skewer as they set, until they are round and cooked through.
  5. Dust with powdered sugar.
  6. Serve with jam.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A small piece of cooked apple in the center is the older idea.
  • Dutch poffertjes use a similar pan and a different batter.

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Holiday table
  • Breakfast
  • powdered sugar
  • raspberry jam
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Finland · No chili heat · one 8-inch dish

Flying Pancakes of the World

Finland · Northern Europe

Åland Pancake

A baked rice and cardamom pancake from the Åland Islands.

30 min prep · 45 min cook · Serves 8 · one 8-inch dish

History

Åland pancake is a baked rice porridge pancake from the Åland Islands, which are Swedish-speaking and autonomous within Finland. Cardamom and rice porridge are the base. It is a local dessert and café food, served with jam, often prune jam, and whipped cream. It is distinct from a thin lettu and from Gotland’s saffron pancake, though both are baked rice cakes of the Baltic.

Cultural background

The porridge is cooked, cooled a little, enriched, and baked until the top browns. Prune jam is the usual partner. Another dark jam is a fair substitute if you name it honestly.

Why it tastes this way

Rice cooked in milk tastes sweet and substantial, and cardamom perfumes it the way it perfumes Nordic buns. Åland’s position between Sweden and Finland shows in the language and in this cake, which sits beside both countries’ oven pancakes and keeps its own name. Prune jam is tart and dark, a preserved fruit that stands up to cream.

How it is made

Rice porridge is mixed with egg, sugar, and cardamom and baked in a buttered dish.

Authentic touch. Cook the rice fully before baking, or the finished cake has hard grains. The bake is for setting and browning, not for cooking raw rice.

How it is used

With prune jam or another dark jam, and whipped cream.

Field notes

  • Åland pancake is a named island dish, not a generic baked custard.
  • Prune jam is the traditional fruit partner.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup short-grain rice
  • 2 cups milk
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1/4 cup sugar
  • 1 teaspoon cardamom
  • 1 tablespoon flour
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • Butter for the dish

Steps

  1. Simmer rice and milk until the rice is soft and the porridge is thick. Cool 10 minutes.
  2. Stir in eggs, sugar, cardamom, flour, and salt.
  3. Pour into a buttered dish.
  4. Bake at 375°F about 40 minutes until set and brown.
  5. Rest 10 minutes.
  6. Serve with prune jam and cream.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Gotland’s pancake uses saffron. This one uses cardamom. Do not treat them as one recipe.
  • A gluten-free version can skip the spoon of flour. The rice is the body.

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • prune jam
  • whipped cream
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Alaska Sourdough Hotcakes

Mildly sour, sturdy cakes from a live starter.

20 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Sourdough starters were how prospectors and settlers leavened bread in Alaska and the Yukon when fresh yeast was hard to keep. Hotcakes from that starter became a camp and roadhouse breakfast. “Sourdough” even became a nickname for an old-timer. The cake depends on a living starter. This is a home version for someone who already keeps one, not a story that every Alaska breakfast used the same recipe.

Cultural background

A spoon of baking soda is often added to the batter just before cooking. It reacts with the acid and lifts the cake faster than the starter alone would on a griddle.

Why it tastes this way

A starter tastes sour because lactic acid bacteria work alongside the yeast. That acid is the flavor people mean by sourdough, and it is also what the soda reacts with. In a cold place, a starter kept near the stove was more reliable than a cake of perishable yeast. The hotcake is a faster use of the same pot that made bread.

How it is made

Active sourdough starter is mixed with flour, water, egg, and salt, rested briefly, and finished with baking soda.

Authentic touch. Stir the soda in just before you cook. If it sits, the bubbles fade and the cakes are heavy. The griddle should be ready.

How it is used

Butter and syrup, or butter and jam. They are a trail breakfast brought indoors.

Field notes

  • You need a lively starter. A neglected jar in the back of the fridge will not lift these.
  • The soda is a griddle trick. It is not a sign the starter failed.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup active sourdough starter
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • Oil for the griddle

Steps

  1. Stir starter, flour, water, egg, sugar, and salt. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Heat the griddle.
  3. Stir in the baking soda.
  4. Cook immediately on an oiled medium griddle.
  5. Flip when the surface is set.
  6. Serve hot. The lift does not wait.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A purely yeast-risen batter with no soda is slower and more breadlike.
  • Milk instead of water is richer and adds dairy.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • butter
  • syrup
  • jam
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · about 10 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Amish Buttermilk Pancakes

A tall, plain buttermilk cake with a tender crumb.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 5 · about 10 pancakes

History

Amish and Mennonite communities in Pennsylvania, Ohio, and Indiana kept farmhouse baking into the present: buttermilk, eggs, and flour, cooked on a griddle for a large household. Their pancakes are not a secret recipe. They are a careful version of the American buttermilk pancake, often served in big breakfasts with fried potatoes, eggs, and fruit preserves. This page describes that table without turning a living religious community into a costume.

Cultural background

The cooking is practical. Batters are big. Sweetness is usually at the table, as syrup, sorghum, or fruit butter, because the same breakfast feeds people who will work.

Why it tastes this way

Buttermilk and soda make a high cake that still tastes a little sour, which is what keeps a plain pancake from tasting like sweet bread. A farm that had a cow had buttermilk. The lack of vanilla and spice is a choice: the cake is fuel and comfort, and the preserves do the perfuming.

How it is made

Flour, buttermilk, egg, and leavener are stirred briefly and cooked as a generous stack.

Authentic touch. Cook them on a steady medium griddle. A smoking pan makes a bitter crust and a raw middle, and a big batch punishes a cook who rushes.

How it is used

Butter and fruit preserves, sorghum, or maple syrup.

Field notes

  • This is a farmhouse buttermilk pancake associated with Amish and Mennonite breakfasts, not a claim of a single secret recipe.
  • Preserves and sorghum often matter more than anything mixed into the batter.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 1/2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/2 cups buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 3 tablespoons melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir the dry ingredients together, then stir in the wet ingredients until the batter is combined.
  2. Stir until the flour is just wet. Rest 5 minutes.
  3. Heat a griddle or heavy skillet over medium heat and film it with butter.
  4. Spoon on the batter. Cook until the underside is browned and the top looks set.
  5. Flip once and cook the second side through.
  6. Keep the cooked cakes warm while you finish the batch, and serve them together.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A cup of cornmeal in a double batch leans toward a corn cake.
  • Vanilla is a diner addition. It is not needed.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • fruit preserves
  • butter
  • sorghum
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Venezuela · No chili heat · 6 arepas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Venezuela · South America

Andean Arepas

A wheat arepa from the Andes, softer and breadier than the corn cake.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 arepas

History

In the Venezuelan Andes, arepas are often made with wheat flour rather than maize, a regional difference that surprises people who know only the corn arepa. They are cooked on a griddle and eaten at breakfast. This page keeps them separate so the corn arepa is not asked to stand for every Andean kitchen.

Cultural background

They are thicker than a tortilla and milder than masarepa cakes. Butter or cheese is a common partner. The dough here is vegan. Butter at the table adds dairy.

Why it tastes this way

Wheat grows better than a lowland maize habit in some highland kitchens, which is why the bread changed grain and kept the name. The flavor is toasted wheat, not nixtamal. A griddle still makes the crust. The regional fact is the point of a separate page.

How it is made

A soft wheat dough is shaped into thick disks and cooked on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Do not roll them as thin as tortillas. Cook them covered on medium-low so the center bakes through.

How it is used

Split or whole, with butter or cheese.

Field notes

  • In the Venezuelan Andes, arepas are often wheat, not corn.
  • They are still called arepas.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons oil
  • 3/4 cup water

Steps

  1. Mix a soft dough and rest 10 minutes.
  2. Shape thick disks.
  3. Cook covered on a medium-low griddle, turning once, until the center is set.
  4. Serve warm.
  5. They are wheat, not corn.
  6. Keep them thicker than tortillas.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Corn arepas are the lowland daily bread.
  • A spoon of anise is used in some Andean doughs.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • butter
  • cheese
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · about 10 small cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Appalachian Buckwheat Cakes

Earthly buckwheat, a little sour, with a sturdy crumb.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 10 small cakes

History

Buckwheat cakes are an old mountain breakfast. Buckwheat grows on thin, cool soil where wheat struggles, which suited upland farms from Pennsylvania down the Appalachian chain. Settlers from German-speaking Pennsylvania and from the British Isles brought griddle habits. The cakes were often started with a bit of leftover batter that soured overnight.

Cultural background

They are winter food: dark, filling, and meant for molasses or sorghum. A lighter wheat pancake replaced them in many houses once white flour was cheap, but buckwheat never left the mountains entirely.

Why it tastes this way

Buckwheat is not a wheat. Its flavor is grassy and mineral, and cool mountain fields are why it was planted. An overnight rest gives a light sourness, the taste of a batter kept in a crock, not of a boxed mix. Molasses and sorghum match it because those were the local sugars. A little wheat flour in this home version keeps the cake from crumbling. Pure buckwheat is a different, more fragile cake.

How it is made

Buckwheat and a portion of wheat flour are mixed with buttermilk, egg, and leavener, rested, and cooked on a hot griddle.

Authentic touch. Cook them smaller than a diner pancake. Buckwheat batter sets slowly, and a huge cake stays raw in the middle.

How it is used

Butter and sorghum or molasses. Sausage is the classic side and makes the plate no longer vegetarian.

Field notes

  • Buckwheat thrived in cool uplands, which is the agricultural reason these cakes are mountain food.
  • This version uses some wheat so the cakes hold. The label is not gluten-free.

Ingredients

  • 3/4 cup buckwheat flour
  • 1/4 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon molasses
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 1/4 cups buttermilk
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir the dry ingredients together, then stir in the wet ingredients until the batter is combined.
  2. Rest the batter 10 minutes. It will look thicker than a white-flour batter.
  3. Heat a griddle or heavy skillet over medium heat and film it with butter or bacon fat.
  4. Spoon on the batter. Cook until the underside is browned and the top looks set.
  5. Flip once and cook the second side through.
  6. Cook small cakes. The crumb should lose its shine before you serve them.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • For a gluten-free cake, use all buckwheat flour and accept a more fragile crumb.
  • A spoon of the batter saved overnight in the refrigerator is the old starter. Use it in the next batch if you like it sourer.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • sorghum
  • molasses
  • butter
  • sausage
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United States · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Appalachian Cornmeal Cakes

Toasted meal, tangy milk, and a sturdy round.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Cornmeal cakes were ordinary bread in the southern mountains, where corn was the grain people grew and milled. Scots-Irish and English settlers brought griddle and oat habits. Cherokee and other Native nations of these mountains had grown corn long before those settlements. This cake is later mountain home cooking. It is not presented here as a Cherokee recipe.

Cultural background

A mix of meal, buttermilk, and egg cooked in a skillet was breakfast or the bread for soup beans. It is humbler than spoonbread and closer to a hoe cake.

Why it tastes this way

Mountain farms stored corn as meal because the grain dried and kept. Buttermilk from a family cow softened it. The taste is toasted meal and acid, not vanilla. Cooks who had no wheat still had a bread. That economy is the flavor.

How it is made

Cornmeal is soaked briefly with buttermilk, bound with an egg, and cooked as thick rounds.

Authentic touch. Use a well-seasoned skillet and a moderate fire. A blazing griddle burns the meal before the center sets.

How it is used

Butter, sorghum, or beside soup beans. Both uses are honest.

Field notes

  • This book does not assign the cake to the Cherokee. Corn in these mountains is older than this recipe.
  • Soup beans and cornbread are a documented mountain plate. The cake can play the bread’s part.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup plain cornmeal
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter or oil

Steps

  1. Stir meal, salt, and soda. Add buttermilk, egg, and fat.
  2. Rest 10 minutes.
  3. Cook on a greased medium griddle as thick rounds.
  4. Flip when the underside is brown and the top is set.
  5. Cook through.
  6. Serve with beans or sorghum.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of wheat flour softens them and means they are no longer gluten-free.
  • Water instead of buttermilk is the leaner, older cake.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • sorghum
  • soup beans
  • butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Colombia · No chili heat · 6 arepas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Colombia · South America

Arepa Antioqueña

A corn arepa griddled and often finished with a little butter, served beside the meal.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 arepas

History

In Antioquia and the Paisa region, the arepa is a corn cake served with meals, often not split and stuffed the Venezuelan way. It is griddled, sometimes with a pat of butter at the end. The bandeja paisa and simpler breakfasts both include it. This recipe is the plain corn cake.

Cultural background

Masarepa and water, salt, and a griddle. Butter at the end is common and adds dairy. The cake itself can be vegan if you skip that pat.

Why it tastes this way

The Paisa arepa is a side bread, so it stays mild and corn-sweet, a contrast to beans, eggs, and meat on the same plate. Griddling toasts it. Butter browns on the hot surface. The choice not to stuff it is cultural, not a missing step. Venezuela’s stuffed arepa is the neighbor, not the template.

How it is made

Masarepa disks are cooked on a griddle until the crust is set.

Authentic touch. Make them a bit thinner than a stuffed Venezuelan arepa if you are serving them beside a full plate. Cook until the crust is dry and the center is set.

How it is used

Hot, beside eggs or beans. Butter is optional.

Field notes

  • Paisa arepas are often a side, not a stuffed sandwich.
  • Butter at the end is optional and adds dairy.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups masarepa
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 2 1/2 cups warm water
  • Butter, optional

Steps

  1. Mix masarepa, salt, and water. Rest 5 minutes.
  2. Shape disks a bit thinner than a stuffed arepa.
  3. Cook on a dry or lightly oiled griddle until the crust is dry and the center is set.
  4. Optional: rub with butter.
  5. Serve beside eggs or beans.
  6. Do not split them unless you want to.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A cheese arepa presses cheese into the dough and adds dairy.
  • Venezuelan arepas are more often split.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • eggs
  • beans
  • butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Colombia · No chili heat · 6 arepas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Colombia · South America

Arepa Boyacense

A toasted corn arepa from Boyacá, often sweetened and served with cheese.

10 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 arepas

History

Arepa boyacense is the arepa of Boyacá in the Colombian highlands, often made with corn flour and a little wheat in some houses, griddled, and eaten with cheese or a touch of sweetness. Recipes differ. This version stays with masarepa and a spoon of sugar, plus cheese on the side, and does not add wheat.

Cultural background

Highland kitchens toast the cake more than they stuff it. Cheese is the partner. The crumb is denser than a fresh-corn arepa.

Why it tastes this way

A drier corn dough toasts deeply on a griddle, which is the flavor of a highland arepa meant to keep and to eat with salty cheese. A little sugar is a regional sweetness, not a dessert frosting. The altitude and the habit of griddling rather than frying are why it tastes toasted instead of oily.

How it is made

A firm masarepa dough with a little sugar is cooked dry on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Cook it longer than a soft cachapa, until the face is well spotted. It should still be moist inside. Do not oil it heavily.

How it is used

With cheese.

Field notes

  • This highland arepa is toasted more than it is stuffed.
  • This version stays with corn and does not add wheat.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups masarepa
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 2 cups warm water
  • Cheese, to serve

Steps

  1. Mix a firm dough. Add a spoon more water only if it cracks.
  2. Shape thick disks.
  3. Cook on a dry medium griddle until well spotted and cooked through.
  4. Serve with cheese.
  5. Do not oil them heavily.
  6. The face should toast.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Some Boyacá recipes add wheat flour. This one does not.
  • Arepa de choclo is the fresh-corn cake.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • cheese
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Colombia · No chili heat · 6 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Colombia · South America

Arepa de Choclo

A sweet fresh-corn arepa, often with cheese, folded or topped.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 6 cakes

History

Arepa de choclo is a Colombian fresh-corn cake, especially associated with places that sell it with cheese as a sweet-salty breakfast. Choclo is fresh corn. It is closer to a cachapa than to a masarepa arepa. Cheese is part of the usual plate, so this recipe includes it and is not vegan.

Cultural background

Street vendors cook them on a griddle. The corn is sweet. The cheese melts. They are eaten with the hands.

Why it tastes this way

Fresh corn’s sugar browns on the griddle, and a salty fresh cheese is the contrast Colombians expect. That sweet-salt pair is the dish. It is a different corn from the dried masarepa of a plain arepa: juicy, seasonal, and closer to a pancake. The griddle, not an oven, is the tool.

How it is made

Fresh corn is blended, cooked as thick cakes, and topped with cheese.

Authentic touch. Blend the corn coarse. If the batter is watery, a spoon of masarepa or cornmeal helps. Put the cheese on while the cake is hot so it softens.

How it is used

Folded or open, hot.

Field notes

  • Choclo means fresh corn, not dried masarepa.
  • Cheese on top is the usual sweet-salt bite.

Ingredients

  • 4 ears sweet corn
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon masarepa, if needed
  • 1 cup crumbled fresh cheese
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Blend the corn and salt. Add masarepa if watery.
  2. Cook thick cakes on a medium griddle.
  3. Top with cheese while hot.
  4. Fold or serve open.
  5. Eat immediately.
  6. The corn should taste sweet.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Without cheese it is closer to a cachapa and can be vegan.
  • Cachapas are the Venezuelan sister.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • queso fresco
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Colombia · No chili heat · 4 arepas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Colombia · South America

Arepa de Huevo

A fried corn cake with an egg inside, a coastal street food.

20 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 arepas

History

Arepa de huevo is a street food of Colombia’s Caribbean coast: a corn dough disk, slit, filled with an egg, sealed, and fried. It is associated with cities such as Cartagena and Barranquilla. It is a fried arepa, not a griddled one, and the egg is the point.

Cultural background

The dough is masarepa, often colored with achiote. The egg should set inside. It is eaten hot, standing up.

Why it tastes this way

Frying a sealed corn cake cooks the egg in a pocket, so you get a crisp shell and a set egg in one bite. Achiote, if you use it, adds color and a mild earthiness common on the coast. The fry is heavier than a comal arepa, which is why it eats like street food. Corn is still the structure. The egg is the filling.

How it is made

Masarepa disks are filled with an egg, sealed, and fried until the egg is cooked.

Authentic touch. Seal the edge well and fry at a steady medium heat. Too hot, and the dough browns before the egg sets. Too cool, and the cake drinks oil.

How it is used

Hot, as a snack.

Field notes

  • The egg is sealed inside and fried on the Caribbean coast.
  • The egg should be cooked through, not liquid.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups masarepa
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 2 cups warm water
  • 4 eggs
  • Oil for frying

Steps

  1. Mix a soft dough and shape 4 disks. Make a pocket in each and slip in an egg. Seal well.
  2. Flatten gently.
  3. Fry in hot oil over medium until the dough is golden and the egg is cooked through.
  4. Drain.
  5. Serve hot.
  6. If the egg is still liquid, fry longer at a lower heat.

The egg must be cooked through. Fry over steady heat.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A griddled arepa with a fried egg on top is easier and is not this street food.
  • Achiote in the dough is traditional color.

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Breakfast
  • aji sauce
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Australia · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Australia · Oceania

Australian Pancakes

A larger, softer breakfast pancake than a pikelet, served with lemon and sugar or syrup.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Australian pancakes, as distinct from pikelets, are the larger breakfast cakes, closer to a thin-to-medium crepe or a fluffy stack depending on the house. Lemon and sugar are a common finish, inherited from British Shrove pancakes. Weekend breakfast is the setting. They are not the same thickness as pikelets, which is why both pages exist.

Cultural background

A thinner batter makes them more crepe-like. This one is in between: pourable, not a drop scone, not paper-thin.

Why it tastes this way

Lemon and sugar are the British finish that stayed, sharp against butter. A breakfast pancake is larger than a tea pikelet because it is the meal, not the accompaniment to a cup of tea. Golden syrup is the other pour. The wheat-and-egg batter is the settler breakfast, stated as that.

How it is made

A pourable batter is cooked as medium pancakes and finished with lemon and sugar.

Authentic touch. Pour a larger round than a pikelet and cook it through. Lemon should go on while the cake is hot.

How it is used

Hot, with lemon and sugar or golden syrup.

Field notes

  • Lemon and sugar are the British finish that stayed.
  • Pikelets are the smaller tea cakes.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 egg
  • 1 cup milk
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter
  • Lemon and sugar, to serve

Steps

  1. Whisk a pourable batter.
  2. Cook medium rounds on a buttered griddle.
  3. Serve with lemon and sugar or golden syrup.
  4. They are larger than pikelets.
  5. Lemon goes on while they are hot.
  6. Eat them the morning they are made.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Pikelets are smaller and thicker.
  • Banana in the batter is a home weekend habit.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • lemon
  • sugar
  • golden syrup
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Australia · No chili heat · about 12 pikelets

Flying Pancakes of the World

Australia · Oceania

Australian Pikelets

Small thick drop scones, buttered, a tea-table cake.

10 min prep · 12 min cook · Serves 4 · about 12 pikelets

History

Australian pikelets are small thick pancakes in the British drop-scone family, a standard in older home-economics cooking and at tea. They are not crumpets, though both get butter. Golden syrup or jam is the finish. They are the settler British cake in Australian homes, not an Aboriginal bread.

Cultural background

They are cooked by the spoonful and eaten warm. Cream is a generous addition. The batter is close to Scotch pancakes.

Why it tastes this way

A thick egg batter on a moderate girdle makes a soft, lightly sweet cake, the tea food British cooks brought and kept. Golden syrup, a cane invert syrup, is the colonial pantry sweet that stuck in Australia and New Zealand. The cake tastes of butter and egg. Jam is the fruit. It is a borrowed recipe that became local afternoon tea.

How it is made

A thick batter is dropped onto a griddle and served with butter and jam or golden syrup.

Authentic touch. Drop them small. Flip when the bubbles set. Serve warm so the butter melts. They dry out if they are overcooked.

How it is used

Warm, with butter.

Field notes

  • Australian pikelets are drop scones for tea.
  • They are not crumpets and not an Aboriginal bread.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 egg
  • 1/2 cup milk
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter

Steps

  1. Mix a thick batter.
  2. Drop small rounds on a buttered medium griddle.
  3. Flip when the bubbles hold.
  4. Serve warm with butter and jam or golden syrup.
  5. Do not cook them large.
  6. They dry out if overcooked.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • New Zealand pikelets are the close cousin.
  • Crumpets are the holey yeast cakes and are not these.

Pairings and uses

  • Snack
  • Breakfast
  • butter
  • jam
  • golden syrup
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Morocco · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Morocco · North Africa

Baghrir

A semolina pancake cooked on one side, full of holes, eaten with butter and honey.

1 hr 10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Baghrir are Moroccan pancakes, sometimes called thousand-hole pancakes, made from semolina and flour, cooked on one side so the top becomes a honeycomb. They are a breakfast and tea food across the Maghreb. Melted butter and honey are poured into the holes. They are not flipped.

Cultural background

Yeast or baking powder lifts the holes. This recipe uses yeast for the traditional open crumb. Serve them warm. The holes are for the butter.

Why it tastes this way

Semolina gives a tender, slightly grainy crumb that wheat alone does not. A wet batter on a moderate pan boils into holes on top and sets before anyone flips it. Butter and honey fill those holes, which is why the texture is the whole point. The Maghreb’s semolina kitchen, shared with couscous, is why the pancake is not a wheat crepe.

How it is made

A thin semolina yeast batter is cooked on one side in a lightly oiled pan.

Authentic touch. Cook on medium-low and do not flip. The top should look dry and full of holes. A high heat burns the bottom while the top is still batter.

How it is used

Warm, with melted butter and honey.

Field notes

  • The holes are the dish. If there are none, the batter was too thick or the pan too hot.
  • They are cooked on one side only.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup fine semolina
  • 1/2 cup flour
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/2 cups warm water
  • Butter and honey, to serve

Steps

  1. Blend or whisk all the batter ingredients until smooth. Rest 1 hour.
  2. Cook small rounds on a lightly oiled medium-low pan.
  3. When the top is full of holes and dry, remove them. Do not flip.
  4. Serve with melted butter and honey.
  5. The batter should be thin.
  6. Eat them the same day.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Algerian and Tunisian kitchens also make thousand-hole pancakes. The Moroccan name baghrir is the one used here.
  • Baking powder is a faster substitute and makes fewer, coarser holes.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • butter
  • honey
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Honduras · No chili heat · 6 baleadas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Honduras · Mexico & Central America

Baleadas

A soft flour tortilla folded around refried beans and cheese.

25 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 baleadas

History

Baleadas are a Honduran breakfast and street food: a large flour tortilla folded over refried beans, crema, and cheese. The north coast is especially associated with them. The tortilla is wheat. Beans and cheese are the simple filling. Eggs or meat make a baleada especial and are variations.

Cultural background

The tortilla should be soft and a bit blistered. Beans are mashed and salted. Crema is tangy. This recipe uses beans and cheese. Crema adds more dairy.

Why it tastes this way

A soft wheat tortilla folds without cracking, which is why the dish works as a portable breakfast. Refried beans are savory and filling. Crema and cheese are the dairy richness of the Honduran plate. It is a flour-tortilla dish, not a corn pupusa, and the wheat is the northern Central American choice for this particular wrap.

How it is made

Flour tortillas are cooked on a comal and folded around refried beans and cheese.

Authentic touch. Cook the tortilla until it puffs in spots and keep it soft. Spread a thin layer of beans. A thick layer tears the bread when you fold it.

How it is used

Warm, folded.

Field notes

  • A baleada is a flour tortilla folded over beans and cheese.
  • A thin layer of beans is what lets it fold.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups flour
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons oil
  • 3/4 cup warm water
  • 1 1/2 cups refried beans
  • 1 cup crumbled cheese

Steps

  1. Mix a soft dough and rest 15 minutes.
  2. Roll 6 thin rounds and cook on a comal until spotted and soft.
  3. Spread beans and cheese and fold.
  4. Serve warm.
  5. Keep the tortillas covered.
  6. A thin layer of beans folds. A thick one tears.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • An egg or avocado is a common addition. Avocado adds no allergen.
  • Flour tortillas of northern Mexico are the bread technique.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Stuffed or filled
  • refried beans
  • cheese
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Flying Pancakes of the World

Belize · Caribbean

Belizean Johnnycake

A frying-pan bread, dense and lightly salted, cooked in a covered skillet.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 6 · one round

History

Belizean johnnycakes are often cooked in a skillet or baked, a Creole bread served with stew beans or at breakfast. They sit in the same broad Caribbean johnnycake family as the Bahamas loaf and are frequently a round cooked on the stove. This recipe is the skillet round.

Cultural background

They are split and buttered, or eaten with beans. Coconut milk appears in some recipes. This one uses water and oil so it is dairy-free.

Why it tastes this way

A simple flour dough cooked in a covered pan tastes toasted outside and soft inside, a bread for beans. The method is practical where an oven was not the tool. Coconut, when used, is the coast. This plain version tastes of wheat and salt so the beans can be the flavor. The Caribbean name is the link to the other johnny cakes.

How it is made

A soft dough is patted into a round and cooked in a covered skillet.

Authentic touch. Cook covered on low heat and turn once. High heat burns the crust and leaves a raw middle.

How it is used

With stew beans or butter.

Field notes

  • Belizean johnnycake is a Creole bread, often skillet-cooked.
  • Low heat is what cooks the center.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons oil
  • 3/4 cup water

Steps

  1. Mix a soft dough and rest 10 minutes.
  2. Pat a round about 3/4 inch thick.
  3. Cook in a lightly oiled covered skillet over low heat, about 10 minutes a side.
  4. The center should be set.
  5. Cool slightly and split.
  6. Serve with beans or butter.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Coconut milk in the dough is a richer coastal version.
  • The Bahamian page is the baked style.

Pairings and uses

  • Vegetarian

India · No chili heat · about 8 dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Benne Dosa

A crisp fermented dosa finished with a notable amount of butter.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 dosas

History

Benne dosa is the butter dosa of Davangere in Karnataka, a tiffin-room specialty. Benne means butter. The dosa batter is the fermented one, and the butter on the tawa is generous. A potato filling is common. The butter is the defining point, so this recipe is not vegan and not dairy-free.

Cultural background

It is a restaurant breakfast more than a temple recipe. People go to Davangere, and to restaurants that copy it, for the butter.

Why it tastes this way

Butter on a hot tawa browns and flavors the rice batter in a way oil does not, which is the entire reason to name the dosa after butter. The ferment still supplies tang. The contrast of crisp edge and rich fat is the plate. It is a city specialty built on an older batter, and the dairy is honest.

How it is made

Fermented dosa batter is cooked on a buttered tawa until crisp.

Authentic touch. Use butter, not only oil, and let it foam on the tawa before the batter goes down. Too little butter and you have made a plain dosa.

How it is used

With chutney and, if you like, a potato filling.

Field notes

  • Benne means butter. The fat is the specialty.
  • Davangere is the city most tied to the name.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups fermented dosa batter
  • 4 tablespoons butter
  • A pinch of salt if the batter needs it

Steps

  1. Heat a tawa and add a spoon of butter.
  2. Spread a ladle of batter thin.
  3. Cook until crisp, adding a little more butter at the edge.
  4. Fold and serve.
  5. Repeat, refreshing the butter.
  6. Chutney on the side.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Oil instead of butter is a different, vegan dosa. Do not call it benne.
  • A potato filling makes it a meal.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • coconut chutney
  • potato masala
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

United Kingdom · No chili heat · one round, 8 pieces

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Beremeal Bannocks

Malty, earthy barley bread cooked on a girdle.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 8 · one round, 8 pieces

History

Bere is an old barley still grown in Orkney, used for bannocks and bannock-like breads. Beremeal bannocks are a documented Orcadian bread, distinct from a generic wheat scone. Bere meal tastes earthier than modern pearl barley flour. Buttermilk and soda are a common way to raise them. This home version uses beremeal if you can get it, with a note that ordinary barley flour is a milder substitute, not the same grain.

Cultural background

They are eaten with butter, cheese, or soup. Orkney’s short season is why a hardy barley mattered.

Why it tastes this way

Bere tastes malty and slightly bitter because it is a different barley from the pale flour in a box of biscuits. Orkney’s wind and short summer favored it. Buttermilk softens the bitterness and soda lifts a dough that has little gluten. The girdle browns the malt. If you use ordinary barley flour, say so. The taste will be milder.

How it is made

Beremeal, a little wheat flour, baking soda, and buttermilk are patted into a round and cooked on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Do not expect wheat elasticity. Pat the dough. Do not knead it like bread. Cook it through, because barley tastes raw when it is underdone.

How it is used

Butter or cheese, warm.

Field notes

  • Bere is a specific old barley associated with Orkney, not a brand name for any brown flour.
  • A little wheat in this home version means it is not barley-only.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup beremeal or barley flour
  • 1/2 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 2/3 cup buttermilk

Steps

  1. Mix the meals, soda, and salt. Add buttermilk to a soft dough.
  2. Pat into a round.
  3. Cook on a medium-low girdle, turning once, until cooked through.
  4. Cool briefly and break apart.
  5. Serve with butter.
  6. The crumb should not look wet in the center.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • All beremeal, without wheat, is more traditional and more fragile. This version uses a little wheat so it holds.
  • Ordinary barley flour is a substitute and should be described as barley, not as bere.

Pairings and uses

  • Bread course
  • Breakfast
  • butter
  • Orkney cheese
  • soup
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

India · Mild · about 6 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Besan Cheela

A savory chickpea-flour pancake with onion, chile, and spices.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 3 · about 6 pancakes

History

Besan cheela, or chilla, is a North Indian savory pancake of chickpea flour, water, onion, and spices, cooked on a tawa. It is a fast breakfast, related to Gujarati pudla. It does not need a ferment. Besan is the flour left when chana dal is ground, and it is a pantry staple.

Cultural background

It is eaten with chutney, pickle, or yogurt. The pancake is vegan as written. Yogurt on the side adds dairy.

Why it tastes this way

Chickpea flour tastes nutty and binds with water alone, so the cake needs no egg or wheat. Ajwain or cumin, chile, and onion make it savory rather than flat. It is a breakfast of the chickpea-growing north, quick because there is no ferment. The gram flavor is the difference from a rice dosa.

How it is made

Besan, water, onion, and spices are mixed into a batter and cooked as pancakes.

Authentic touch. Rest the batter 10 minutes so the besan hydrates, or the center tastes raw. Cook it on medium. Besan burns at the edge before the middle sets if the pan is screaming.

How it is used

With green chutney or pickle.

Field notes

  • Besan cheela is an instant savory pancake. It is not fermented.
  • Chickpea flour keeps it gluten-free if the besan is pure.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup besan (chickpea flour)
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 1 small onion, finely chopped
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Whisk besan, water, onion, chile, cumin, salt, and cilantro. Rest 10 minutes.
  2. Cook small pancakes on a medium oiled tawa.
  3. Flip when the underside is browned and the top looks set.
  4. Cook through.
  5. Serve with chutney.
  6. The center should not taste of raw flour.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Moong dal cheela is the lentil cousin.
  • Pudla is the Gujarati besan pancake.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Savory meal
  • green chutney
  • pickle
  • yogurt
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Philippines · No chili heat · one 8-inch cake

Flying Pancakes of the World

Philippines · Southeast Asia

Bibingka

A baked rice cake, tender, traditionally cooked in a clay pan lined with banana leaf.

15 min prep · 35 min cook · Serves 8 · one 8-inch cake

History

Bibingka is a Filipino rice cake especially associated with Christmas dawn masses, Simbang Gabi, when it is sold outside churches. Traditional bibingka is baked in a clay pot lined with banana leaf, with heat above and below. Banana leaf is ideal and not required for a home pan. This is a baked rice cake, included as a holiday cousin of the pancake, not as a crepe.

Cultural background

Salted egg or cheese on top are common finishes. Butter and sugar too. The crumb is tender and a little sweet.

Why it tastes this way

Rice flour and coconut milk taste of the Philippine celebration table, richer than a daily tortilla and softer than a cracker. Christmas season is why the cake is a special breakfast. Banana leaf, when you have it, perfumes the edge. Cheese or salted egg on top is salty against the sweet crumb, a Filipino contrast. A home oven is an adaptation of the clay pot.

How it is made

Rice flour, coconut milk, egg, and sugar are baked in a lined pan until set.

Authentic touch. Bake it until the center is just set. It dries out if you treat it like a brownie. A banana leaf in the pan is traditional. Parchment works and will not taste of leaf.

How it is used

Warm, with butter and grated cheese or a slice of salted egg if you eat them.

Field notes

  • Bibingka is the Christmas rice cake sold around Simbang Gabi.
  • A banana leaf perfumes the edge. Parchment will not.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup rice flour
  • 1 cup coconut milk
  • 1/3 cup sugar
  • 2 eggs
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons butter

Steps

  1. Heat the oven to 375°F. Butter a pan. Line it with banana leaf if you have one.
  2. Whisk the batter and pour it in.
  3. Bake about 30 minutes, until just set.
  4. Brush with butter.
  5. Cool 10 minutes.
  6. Serve warm.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Salted egg on top is a classic salty finish.
  • Bibingka is baked. It is not a street crepe.

Pairings and uses

  • Holiday table
  • Breakfast
  • butter
  • cheese
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Ireland · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ireland · Western Europe

Boxty

Half grated raw potato, half mashed potato, browned in a pan.

25 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Boxty is an Irish potato bread especially associated with Ulster, Connacht, and the northwest. A traditional rhyme says boxty on the griddle and boxty in the pan. There are pan cakes, baked loaves, and boiled dumplings. This page is the pan cake: raw grated potato and mashed potato together. Potatoes became a central Irish food in the eighteenth century, and boxty is one of the breads that came out of that dependence, including through the years around the Famine.

Cultural background

It is a meal with butter, or with bacon and eggs. The raw potato keeps it from tasting like a leftover-mash patty.

Why it tastes this way

Grated raw potato brings water and a clean starch that crisps. Mashed potato brings a soft, cooked sweetness. Together they taste more potato than a flour pancake and more tender than a latke made only of shreds. The wheat flour is only a binder. Ireland’s cool, wet climate favored the potato as a staple, which is why a bread was built from it.

How it is made

Equal parts squeezed raw potato and mashed potato are mixed with a little flour, egg, and milk, then fried as cakes.

Authentic touch. Squeeze the grated potato dry, and keep the starch that settles from the liquid. Stir the batter just before frying. It darkens if it waits.

How it is used

Butter, or beside bacon and eggs. Some cooks serve it with a fried egg on top.

Field notes

  • Pan, griddle, and boiled boxty are three methods. This recipe is the pan cake.
  • Use a floury potato for the mash and a dry grate. A waxy potato stays gluey.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups grated raw potato, squeezed dry
  • 1 cup cold mashed potato
  • 1/2 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 large egg
  • 1/4 cup milk
  • 3/4 teaspoon salt
  • Butter or oil for frying

Steps

  1. Squeeze the grated potato in a towel. Add back any settled starch.
  2. Mix with mash, flour, egg, milk, and salt.
  3. Heat a buttered skillet over medium.
  4. Fry flattened rounds until brown on both sides and cooked through.
  5. Keep the heat moderate so the raw potato cooks before the crust burns.
  6. Serve hot with butter.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Boxty bread is the same idea baked as a round loaf.
  • Boxty dumplings are boiled. They are a different dish from the pan cake.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Supper
  • Side dish
  • butter
  • bacon
  • eggs
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Brazil · No chili heat · 6 crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Brazil · South America

Brazilian Tapioca Crepes

A crisp-edged crepe of hydrated tapioca starch, filled with cheese or coconut.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 crepes

History

Tapioca crepes are a Brazilian street and home breakfast made from gummed tapioca starch, not a wheat batter. The starch is moistened until it clumps, then scattered in a pan, where it binds into a crepe. Cheese or coconut and condensed milk are fillings. The starch itself is cassava, a crop with deep Indigenous roots in Brazil. Use commercial tapioca starch.

Cultural background

A good tapioca has a lacy edge and a flexible center. Queijo coalho is a traditional salty cheese. This filling uses a firm cheese and notes coconut as the vegan option.

Why it tastes this way

Tapioca starch has almost no flavor and a lot of chew, so the filling is the taste: salty cheese or sweet coconut. The pan hydrates the starch into a sheet, which is a different chemistry from wheat gluten. Cassava is the Indigenous and later national starch. The crepe format is a modern street breakfast. Both can be true.

How it is made

Moistened tapioca starch is cooked as a crepe and filled.

Authentic touch. Moisten the starch until it holds when squeezed and still crumbles. Scatter it in an even layer in a moderate pan. Do not stir. It will knit itself. Flip once, quickly.

How it is used

Folded, hot. Cheese is the dairy filling. Coconut and a little sugar are the vegan one.

Field notes

  • The crepe is tapioca starch, not wheat.
  • Cheese is one filling. Coconut and sugar are the vegan one.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups tapioca starch
  • About 3/4 cup water
  • 1 cup grated firm cheese, or 1 cup grated coconut plus 2 tablespoons sugar

Steps

  1. Sprinkle water over the starch and toss until it clumps when squeezed and still crumbles.
  2. Scatter a thin even layer in a medium pan. Do not stir.
  3. When the sheet knits, flip it.
  4. Add cheese, or coconut and sugar, and fold.
  5. Serve immediately.
  6. Use commercial tapioca starch.

Use commercial cassava flour or yuca sold ready to cook. Do not process bitter cassava at home from these pages.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Coconut and sugar instead of cheese keep it vegan.
  • Beiju is the plainer cassava bread.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Snack
  • cheese
  • coconut
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Bulgaria · No chili heat · 8 crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Bulgaria · Eastern Europe

Bulgarian Palachinki

Thin pancakes with jam, honey, or white cheese.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 crepes

History

Palachinki are Bulgarian crepes, part of the Balkan crepe family. They are eaten with jam, honey, walnuts, or a savory white cheese. This recipe uses honey and jam so the written cake stays nut-free. Sirene, the brined white cheese, is the savory option.

Cultural background

They are a home breakfast and a dessert. The batter is thin. Honey is a natural partner in a country with a strong beekeeping tradition.

Why it tastes this way

Honey tastes floral and less jammy than apricot, so a plain crepe with honey is a different plate from the Hungarian jam roll. White cheese makes the same batter savory and salty. Both uses are Bulgarian. The crepe technique is the regional one. The sweetener is the local choice.

How it is made

Thin crepes are folded and finished with honey or jam.

Authentic touch. If you use honey, drizzle it on the hot crepe so it thins. Cold honey sits in a lump.

How it is used

Warm, with honey or jam. Sirene is the savory alternative.

Field notes

  • Palachinki are the Bulgarian crepe.
  • Honey is a classic finish and is not the same plate as jam.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1 cup milk
  • 1/4 cup water
  • Pinch of salt
  • 1 tablespoon oil
  • Honey, to serve

Steps

  1. Whisk a thin batter.
  2. Cook crepes in a lightly oiled pan.
  3. Fold and drizzle with honey.
  4. Serve warm.
  5. Jam can replace honey.
  6. Do not make them thick.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Walnuts and honey are a traditional combination and add tree nuts.
  • Sirene inside the fold makes them savory and saltier.

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • honey
  • jam
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Venezuela · No chili heat · about 6 cachapas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Venezuela · South America

Cachapas

A sweet-corn pancake, soft and irregular, often folded with cheese.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 6 cachapas

History

Cachapas are Venezuelan pancakes of fresh ground sweet corn, cooked on a griddle and often folded around cheese, queso de mano or similar. They are street and home food. The batter is the corn itself, sometimes loosened with a little milk or water. This recipe keeps the batter dairy-free and serves cheese as an optional fold, so the cake can be vegan and the cheese is a choice.

Cultural background

The corn must be sweet and juicy. Canned corn, drained and blended, is a workable off-season substitute and tastes less alive. Say which you used.

Why it tastes this way

Fresh corn is sweet because the kernels are immature and full of sugar, which is why a cachapa tastes like corn and dessert at once even when the filling is salty cheese. The griddle browns the natural sugar. No wheat is required. The cheese, when used, is salty and squeaky, the contrast. Street griddles make them irregular. That rustic edge is normal.

How it is made

Fresh corn is blended with a little salt and cooked as thick pancakes.

Authentic touch. Blend the corn until it is a coarse batter, not a juice. Cook on medium and cover briefly so the center sets. It will not look like a smooth wheat pancake.

How it is used

Folded, with cheese if you eat dairy, or plain.

Field notes

  • Cachapas are fresh-corn pancakes. Cheese is optional here.
  • Canned corn works and tastes less alive than fresh.

Ingredients

  • 4 ears sweet corn, kernels cut off
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon cornmeal, if needed
  • Oil
  • Cheese, optional

Steps

  1. Blend the corn and salt to a coarse batter. Add cornmeal if it is watery.
  2. Cook thick rounds on a medium oiled griddle, covered briefly.
  3. Flip when the underside is spotted.
  4. Fold with cheese if you eat dairy.
  5. Serve hot.
  6. They will look rustic.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of cornmeal helps a watery blend hold.
  • Costa Rican chorreadas are the close cousin.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • queso de mano
  • butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · about 10 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

California Sourdough Pancakes

Tangy sourdough cakes, richer than the Alaskan camp version.

1 hr 10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 10 pancakes

History

San Francisco’s sourdough bakers, and the prospectors who kept starters before them, made the city proud of a sour loaf. Sourdough pancakes are the breakfast use of that starter, common in California kitchens that keep a jar on the counter. They are related to Alaska sourdough hotcakes and are written here with milk and butter, the settled-kitchen version rather than the camp one.

Cultural background

A starter, flour, and milk are mixed the night before or an hour before, then eggs and soda finish the batter in the morning. Bakeries and home cooks both do it.

Why it tastes this way

The Bay Area’s bakers built a reputation on a tart starter, and the pancake borrows that acid. Milk and butter round the sourness so it tastes dairy-rich rather than like a lean camp cake. Baking soda at the end is the same lift used in the North. The flavor is wheat, acid, and butter.

How it is made

Starter, flour, and milk ferment briefly, then egg, butter, and baking soda finish a griddle batter.

Authentic touch. If the overnight batter smells sharply of nail polish or anything rotten, throw it out. A healthy sour smell is tangy and bready, not chemical.

How it is used

Butter and fruit or maple syrup.

Field notes

  • A pleasant tang is the goal. A spoiled starter is not a more authentic one.
  • California and Alaska sourdough cakes are the same idea in two kitchens.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup active sourdough starter
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 cup milk
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda

Steps

  1. Stir starter, flour, milk, sugar, and salt. Cover and rest 1 hour, or overnight in the refrigerator.
  2. Stir in egg and butter. Heat the griddle.
  3. Stir in the baking soda and cook at once.
  4. Flip when the surface is bubbly and set.
  5. Serve hot.
  6. Save a spoon of starter, not of this egg batter, to keep your jar going.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • The Alaska page is the leaner, water-based camp cake.
  • Skip the overnight rest and use a cup of very active starter the same morning, as in the Alaska method.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • butter
  • berries
  • maple syrup
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United States · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Cape Cod Cranberry Pancakes

Sharp cranberry against a mild buttermilk cake.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Cranberries are native to the wetlands of southeastern Massachusetts. Wampanoag and other Native people used them long before commercial bogs. A wheat pancake full of chopped cranberries is a modern regional breakfast, common in the fall when the bogs are harvested. It is not a menu from 1621, and it should not be told as one.

Cultural background

The berry is too sharp to eat like a blueberry, so it is chopped and balanced with a little sugar in the batter. The cake shows up from September through the winter holidays.

Why it tastes this way

A cranberry’s acid and pectin come from a bog plant adapted to sandy, wet ground. That acid cuts a rich batter and can taste harsh if the pieces are large. A little sugar in the cake, plus maple at the table, is how New England makes the fruit breakfast-sweet without turning it into cranberry sauce. The geography is the bog, not a generic berry patch.

How it is made

Chopped fresh cranberries are folded into a lightly sweetened buttermilk batter.

Authentic touch. Chop the berries. Whole cranberries pop, roll, and stay sour in one bite. A dust of sugar on the chopped fruit before it goes in helps.

How it is used

Butter and maple syrup. A spoon of cranberry sauce on top is redundant but very much of the region in November.

Field notes

  • This is a modern breakfast. It is not presented as a 1621 dish.
  • Chopping the berries is what makes the cake pleasant rather than a mouthful of acid.

Ingredients

  • 3/4 cup fresh cranberries, chopped
  • 1 tablespoon sugar, for the berries
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter

Steps

  1. Toss the chopped cranberries with 1 tablespoon sugar.
  2. Mix the batter and fold in the berries.
  3. Rest 5 minutes.
  4. Cook on a buttered medium griddle until set through.
  5. Flip once. The berries can stain the pan. That is normal.
  6. Serve with maple syrup.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Dried cranberries, soaked 10 minutes, work when fresh bogs are out of season.
  • Orange zest is a common holiday addition and is optional.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Holiday table
  • maple syrup
  • butter
  • cranberry sauce
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Carolina Corn Griddle Cakes

Sweet-corn meal, buttermilk, and a crisper edge than a wheat cake.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Corn was the daily grain of the Carolinas long before wheat pancakes filled a diner menu. Indigenous farmers in the region grew corn, and later Black and white Southern cooks turned meal into breads, mush, and griddle cakes. This cake is the breakfast form: cornmeal loosened with buttermilk and an egg, cooked in rounds.

Cultural background

It belongs with breakfast or with a plate of vegetables. It is closer to a hoe cake than to a fluffy flour pancake, with enough egg and buttermilk to make it tender.

Why it tastes this way

Coarse meal keeps a sandy, toasted flavor that fine wheat flour cannot. Buttermilk softens that grit and adds acid. The coastal and piedmont South could rely on corn when wheat was a bought luxury, which is why so many Carolina breads start with meal.

How it is made

Cornmeal, buttermilk, egg, and a little chemical leavener are cooked as small thick cakes.

Authentic touch. Let the batter stand 10 minutes so the meal swells. If you cook it at once, the center tastes raw.

How it is used

Butter, honey, or a savory plate of eggs and greens. They do not need a heavy sweet syrup.

Field notes

  • Resting the meal is the step that keeps the center from tasting like dry polenta.
  • Plain cornmeal keeps the cake gluten-free. Corn muffin mix usually does not.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup plain cornmeal
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/4 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir the dry ingredients. Whisk in buttermilk, egg, and butter.
  2. Rest 10 minutes.
  3. Cook on a buttered medium griddle as small thick rounds.
  4. Flip when the edges look dry.
  5. Cook until the center is set.
  6. Serve hot.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of flour makes them softer and removes the gluten-free label.
  • Cracklings in the batter are an older savory turn and add meat.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • Side dish
  • butter
  • honey
  • eggs
  • greens
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

India · No chili heat · about 8 crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Chakuli Pitha

A soft, sour rice-and-lentil crepe from Odisha, a dosa cousin.

40 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 crepes

History

Chakuli pitha is an Odia fermented crepe of rice and black gram, clearly in the dosa family and named as a pitha. It is breakfast food, served with vegetable curries or a sweet side. The ferment is shorter or longer depending on the weather. It is not a copy of a Tamil dosa. It is the Odisha cake.

Cultural background

The texture is often a little thicker and softer than a crisp restaurant dosa. Coconut or a potato curry can sit beside it.

Why it tastes this way

Rice and urad, fermented, taste sour for the same reason dosa does. Odisha’s pitha tradition gives the cake its name and its place in a regional breakfast, often next to other rice dishes. The climate’s warmth is what makes the ferment reliable in season. A softer cook keeps it a pitha rather than a wafer.

How it is made

Soaked rice and urad dal are ground, fermented, and cooked as soft crepes.

Authentic touch. Spread it a little thicker than paper dosa and cook it on medium. If you crisp it to a cracker, you have made a different dosa.

How it is used

With a vegetable curry or coconut chutney.

Field notes

  • Chakuli is Odisha’s fermented rice-dal crepe.
  • It belongs with pitha as well as with dosa.

Ingredients

  • 3/4 cup rice
  • 1/4 cup urad dal
  • Water
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Soak rice and dal 6 hours. Grind smooth.
  2. Ferment 8 to 12 hours until slightly risen.
  3. Add salt and thin if needed.
  4. Cook as medium-thin crepes on a hot oiled tawa until set and lightly browned.
  5. Serve warm.
  6. They should still bend.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chitau pitha is the holey Odia rice cake.
  • A very crisp cook is closer to dosa. Both are legitimate. This recipe aims at the softer side.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • vegetable curry
  • coconut chutney
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Chitau Pitha

A soft, holey rice cake from Odisha, served with milk or molasses.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Chitau pitha is an Odia rice cake cooked to form holes, traditionally on a covered earthen pan, and associated with festivals including the month of Shravan. It is eaten with milk, ghee, or molasses. This home version uses a covered nonstick pan and serves it with molasses so the cake itself stays vegan. Ghee is the dairy variation.

Cultural background

The look is close to chitoi and baghrir: a sponge on top, a soft body. The grain is rice.

Why it tastes this way

Steam in a covered pan makes the holes, and rice batter tastes clean and slightly sweet. Festival calendars in Odisha give the cake a season. Molasses soaks the sponge. Milk, if you use it, makes it a milder breakfast. The covered cook is the technique. An open pan just dries the batter.

How it is made

Rice-flour batter is cooked covered until holey and served with molasses.

Authentic touch. Cover the pan and resist flipping. The top should look set and pitted before you take it off. A thick batter makes a dumpling. Keep it pourable.

How it is used

With molasses, or with warm milk if you eat dairy.

Field notes

  • Chitau pitha is the holey Odia rice cake.
  • A covered pan is the home stand-in for the traditional covered griddle.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup rice flour
  • 1 1/4 cups water
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • Molasses, to serve

Steps

  1. Whisk a thin batter and rest 10 minutes.
  2. Cook small rounds in a lightly oiled covered pan over medium until the top is holey and set.
  3. Do not flip.
  4. Serve with molasses.
  5. Keep the heat moderate.
  6. Eat them fresh.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Ghee on top is a festive finish and adds dairy.
  • Chakuli is the fermented crepe, not this cake.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • molasses
  • milk
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Bangladesh · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Bangladesh · South Asia

Chitoi Pitha

A soft rice cake full of holes, like a Bengali cousin of baghrir.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Chitoi pitha is a Bengali rice cake cooked in a special pan that encourages holes, or in a covered pan that steams the top. It is a winter pitha, often eaten with molasses or with a savory filling in some households. The batter is rice, not wheat. It is part of the pitha season that also produces patishapta.

Cultural background

The holes soak up molasses. A savory chitoi exists. This is the plain rice cake with molasses.

Why it tastes this way

Rice batter on a moderate covered pan forms holes the way baghrir does, from steam escaping. The cake tastes of rice and, when molasses is poured on, of the jaggery that defines Bengali winter sweets. No ferment is required for this home version, so it is milder than baghrir. The pan and the cover do the work.

How it is made

A thin rice-flour batter is cooked covered on one side until holey, then finished with molasses.

Authentic touch. Cook covered and do not flip. Pour molasses while the cake is hot so it runs into the holes. A thin batter makes more holes than a thick one.

How it is used

Hot, with molasses or jaggery syrup.

Field notes

  • Chitoi is a holey rice cake. The holes are for the molasses.
  • It is a winter pitha, not a fermented dosa.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup rice flour
  • 1 1/4 cups water
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • Molasses or melted jaggery, to serve

Steps

  1. Whisk rice flour, water, and salt into a thin batter. Rest 10 minutes.
  2. Oil a nonstick pan lightly and heat it to medium.
  3. Pour a small round, cover, and cook until the top is set and holey. Do not flip.
  4. Serve with molasses.
  5. Keep the batter thin.
  6. Eat them hot.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A browned garlic and chile topping makes a savory chitoi.
  • Patishapta is the filled crepe of the same season.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • molasses
  • jaggery
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Costa Rica · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Costa Rica · Mexico & Central America

Chorreadas

Sweet fresh-corn pancakes, cousins of cachapas.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Chorreadas are Costa Rican fresh-corn pancakes, cooked on a griddle and served with sour cream or a crumbly cheese. They are the local sister of Venezuelan cachapas and Nicaraguan güirilas. The batter is corn. This written cake is vegan. Sour cream is the usual partner and adds dairy at the table.

Cultural background

They are a bit irregular. Sweet corn is the requirement. They are breakfast or a snack, not a stuffed dinner.

Why it tastes this way

Fresh corn’s sugar browns on a griddle and tastes like the season. A Costa Rican table often meets that sweetness with sour cream, the same sweet-salt idea as a cheese cachapa. No wheat is needed. The cake is a Central American corn pancake with its own name.

How it is made

Fresh corn is blended with a pinch of salt and cooked as small pancakes.

Authentic touch. Blend to a coarse batter and cook on medium. Cover for a minute if the center is slow. They should be spotted, not burnt.

How it is used

With sour cream or cheese if you eat dairy, or plain.

Field notes

  • Chorreadas are Costa Rica’s fresh-corn pancakes.
  • Sour cream is a table partner, not required in the batter.

Ingredients

  • 4 ears sweet corn
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon masa harina, if needed
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Blend corn and salt to a coarse batter.
  2. Cook small cakes on a medium oiled griddle until spotted and set.
  3. Serve plain or with sour cream.
  4. They should taste of corn.
  5. Cover briefly if the center is slow.
  6. Eat them the same day.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of milk in the blend adds dairy and richness.
  • Cachapas and güirilas are the neighboring cakes.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • sour cream
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free
