United Kingdom · No chili heat · about 10 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Crempog

Thick, buttermilk-rich Welsh pancakes, often stacked with butter.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 10 pancakes

History

Crempogau are Welsh pancakes, thicker than a crepe, made with buttermilk or sour milk. They are associated with celebrations and with ordinary tea, and recipes vary by household. Some are stacked with butter between the layers. They are a Welsh cake in the pancake family, distinct from griddled Welsh cakes that are a currant dough.

Cultural background

A crempog is cooked in spoonfuls and eaten warm. The plural is crempogau. Butter is part of serving, not only of cooking.

Why it tastes this way

Buttermilk makes them tangy and tender, and a bit of butter in the batter makes them rich enough to stack. Wales’s dairy country is the reason sour milk was the liquid on hand. They taste less of sugar than a Scotch pancake and less of lemon than an English Shrove pancake. Butter between the layers melts and salts the stack.

How it is made

A buttermilk batter is cooked as small thick pancakes and stacked with butter.

Authentic touch. Cook them thicker than a crepe and stack them while hot so the butter you spread between them melts. A cold crempog with hard butter is a lesser thing.

How it is used

Butter, and honey or jam if you want them sweeter.

Field notes

  • Crempog and Welsh cake are both Welsh and are not the same recipe.
  • Stacking with butter is a traditional way to serve them.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/4 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 3/4 cup buttermilk
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter, plus more to stack

Steps

  1. Stir the dry ingredients together, then stir in the wet ingredients until the batter is combined.
  2. Rest 5 minutes.
  3. Heat a griddle or heavy skillet over medium heat and film it with butter.
  4. Spoon on the batter. Cook until the underside is browned and the top looks set.
  5. Flip once and cook the second side through.
  6. Stack the cakes with butter between them and serve warm.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Some recipes use a yeast batter. This one uses baking powder and soda for a same-day cake.
  • Welsh cakes are the currant girdle cake on the next page, not a crempog.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Holiday table
  • butter
  • honey
  • jam
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United States · No chili heat · about 12 fritters

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Creole Calas

Fried rice, warm spice, and a crisp shell.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 12 fritters

History

Calas are New Orleans rice fritters. In the nineteenth century, Black Creole women sold them on the streets, and the cry of “calas” was part of the city’s morning. The fritter is widely connected to West African rice cooking carried by enslaved people into Louisiana, where rice was already a crop. They are fried cakes, not flipped pancakes, and they belong in this journal as the rice cousin of the New Orleans griddle.

Cultural background

Cooked leftover rice is mixed with flour, egg, and spice, then fried. They were a street breakfast. Some vendors were free women of color who built small businesses around the fritter.

Why it tastes this way

Rice is the starch because coastal Louisiana grew it. A day-old pot of rice, not raw grains, is what makes the interior custardy. Nutmeg and a little sugar are the Creole seasoning. The fry, rather than a dry griddle, is why the outside shatters and the inside stays soft.

How it is made

Cooked rice, egg, flour, sugar, and spice form a batter that is dropped into hot oil and fried.

Authentic touch. Use cold cooked rice and break it up with a fork, but do not puree it. You should still see grains in the crumb.

How it is used

Dust with powdered sugar and eat them hot, the way they were sold in the morning.

Field notes

  • Calas are a fried rice cake. They are not beignets, which are a yeast dough.
  • Street selling by Black Creole women is part of the documented city history of the fritter.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups cooked leftover rice, cold
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1/2 cup all-purpose flour
  • 3 tablespoons sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon nutmeg
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • Neutral oil for frying
  • Powdered sugar, to serve

Steps

  1. Beat the eggs and stir in the rice, crushing it slightly.
  2. Add flour, sugar, baking powder, nutmeg, and salt.
  3. Heat 1 inch of oil to about 350°F, or until a drop of batter sizzles at once.
  4. Fry spoonfuls until deep golden, turning once.
  5. Drain and dust with powdered sugar.
  6. Eat them hot.

Fry in a deep pan over steady heat. Do not leave hot oil unattended.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A pinch of yeast and a rest makes a lighter fritter. Baking powder is the quicker home version written here.
  • Without nutmeg they taste plainer. The spice is part of the New Orleans habit.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Snack
  • powdered sugar
  • coffee
  • café au lait
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · about 8 crumpets

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Crumpets

Spongy, salty, and full of holes, made to be toasted and buttered.

1 hr 20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 crumpets

History

Crumpets are English griddle cakes cooked in rings so the batter stays tall and forms holes on top. They are related to earlier muffins and pikelets. The modern crumpet, with its yeast or leavener and its tea-table role, settled in the nineteenth century as a bakery and home cake. You toast them. You do not eat them as a floppy pancake straight from the first cook, though a cook may sneak one.

Cultural background

Butter is the topping that fills the holes. Cheese, or butter and jam, are the other honest uses. They are toast more than they are a stack.

Why it tastes this way

A wet, yeasted batter in a hot ring sets at the bottom while bubbles burst on top and leave holes. Those holes are the point, because melted butter runs into them. Baking soda at the end gives a last burst of bubbles. The taste is wheat and salt, not sugar. Sugar would darken the holes and turn them into a cake.

How it is made

A yeast batter is thinned, finished with baking soda, and cooked in greased rings on a griddle until the top is full of holes.

Authentic touch. The batter must be loose, almost pourable. If it is thick, you get a pikelet with no holes. Cook on medium-low so the bottom does not burn before the top sets.

How it is used

Toasted, with butter. They should be heated through so the butter melts.

Field notes

  • Holes are the test of a crumpet. A smooth top means the batter was too thick or the pan too cool.
  • They are meant to be toasted, not only eaten soft from the first griddle.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup warm milk
  • 1/2 cup warm water
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 2 tablespoons warm water, for the soda

Steps

  1. Stir flour, yeast, sugar, salt, milk, and 1/2 cup water into a thick batter. Cover 1 hour, until bubbly.
  2. Dissolve the soda in 2 tablespoons water and stir it in. The batter should be pourable.
  3. Grease crumpet rings and a medium-low griddle.
  4. Fill each ring halfway. Cook until the top is set and holey.
  5. Remove the rings, flip, and brown the top briefly.
  6. Toast before serving with butter.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Yorkshire pikelets are a close, often ringless relative.
  • Midlands pikelets are thicker drop scones and are a different page.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Bread course
  • butter
  • jam
  • cheese
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

Denmark · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Denmark · Northern Europe

Danish Pandekager

Thin, lightly sweet pancakes rolled with jam or sugar.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Pandekager are Denmark’s thin pancakes, close to a crêpe, served with jam, sugar, or ice cream. They are a common dessert. Æbleskiver are the other famous Danish pancake, round and cooked in a special pan, and they are not the same dish.

Cultural background

Children and adults both expect them to be thin enough to roll. Strawberry jam is a usual filling. The batter is egg and milk.

Why it tastes this way

A thin egg batter tastes delicately of vanilla or sugar if you add them, and of browned butter from the pan. Denmark’s dairy is the richness. Jam supplies acid and fruit in a season when fresh berries are not on the table. The cake is a vehicle for that jam, which is why it stays thin.

How it is made

A thin batter is cooked in a buttered pan and rolled with jam.

Authentic touch. Swirl a thin layer. A thick pandekage is just a pancake from somewhere else. Sugar the cake while it is hot if you are not using jam.

How it is used

With jam or a dust of sugar. Ice cream makes it a dessert course.

Field notes

  • Pandekager are thin. Æbleskiver are the round pan cakes.
  • Jam or sugar is the usual finish.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1 1/2 cups milk
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 teaspoon vanilla
  • Butter for the pan
  • Jam, to serve

Steps

  1. Whisk the batter and rest 10 minutes.
  2. Cook thin pancakes in butter.
  3. Spread with jam and roll.
  4. Serve warm.
  5. Stack extras under a towel.
  6. They reheat in a dry pan.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Æbleskiver are the spherical cousins.
  • A squeeze of lemon is less Danish than jam, and still good.

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • jam
  • sugar
  • ice cream
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United Kingdom · No chili heat · about 6 oatcakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Derbyshire Oatcakes

A soft oat pancake, a neighbor to the Staffordshire cake.

1 hr 10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 6 oatcakes

History

Derbyshire oatcakes are the neighboring county’s soft oat pancake, sold and cooked at home in a tradition that overlaps Staffordshire’s. They are large, yeasted, and savory. Treating them as a separate named cake respects the county line cooks actually use, without inventing a dramatic difference that is not there. The batter is oat-forward and flexible.

Cultural background

They are breakfast wrappers for cheese or bacon, much like Staffordshire oatcakes. Some Derbyshire versions are a little thicker.

Why it tastes this way

The same industrial Midlands pantry, oats plus a little wheat and yeast, produces a savory pancake because oats were cheaper than a white-flour diet and yeast made a bread you could fold. Small differences in thickness and sweetness are house differences. The flavor is oat and salt.

How it is made

An oat and wheat yeast batter is cooked as soft pancakes on a griddle.

Authentic touch. If you want them a little thicker than Staffordshire cakes, use a bit less water. Do not add sugar. Sweet oatcakes are a different British food.

How it is used

Cheese or eggs. Roll or fold them.

Field notes

  • Derbyshire and Staffordshire oatcakes are close relatives with local names.
  • Neither one is a crisp Scottish oatcake.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/4 cups oat flour
  • 1/4 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/4 cups warm water
  • 1 egg
  • Oil for the griddle

Steps

  1. Mix all the batter ingredients and rest 1 hour.
  2. Cook thin rounds on a medium oiled griddle.
  3. Flip when the top sets.
  4. Stack under a towel.
  5. Fill while warm.
  6. They keep a day in the fridge and reheat in a dry pan.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • The Staffordshire page is the twin, with the shop tradition of Stoke called out there.
  • A pinch of sugar appears in some home recipes. This one stays savory.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • cheese
  • eggs
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · Mild · one 8-inch cake

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Dibba Rotti

A thick, pan-fried rice and dal cake, crisp outside and soft inside.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · one 8-inch cake

History

Dibba rotti is an Andhra dish: a thick cake of rice and lentil batter, often fermented idli or dosa batter, cooked in a deep pan with oil so the outside fries and the inside stays soft. It is cut into wedges. It is a tiffin food, richer than a steamed idli and thicker than a dosa.

Cultural background

Mustard, cumin, and onion are often mixed into the batter. The pan is covered for part of the cook. This is closer to a thick pancake than idli is, which is why it is here and steamed idli is not.

Why it tastes this way

Oil in a deep pan fries the crust while the thick ferment steams inside, so you taste sour batter and a fried edge at once. Onion and spices make it a snack rather than a plain bread. Andhra tiffin uses the same fermented batter in several shapes. Dibba rotti is the fried thick one.

How it is made

Seasoned fermented batter is cooked as one thick cake in an oiled covered pan, then cut.

Authentic touch. Use a small skillet so the cake is deep enough to stay soft inside. Cover it. Uncover at the end to crisp the top. Do not try to make it as thin as dosa.

How it is used

In wedges, with chutney.

Field notes

  • Dibba rotti is thick and pan-cooked, not steamed like idli.
  • The same batter can make dosa. The pan and the thickness are the dish.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups fermented dosa or idli batter
  • 1 small onion, chopped
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin
  • 2 tablespoons oil
  • Salt

Steps

  1. Stir onion, chile, cumin, and salt into the batter.
  2. Heat oil in a small skillet, pour in the batter, and spread it about 3/4 inch thick.
  3. Cover and cook on medium-low until the bottom is crisp and the center is set.
  4. Flip or brown the top under a broiler.
  5. Rest 5 minutes and cut.
  6. Serve with chutney.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Leftover idli batter is the usual start.
  • A spicy gunpowder sprinkle is a condiment, not the cake.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • coconut chutney
  • pickle
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Dried Apple Pancakes

Soft wheat cake with tart, plumped dried apple.

25 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Drying apple slices was a standard way to keep the orchard through an Appalachian winter. Dried-apple stack cakes, a layered cake, are a famous mountain dessert and are not this recipe. These pancakes use the same pantry fruit in a breakfast batter: apples soaked until soft, then folded in. The dried fruit, not a fresh supermarket apple, is the mountain detail.

Cultural background

Winter breakfast, when the root cellar and the dried-fruit strings were what remained of the orchard. Fresh apple pancakes are a different, juicier cake.

Why it tastes this way

Drying concentrates acid and perfume and removes water, so the fruit tastes tarter than a fresh slice and does not flood the batter. Soaking brings back enough moisture to make it tender. The method is about climate: a short growing season and a long winter made dried fruit the reliable sweetness.

How it is made

Dried apples are soaked, chopped, and folded into a buttermilk batter.

Authentic touch. Soak the apples until they are supple, then chop them. Hard chips stay dry in the crumb and scorch.

How it is used

Butter. They are already fruity, so syrup can be light.

Field notes

  • Dried-apple stack cake is a layered special-occasion cake. These are breakfast pancakes that use the same fruit.
  • Soaking is not optional if the apples are leathery.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup dried apple slices
  • 1 cup hot water, for soaking
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter

Steps

  1. Soak the apples in hot water 15 minutes. Drain, chop, and pat dry.
  2. Mix the batter and fold in the apples.
  3. Rest 5 minutes.
  4. Cook on a buttered medium griddle.
  5. Flip once. Pieces of apple brown faster than the batter.
  6. Serve warm.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A pinch of cinnamon is common. Some mountain cooks use none.
  • Fresh apples are a fair summer swap and a different texture.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • butter
  • sorghum
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

Netherlands · No chili heat · 4 large pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Netherlands · Western Europe

Dutch Pannenkoeken

A large, thin-but-sturdy pancake, often eaten with bacon or apple.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 large pancakes

History

Pannenkoeken are the Dutch pancake: larger and a bit thicker than a French crêpe, cooked one to a plate, and eaten with sweet or savory toppings. Pannenkoeken restaurants serve them as a meal. Bacon cooked into the batter, or apple, or a syrup pour, are all ordinary. They are not the American Dutch baby, whose name comes from Deutsch, and they are not poffertjes.

Cultural background

A family pan cooks one large cake. You eat it with a knife and fork. Stroop, a syrup, or powdered sugar are sweet finishes. Cheese and bacon are the savory ones.

Why it tastes this way

A batter with more flour than a crêpe stays sturdy enough to carry bacon or apple and to be eaten as dinner. Dairy and pork on the same plate reflect a farming kitchen that had milk, eggs, and a slice of spek. The cake tastes mildly of egg and butter, and the topping does the rest. Plate-sized cooking is why the edge is crisper than the center.

How it is made

A pourable batter is cooked as one large pancake, with apple or bacon added in the pan if you are using them.

Authentic touch. Spread the batter to the edge of a wide pan and do not stack them if you can help it. A pannenkoek is one cake on a plate. Cover the pan so the center cooks before the edge dries out.

How it is used

With stroop or powdered sugar, or with cheese. Bacon makes it not vegetarian. The batter below is the plain cake.

Field notes

  • Dutch pannenkoeken are not Dutch babies. The American name points at German pancakes.
  • One large cake per person is the usual serving.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/4 cups all-purpose flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1 1/2 cups milk
  • Pinch of salt
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter
  • Butter for the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk into a smooth, pourable batter. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Heat a wide skillet and butter it.
  3. Pour a generous layer and tilt to cover.
  4. Cook covered on medium until the top sets, then flip.
  5. The center should be cooked, not wet.
  6. Serve flat on a plate with stroop, sugar, or cheese.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Spekdik is the bacon pancake of the northeast, on its own page.
  • Appelpannenkoek has its own page too.

Pairings and uses

  • Supper
  • Savory meal
  • Breakfast
  • stroop
  • powdered sugar
  • apple
  • cheese
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

India · Mild · 4 dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Egg Dosa

A fermented dosa with an egg cracked on top and cooked until set.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 dosas

History

Egg dosa is a tiffin-room and home variation: a dosa topped with a beaten or cracked egg, chile, and onion. It is a modern, widely cooked breakfast, not a temple food. The batter is the fermented rice-dal batter. The egg is the addition. It is vegetarian in the Indian sense that includes eggs, and it is not vegan.

Cultural background

The egg sets on the hot dosa. Some cooks scramble it on the crepe. Some leave it sunny-side. This recipe spreads a beaten egg so it cooks through.

Why it tastes this way

Egg adds richness and protein to a tangy crepe and browns at the edge where it meets the hot tawa. Onion and chile keep it savory. It is a restaurant habit of the same shops that sell plain dosa, a way to make the crepe a fuller breakfast. The ferment is still the sour note underneath.

How it is made

A dosa is cooked, topped with beaten egg, onion, and chile, covered until the egg sets, and folded.

Authentic touch. Pour the egg on the dosa while it is still on the heat and cover the pan so the egg sets. A raw egg on a plated dosa is not the dish.

How it is used

With chutney.

Field notes

  • Egg dosa is a popular variation, not a separate ancient batter.
  • The egg should be cooked, not raw on the plate.

Ingredients

  • 4 ladles fermented dosa batter
  • 4 large eggs
  • 1 small onion, finely chopped
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • Salt
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Beat each egg with a pinch of salt, a little onion, and chile. Keep them separate.
  2. Cook a dosa on a hot oiled tawa.
  3. Pour one egg mixture over it, spread, and cover until the egg sets.
  4. Fold and serve.
  5. Repeat.
  6. Chutney on the side.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A cracked whole egg, like an appam egg, is a different look. Cook the white through.
  • Plain dosa is the egg-free page.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • coconut chutney
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 8 muffins

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

English Muffins

A yeasted griddle bread with a toasted, nubby crumb.

1 hr 30 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 8 · 8 muffins

History

What Americans call an English muffin is a yeasted griddle bread, related to the muffins sold by muffin men in English streets in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries. Those muffins were cooked on a griddle, not baked in a tin like a modern American muffin. The fork-split, nooks-and-crannies style was industrialized later, especially in the United States, but the method is the griddle.

Cultural background

They are toasted and buttered, or used under eggs. This is a home version cooked in a covered skillet.

Why it tastes this way

Yeast gives a bread flavor that baking powder cannot, and a griddle crust on both sides is why the outside tastes toasted while the inside stays soft. Cornmeal on the board keeps them from sticking and adds a faint grit. They are a city bread that moved from street sellers to a breakfast industry.

How it is made

A soft yeast dough is shaped into rounds, dusted with cornmeal, and cooked slowly on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Cook them covered, on low heat, longer than a pancake. If the heat is high you get a raw ring inside a burnt crust. Split them with a fork, not a knife, if you want a rough surface for butter.

How it is used

Toasted, with butter, or as the base of eggs.

Field notes

  • English muffins are griddled, which is why they are in a pancake journal.
  • Splitting with a fork gives the rough crumb people toast.

Ingredients

  • 2 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup warm milk
  • 1 tablespoon oil
  • Cornmeal, for dusting

Steps

  1. Mix the dough until soft and slightly sticky. Knead briefly. Rise 1 hour.
  2. Divide into 8 rounds, dust with cornmeal, and rest 20 minutes.
  3. Cook on a medium-low dry or lightly oiled griddle, covered, about 8 minutes a side.
  4. The sides should look set, not doughy.
  5. Cool before splitting.
  6. Toast to serve.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A sweeter dough is a home choice. The classic muffin is only mildly sweet.
  • Whole-meal flour makes a heartier bread that cooks the same way.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • butter
  • jam
  • eggs
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

Estonia · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Estonia · Eastern Europe

Estonian Pannkoogid

Thin pancakes, rolled with jam or served with savory fillings.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Pannkoogid are Estonian pancakes, thin and crepe-like, influenced by the same Baltic and Nordic habits as Finland’s lettu and Russia’s blini, without being either. Vastlapäev, Estonia’s Shrove Tuesday, is associated with pea soup and whipped-cream buns more than with these pancakes. This page does not claim pannkoogid as the Vastlapäev dish. They are ordinary dessert and supper crepes.

Cultural background

Jam, condensed milk in some modern homes, or a savory minced filling are ways to eat them. The recipe is the plain cake with jam.

Why it tastes this way

A thin egg batter tastes mild and buttery, a base for jam in a kitchen that also bakes cardamom rolls. Estonia’s place between Nordic and Russian food shows up in the pancake’s thinness, closer to a Nordic crepe than to a thick yeasted blin. The holiday honesty matters: Vastlapäev has its own buns, and this cake is not those buns.

How it is made

A thin batter is cooked as crepes and rolled with jam.

Authentic touch. Cook them thin and roll with a modest amount of jam. Condensed milk is a modern sweetness and much heavier.

How it is used

Warm, with jam.

Field notes

  • Vastlapäev is more strongly tied to pea soup and cream buns than to these pancakes.
  • Pannkoogid are still the Estonian crepe for ordinary days.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1 1/4 cups milk
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter
  • Jam, to serve

Steps

  1. Whisk the batter.
  2. Cook thin pancakes in a buttered pan.
  3. Roll with jam.
  4. Serve warm.
  5. Flip only long enough to set the second side.
  6. Keep them pliable.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A savory meat filling makes them a meal.
  • Finnish lettu is the close thin pancake across the gulf.

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • jam
  • sour cream
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Philippines · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Philippines · Southeast Asia

Filipino Hotcakes

A soft breakfast pancake, often served with butter and sugar or syrup.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Filipino hotcakes are the breakfast pancakes of home kitchens and fast-food breakfasts, a wheat baking-powder cake served with butter and sugar or syrup. They are a twentieth-century breakfast, not bibingka and not a rice cake. Condensed milk on top is a local sweetness.

Cultural background

They are thick enough to stack and soft rather than crisp. Butter and sugar, not maple, are the usual home finish.

Why it tastes this way

The batter tastes mild and eggy, a café pancake. Condensed milk is the Filipino pour, cooked milk and sugar, closer to the region’s sweets than maple is. The cake is an adopted breakfast that became ordinary. It sits in this journal beside bibingka so the two are not confused.

How it is made

A simple milk batter is cooked as pancakes and finished with butter and condensed milk or sugar.

Authentic touch. Cook on medium and do not press them. A spoon of condensed milk on the hot cake is enough. More makes them soggy.

How it is used

Stacked, warm.

Field notes

  • Filipino hotcakes are the wheat breakfast pancake.
  • Condensed milk is a common local pour.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup milk
  • 1 egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter
  • Condensed milk, to serve

Steps

  1. Mix a batter and rest 5 minutes.
  2. Cook on a buttered medium griddle.
  3. Serve with butter and a spoon of condensed milk.
  4. Do not press them flat.
  5. They should be soft.
  6. Bibingka is a different cake.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Ripe banana mashed into the batter is a home variation.
  • Bibingka is the rice cake of Christmas, not this.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • condensed milk
  • butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Finland · No chili heat · about 16 small cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Finland · Northern Europe

Finnish Blini

Small yeasted buckwheat cakes for the Shrovetide table.

1 hr 30 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 16 small cakes

History

Finland’s Laskiainen is the Shrovetide festival, with pea soup and cream buns as the foods most often named. Blini are also served in Finland, especially in communities with Russian influence, as small yeasted buckwheat cakes with toppings such as sour cream or fish roe. This page is those blini, not a claim that they replaced pea soup. The Orthodox and eastern connection explains the cake better than a generic “Finnish pancake” label.

Cultural background

They are small, risen, and eaten with savory or rich toppings. Roe adds fish. This recipe stops at the cake and sour cream.

Why it tastes this way

Buckwheat and yeast taste deeper and tangier than a baking-powder pancake, which is why blini suit sour cream and salt rather than maple. The eastern European method moved into Finnish tables through shared borders and Orthodox tradition. Shrovetide is a late-winter feast before the fast, which is why rich toppings show up then.

How it is made

A yeasted batter of buckwheat and wheat is risen and cooked as small cakes.

Authentic touch. Let the batter rise until it is bubbly. Flat blini are just buckwheat pancakes. The rise is the dish. Cook them small.

How it is used

Sour cream, melted butter, or fish roe if you eat fish. The roe is not in the batter.

Field notes

  • Laskiainen foods most famously include pea soup and buns. Blini are also served and are not a substitute story.
  • The yeast rise is what makes them blini rather than lettu.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup buckwheat flour
  • 1/2 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup warm milk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir the batter and rest it 1 hour, until bubbly.
  2. Cook small cakes on a buttered medium griddle.
  3. Flip when the tops are full of holes and set.
  4. Serve warm with sour cream.
  5. They should taste faintly tangy.
  6. Do not make them large or the center stays heavy.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Russian blini are the larger tradition this cake belongs to.
  • A purely wheat batter is milder and less typical.

Pairings and uses

  • Holiday table
  • Savory meal
  • Breakfast
  • sour cream
  • melted butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Finland · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Finland · Northern Europe

Finnish Lettu

A thin Finnish pancake, often served with jam or whipped cream.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Lettuja, also called ohukaisia, are Finland’s thin pancakes, related to Swedish pancakes across the Baltic. They are a home dessert, rolled or folded with jam. A thicker baked pannukakku is the other Finnish pancake and has its own page, including an Upper Peninsula version.

Cultural background

They are everyday food, not only holiday food. Berry jam from the summer, especially lingonberry or strawberry, is the filling.

Why it tastes this way

A thin batter cooks quickly in butter and tastes mild, which is why the jam matters. Finland’s berry woods are the fruit source, preserved for winter. The pancake itself is egg and milk and flour, a dairy-country dessert. Cardamom is optional here. Many Finnish pancakes are plain and let the berries speak.

How it is made

A thin egg batter is cooked in a buttered pan and folded with jam.

Authentic touch. Cook them thin enough to fold without a crack. Resting the batter helps. The first lettu will look worse than the rest.

How it is used

Jam, whipped cream, or a both.

Field notes

  • Lettu and ohukainen both name the thin Finnish pancake.
  • The baked pannukakku is a different dish.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1 1/2 cups milk
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter
  • Jam, to serve

Steps

  1. Whisk the batter smooth.
  2. Cook thin pancakes in a buttered skillet.
  3. Fill with jam and fold.
  4. Serve warm.
  5. Whipped cream is optional.
  6. Do not overbrown them or they will not fold.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Pannukakku is baked.
  • A savory filling is less usual than jam.

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • jam
  • whipped cream
  • berries
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Finland · No chili heat · one 9-by-13-inch pan

Flying Pancakes of the World

Finland · Northern Europe

Finnish Pannukakku

A cardamom-scented baked pancake, custardy in the center.

10 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 8 · one 9-by-13-inch pan

History

Pannukakku is Finland’s oven pancake, baked in a buttered pan and cut into squares. Cardamom is a frequent flavor, as it is in Finnish pulla. The Upper Peninsula of Michigan bakes a community version of the same dish, written separately because the American context is part of that story.

Cultural background

It is breakfast or a simple dessert with jam. One pan replaces a stack of flipped cakes.

Why it tastes this way

Cardamom’s perfume in a milky baked batter is the Finnish signature, a spice that became ordinary in Nordic baking through trade and stayed in Sunday buns and this pancake. The oven gives a browned rim and a soft middle. Jam adds the berry. The method is practical in a kitchen that is already heating the oven.

How it is made

Eggs, milk, flour, sugar, and cardamom are baked in a hot buttered pan.

Authentic touch. Melt the butter in the pan in the oven so the batter sizzles when it goes in. That sound is the bottom crust forming.

How it is used

With jam or berries.

Field notes

  • Cardamom is a traditional Finnish baking spice.
  • Pannukakku is baked. Lettu is cooked in a skillet.

Ingredients

  • 3 large eggs
  • 2 cups milk
  • 1 cup flour
  • 1/4 cup sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1/2 teaspoon cardamom
  • 3 tablespoons butter

Steps

  1. Heat the oven to 425°F and melt the butter in the pan in the oven.
  2. Whisk the rest into a smooth batter.
  3. Pour into the hot pan.
  4. Bake 25 to 30 minutes until puffed at the edges and set.
  5. It will fall when cut.
  6. Serve with jam.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • The Upper Peninsula page is the Finnish American pan.
  • Lettus are the thin flipped version.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • jam
  • berries
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Georgia Peach Pancakes

Juicy peach in a lightly sweet buttermilk cake.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Georgia’s peach orchards are a real commercial crop, famous since the late nineteenth century, though South Carolina also grows peaches at scale. Pancakes with fresh peach slices are a modern breakfast that uses the local fruit in season. They are not a documented antebellum recipe. Out of season, the honest move is to wait or use frozen peaches, not a syrup that only tastes like peach candy.

Cultural background

Roadside stands and home kitchens in peach country cook this in summer. The cake is ordinary buttermilk batter. The fruit is the news.

Why it tastes this way

A ripe peach is sweet, acidic, and full of water. That water keeps the crumb tender and can also make the center gummy if the slices are piled on too thick. Summer heat is why the fruit is here at all: peaches want a warm season. A pinch of sugar in the batter is enough because the fruit already carries sugar.

How it is made

Buttermilk pancakes are cooked with ripe peach slices set into the top before the flip.

Authentic touch. Pat the slices dry and lay them on the batter after you pour it, rather than stirring raw peach into the bowl. The cakes brown instead of steaming.

How it is used

Butter, and only a little syrup. A spoon of plain yogurt is a tart contrast. Whipped cream makes it dessert.

Field notes

  • This is a modern seasonal breakfast, stated here as that, not as an old folk recipe.
  • South Carolina grows a large share of the commercial peach crop too.

Ingredients

  • 1 ripe peach, thinly sliced and patted dry
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir the dry ingredients together, then stir in the wet ingredients until the batter is combined.
  2. Pour the batter, then lay two or three peach slices on each cake before bubbles set.
  3. Heat a griddle or heavy skillet over medium heat and film it with butter.
  4. Spoon on the batter. Cook until the underside is browned and the top looks set.
  5. Flip once and cook the second side through.
  6. Flip carefully. The fruit side browns quickly, so keep the heat at medium.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Frozen peaches, thawed and dried, work in winter. Canned peaches in syrup are much sweeter.
  • A dust of nutmeg is enough spice. Peach pie spice mixes muddy the fruit.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • fresh peaches
  • butter
  • yogurt
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

Germany · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Germany · Western Europe

German Eierkuchen

An eggier German pancake, served with sugar or applesauce.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Eierkuchen are German egg pancakes. In much of Germany a Pfannkuchen is also a pancake, but in Berlin Pfannkuchen means a jam donut. The naming trap is real, so this page uses Eierkuchen for the pancake. They are thicker than a French crepe and are eaten with sugar, cinnamon, or applesauce.

Cultural background

A home supper can be pancakes, applesauce, and nothing else. That is a recognized simple meal, not an incomplete plate.

Why it tastes this way

More egg than a lean crepe makes them taste custardy and browned. Applesauce is the fruit of a German pantry and cuts the egg. Sugar and cinnamon are the other finish. The Berlin naming oddity is why a traveler can order a pancake and receive a donut. The egg in the name is the flavor clue.

How it is made

An egg-rich batter is cooked as medium pancakes and served with applesauce or sugar.

Authentic touch. Cook them in a little butter and serve them flat or folded, not rolled tight. They are sturdier than flensjes and will crack if you treat them like lace.

How it is used

Applesauce, or sugar and cinnamon.

Field notes

  • In Berlin, Pfannkuchen usually means a donut. Eierkuchen is the safer word for the pancake.
  • Applesauce is a standard partner.

Ingredients

  • 3 large eggs
  • 3/4 cup flour
  • 3/4 cup milk
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • Butter for the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk a smooth batter.
  2. Cook medium pancakes in butter until browned on both sides.
  3. Serve with applesauce or cinnamon sugar.
  4. They should be set in the center.
  5. Do not thin them to crepe consistency.
  6. Eat them fresh.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Berliner Pfannkuchen are donuts. Do not use that recipe for this cake.
  • Kartoffelpuffer are the potato pancakes.

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • applesauce
  • cinnamon sugar
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

India · No chili heat · about 8 crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Ghavan

A soft, white rice crepe from the Konkan coast, unfermented.

20 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 crepes

History

Ghavan is a Maharashtrian rice crepe, especially of the Konkan coast, made from soaked rice ground with water and cooked soft, in the family of neer dosa. It is often served with coconut chutney or a spicy potato dish. There is no dal ferment. The crepe is mild.

Cultural background

It is breakfast. The rice should be ground smooth. A lumpy ghavan is unpleasant and not traditional.

Why it tastes this way

Fresh rice batter without souring tastes sweet and delicate, a coastal choice where rice and coconut are the pantry. The Konkan coast’s food is not the same as a Deccan millet bread, and ghavan shows that: a soft white crepe, not a spicy thick pancake. Chutney brings the heat the batter does not have.

How it is made

Soaked rice is ground fine with water and cooked as soft crepes.

Authentic touch. Grind longer than you think. The batter should feel silky. Cook it pale. Browning it deeply makes it a cracker.

How it is used

With coconut chutney.

Field notes

  • Ghavan is an unfermented Maharashtrian rice crepe.
  • Smooth grinding is the step that matters most.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup rice, soaked 4 hours
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Grind rice, water, and salt until completely smooth. The batter should be thin.
  2. Cook soft crepes on a medium oiled nonstick pan until they lift.
  3. Fold and serve.
  4. Do not aim for a dark crust.
  5. Coconut chutney is the partner.
  6. Eat them immediately.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Neer dosa is the Karnataka cousin.
  • A pinch of cumin in the batter is a home variation.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • coconut chutney
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · Mild · about 8 dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Godhuma Dosa

A soft whole-wheat dosa, milder and quicker than a fermented rice dosa.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 dosas

History

Godhuma dosa is a wheat dosa from Andhra cooking, often instant, made with whole wheat flour and a little rice flour or cumin. It is a home breakfast when there is no fermented batter. Godhuma means wheat. It is not gluten-free, and it should not be described as a classic fermented dosa.

Cultural background

Onion and chile can be mixed in. This version keeps them in the batter so the cake is savory. It is eaten with chutney.

Why it tastes this way

Wheat flour makes a softer, breadier crepe than fermented rice, and it tastes of the grain’s sweetness. Cumin and chile are there because an unfermented batter would otherwise be plain. It is a practical Andhra home dosa, fast, and different in flavor from the overnight batter. The wheat is the name of the dish.

How it is made

Whole wheat flour, water, cumin, and chile are mixed and cooked as soft dosas.

Authentic touch. Rest 15 minutes so the atta hydrates. Cook on medium and keep it supple. A crisp wheat dosa tastes dry and dusty.

How it is used

With chutney or a potato curry.

Field notes

  • Godhuma dosa is a wheat dosa. It contains gluten.
  • It is an instant batter, not an overnight ferment.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup whole wheat flour
  • 1 cup water
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 small onion, finely chopped
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Whisk everything except the oil. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Cook thin soft dosas on a medium oiled tawa.
  3. Flip once.
  4. Serve with chutney.
  5. They should bend.
  6. Add a spoon of water if the batter thickens as it sits.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of yogurt in the batter is common and adds dairy.
  • Rice dosa is the fermented cousin.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • chutney
  • potato curry
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Nicaragua · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Nicaragua · Mexico & Central America

Güirilas

Soft fresh-corn cakes, traditionally eaten with cuajada cheese.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Güirilas are Nicaraguan fresh-corn cakes, close to chorreadas and cachapas, traditionally paired with cuajada, a fresh cheese. They are breakfast and a side. The corn cake is vegan here. The cheese is the traditional partner and is dairy.

Cultural background

The texture is soft. The corn is sweet. They are cooked on a griddle and eaten the same morning.

Why it tastes this way

Young corn tastes sweet and moist, and a fresh salty cheese is the Nicaraguan contrast, the same logic as the other Central American corn pancakes with different names. Keeping the name güirila matters so Nicaragua is not only a footnote on the cachapa page. The griddle toasts the milk sugars in the corn.

How it is made

Fresh corn is blended and cooked as thick soft cakes.

Authentic touch. Do not add much liquid. The kernels supply it. Cook until the center is set and the face is spotted gold.

How it is used

With cuajada or another fresh cheese, if you eat dairy.

Field notes

  • Güirilas are Nicaragua’s fresh-corn cakes.
  • Cuajada, a fresh cheese, is the traditional partner.

Ingredients

  • 4 ears sweet corn
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Oil
  • Fresh cheese, to serve

Steps

  1. Blend the corn and salt.
  2. Cook thick soft cakes on a medium griddle until spotted and set.
  3. Serve with cuajada or another fresh cheese.
  4. Do not add much water.
  5. The cakes should stay soft.
  6. Eat them warm.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chorreadas are the Costa Rican name in the same family.
  • A savory herb in the batter is a home change.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Side dish
  • cuajada
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · Mild · about 6 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Gujarati Pudla

A soft besan pancake with turmeric, sesame, and green chile.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 3 · about 6 pancakes

History

Pudla is the Gujarati besan pancake, close to cheela and seasoned in a Gujarati way: turmeric, sesame, sometimes a pinch of sugar, and green chile. It is breakfast or a tea snack. Sesame is an allergen in this recipe because it is in the batter.

Cultural background

It is eaten with pickle or chutney. The crumb is a little softer and more yellow than a plain cheela because of turmeric and a spoon of oil in the batter.

Why it tastes this way

Turmeric colors it and tastes earthy. A pinch of sugar is a Gujarati balance against chile, the sweet-hot habit of the region’s cooking. Sesame adds a toasted note. Besan is the protein of a vegetarian pantry. The pancake is fast, which suits a breakfast that is not waiting on a ferment.

How it is made

Besan, turmeric, sesame, chile, and a pinch of sugar are cooked as pancakes.

Authentic touch. Stir sesame in at the end so it stays whole. Cook on medium and oil the pan. Pudla should be supple, not a cracker.

How it is used

With pickle or cilantro chutney.

Field notes

  • Pudla is the Gujarati name to know for this besan pancake.
  • Sesame is in the batter.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup besan
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 1/4 teaspoon turmeric
  • 1 teaspoon sesame seeds
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 1/2 teaspoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon oil, plus more for the pan
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro

Steps

  1. Whisk everything except the pan oil into a batter. Rest 10 minutes.
  2. Cook pancakes on a medium oiled tawa.
  3. Flip when set.
  4. Cook until both sides are golden and the center is done.
  5. Serve with pickle.
  6. The turmeric will stain the pan. That is normal.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Besan cheela is the plainer North Indian cousin.
  • A handful of grated bottle gourd is a common vegetable pudla.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • pickle
  • cilantro chutney
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United States · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Hawaii Poi Pancakes

Mildly tangy taro paste in a soft wheat pancake.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Poi, mashed cooked taro, is a foundational Hawaiian food, thinned with water and often fermented until it tastes sour. Poi pancakes are a later home and restaurant breakfast that folds fresh or day-old poi into a wheat batter. They are not a substitute for the bowl of poi at a Hawaiian table. They are one way island cooks used a staple in a pancake shape that arrived with later cuisines.

Cultural background

Taro was a canoe-crop agriculture of immense skill, grown in flooded loʻi. The pancake is a small, modern use of the paste. If you cannot get poi, this is not the week to invent a taro candy and call it the same thing.

Why it tastes this way

Poi tastes clean when fresh and lightly sour when fermented, because the corm’s starch changes as it sits. That tang is the flavor in the cake, similar in role to buttermilk and entirely different in origin. Wheat flour is the later ingredient. The taro is the older one. A fermented poi makes a tangier cake. A fresh, sweet poi makes a milder, lavender-gray one.

How it is made

Unsweetened poi is whisked into a buttermilk pancake batter and cooked on a moderate griddle.

Authentic touch. Use unsweetened poi, stirred smooth. Sweetened taro bubble-tea powder is a different product and will make a dessert that is not this pancake.

How it is used

Butter and coconut syrup or honey. They are mild, so a tart fruit on the side helps.

Field notes

  • Poi pancakes are a later breakfast. The bowl of poi is the older dish.
  • Use real poi, not a sweetened taro flavoring.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup unsweetened poi, stirred smooth
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 3/4 cup milk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir the dry ingredients together, then stir in the wet ingredients until the batter is combined.
  2. Whisk the poi into the milk before it meets the flour so there are no lumps of paste.
  3. Heat a griddle or heavy skillet over medium heat and film it with butter.
  4. Spoon on the batter. Cook until the underside is browned and the top looks set.
  5. Flip once and cook the second side through.
  6. Cook on medium. The cakes brown a little faster than a plain batter.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • More poi and less flour makes a denser, more taro-forward cake that is harder to flip.
  • If the poi is already very sour, use regular milk instead of buttermilk so the cake is not harsh.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • honey
  • butter
  • pineapple
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United States · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Hoe Cakes

Toasted cornmeal, salt, and the taste of a hot greased griddle.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Hoe cakes are a Southern cornmeal bread cooked on a griddle. The usual explanation of the name is that cooks fried the batter on the blade of a field hoe. Enslaved African American cooks, and later Black Southern cooks, kept corn griddle breads in daily use. West African millet and corn cakes cooked on a griddle are part of the wider history. The hoe story is a traditional account, and the bread itself is well documented.

Cultural background

Hoe cakes were field food and kitchen food: portable, cheap, and made from the corn that dominated Southern rations and farms. They still show up next to greens, beans, and fish.

Why it tastes this way

Corn, not wheat, is the flavor, and that corn is tied to Indigenous agriculture in the Americas and to the rations and fields of slavery. A thin batter and a hot pan toast the meal instead of steaming it, which is why a hoe cake tastes browned and a boiled dumpling from the same meal does not. Little or no sugar keeps it a bread.

How it is made

Cornmeal is stirred with boiling water or cold water, salt, and a little fat, then cooked as thin rounds on a greased griddle.

Authentic touch. Spread the batter thin and cook it slower than a fluffy pancake. The cake is done when the corn smells toasted and the center is set, not when it puffs.

How it is used

Eat them hot with butter, potlikker, greens, or fried fish. They are a bread, not a dessert.

Field notes

  • The hoe-blade story is traditional. Treat it as an explanation people tell, not as a documented patent.
  • Use plain cornmeal. A self-rising mix often contains wheat.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup plain cornmeal, not a wheat mix
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt
  • 1 cup boiling water, plus more if needed
  • 2 tablespoons oil or bacon drippings for the pan

Steps

  1. Stir salt into the cornmeal. Pour on the boiling water and stir until you have a thick, spreadable batter. Rest 5 minutes.
  2. Add a spoon of water if the batter seized up.
  3. Heat a skillet over medium and film it with oil.
  4. Spread small rounds about 1/4 inch thick.
  5. Cook until the underside is browned and the center is set, then flip.
  6. Serve hot as a bread.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Some cooks add an egg and a spoon of flour. That is a softer cake and no longer gluten-free.
  • A pinch of sugar is a modern breakfast tilt. The older cake is savory.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • Side dish
  • greens
  • fried fish
  • butter
  • beans
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Mexico · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Mexico · Mexico & Central America

Hotcakes de Elote

Sweet corn kernels in a soft pancake.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Hotcakes de elote fold fresh or canned sweet corn into the Mexican breakfast pancake. They are a home and fonda variation, using the country’s central grain in a modern batter. They are not corn tortillas and not gorditas. The wheat batter is still there, so they are not gluten-free.

Cultural background

The corn should be sweet. A savory chile pancake would be a different dish. Butter and honey or cajeta finish them.

Why it tastes this way

Sweet corn adds moisture and a summer flavor to a mild batter. Mexico’s long maize agriculture is why corn is the grain people reach for, even inside a wheat café pancake. The combination is modern. The corn is old. Stating both is the accurate way to write the page.

How it is made

Sweet corn is folded into a hotcake batter and cooked on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Pat the kernels dry. Wet corn makes the center soggy. Fold them in. Do not blend them if you want the bites.

How it is used

With butter and honey.

Field notes

  • The corn is the local grain inside a modern wheat pancake.
  • Pat the kernels dry so the center sets.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup milk
  • 1 egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter
  • 1 cup corn kernels, patted dry

Steps

  1. Mix the batter and fold in the corn.
  2. Cook on a buttered medium griddle.
  3. Flip when the edges set.
  4. Serve with honey.
  5. Pat the corn dry first.
  6. The centers should be cooked.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Blended corn makes a more custardy batter and a different texture.
  • Plain hotcakes are the page without corn.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • honey
  • butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Iceland · No chili heat · about 10 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Iceland · Northern Europe

Icelandic Lummur

Small, thick oatmeal pancakes, lightly sweet.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 10 cakes

History

Lummur are Icelandic oatmeal pancakes, thicker than pönnukökur, made with oats and often served with butter, sugar, or jam. They are a homey cake, sometimes associated with Thorri and midwinter festivities, and also with ordinary cooking. Oats make them heartier than the thin pancake.

Cultural background

They are cooked as small rounds. A sour note from buttermilk or skyr in some recipes is pleasant. This version uses milk and a spoon of skyr-style yogurt if you have it, with milk as the measured liquid so the recipe is clear.

Why it tastes this way

Oats in a small thick cake taste toasted and filling, which suits a midwinter table more than a lacy dessert crêpe. Iceland imported grain for a long time, and oats were a practical one. Sugar on top, rather than a very sweet batter, keeps them a breakfast cake. The thickness is the difference from pönnukökur.

How it is made

Oats and flour are mixed with egg and milk, rested, and cooked as small thick pancakes.

Authentic touch. Let the oat batter rest 10 minutes so the flakes swell. Cook the cakes small. A large lumma stays raw inside.

How it is used

Butter and sugar or jam.

Field notes

  • Lummur are the oatmeal pancakes of Iceland.
  • A rest lets the oats hydrate.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup rolled oats, ground or fine
  • 1/2 cup flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 cup milk
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir the batter and rest 10 minutes.
  2. Cook small cakes on a buttered medium griddle.
  3. Flip when the tops set.
  4. Cook until the center is done.
  5. Serve with butter and sugar.
  6. They are thicker than pönnukökur on purpose.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Pönnukökur are the thin rolled pancakes.
  • A spoon of skyr in the batter adds tang and is still dairy.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • butter
  • sugar
  • jam
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Ireland · No chili heat · about 12 scones

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ireland · Western Europe

Irish Buttermilk Drop Scones

Small, thick buttermilk cakes, lightly sweet.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 12 scones

History

Drop scones are thick little pancakes, dropped by the spoon onto a griddle. In Ireland they are a teatime and breakfast cake, distinct from the thin Shrove pancake. The method is the same family as Scotch pancakes. Buttermilk is the Irish liquid, because soda bread culture already kept sour milk in the kitchen.

Cultural background

They are eaten warm with butter and jam. They are not cut into farls and they are not tossed like crepes.

Why it tastes this way

A thicker batter and baking powder make a soft, cakey bite instead of a lacy crepe. Buttermilk adds tang that jam then sweetens. The contrast with Shrove pancakes is the point: one is a Lenten-eve crepe, the other is a soda-bread cousin cooked in rounds.

How it is made

A thick buttermilk batter is dropped by the spoon onto a warm griddle.

Authentic touch. Drop them thick and do not spread. They are ready to flip when the bubbles stay open.

How it is used

Butter and jam, still warm.

Field notes

  • Drop scones and Shrove pancakes are both Irish and are not the same thickness.
  • They belong to the soda and buttermilk kitchen.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/4 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 2/3 cup buttermilk
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir the dry ingredients together, then stir in the wet ingredients until the batter is combined.
  2. The batter should be thick. Rest it 5 minutes.
  3. Heat a griddle or heavy skillet over medium heat and film it with butter.
  4. Spoon on the batter. Cook until the underside is browned and the top looks set.
  5. Flip once and cook the second side through.
  6. Drop by the tablespoon and flip when the bubbles hold.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Raisins in the batter are a common home addition.
  • Scotch pancakes are the Scottish twin.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • butter
  • jam
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Ireland · No chili heat · 4 farls

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ireland · Western Europe

Irish Potato Farls

Soft potato flatbread, spotted brown, with a floury face.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 farls

History

Potato farls are a northern Irish griddle bread made from mashed potato and flour, cut into quarters. “Farl” means a quarter. They are an Ulster breakfast bread, cousins of boxty but smoother, because the potato is cooked first. Scottish tattie scones are a close relative. The bread belongs to the potato diet of Ireland and to the griddle that also cooked soda farls.

Cultural background

An Ulster fry often includes a farl alongside soda bread. The farl itself can be vegetarian. The fry around it usually is not.

Why it tastes this way

Mashed potato and flour, cooked dry on a griddle, taste mild and slightly sweet, with a fried spot where the surface dries. There is no egg and often no milk, so the flavor is potato rather than batter. The north’s breakfast griddle, used for several breads in one morning, is why farls are flat and quick instead of oven-risen.

How it is made

Hot mashed potato is mixed with flour and salt, rolled into a round, quartered, and cooked on a dry or lightly floured griddle.

Authentic touch. The dough should be soft and rolled thicker than a cracker, about a half inch. A dry dough cracks. Cook it on a moderate griddle so the center heats before the flour scorches.

How it is used

Butter, or as part of a breakfast fry. They are a bread.

Field notes

  • Farl means a quarter of the round.
  • Potato farls and boxty both use potato. Farls start with mash only.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups warm mashed potato, plain
  • 1/2 cup all-purpose flour, plus more for rolling
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt

Steps

  1. Mix the warm mash, flour, and salt into a soft dough.
  2. Roll on a floured board into a round about 1/2 inch thick. Cut into quarters.
  3. Heat a dry griddle over medium.
  4. Cook the farls until spotted brown, a few minutes a side.
  5. They should be hot in the center.
  6. Serve with butter.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A knob of butter in the mash is richer and adds dairy.
  • Tattie scones in Scotland are the close cousin.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • butter
  • eggs
  • bacon
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Ireland · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ireland · Western Europe

Irish Shrove Tuesday Pancakes

Thin, lemony pancakes fried in butter for Shrove Tuesday.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

In Ireland, Shrove Tuesday is Pancake Tuesday, the day before Lent. Households used up eggs, milk, and butter in thin pancakes before the fast. The custom is the same Christian calendar as England’s Shrove pancakes, and the Irish cake is typically thin, closer to a crepe than to a thick American stack. Lemon and sugar are the usual finish.

Cultural background

Families still cook a stack and argue over who flips. It is a one-day ritual that many people also cook on ordinary nights because the batter is simple.

Why it tastes this way

The taste is egg, butter, and lemon because those were the rich foods to finish before Lent, not because the pancake needed a special spice. A thin batter cooks fast and takes a sharp squeeze of lemon, which cuts the butter. The religious calendar, more than the climate, is why the cake has a date attached.

How it is made

A thin batter of flour, egg, and milk is cooked in a small skillet and finished with lemon and sugar.

Authentic touch. The pan should be hot and only filmed with butter. A thick pool of batter becomes a different, heavier cake and will not toss.

How it is used

Lemon juice and sugar. A few cooks use jam or a chocolate spread. Lemon and sugar are the Shrove plate.

Field notes

  • Pancake Tuesday in Ireland is Shrove Tuesday, the day before Ash Wednesday.
  • The cake is thin. A fluffy stack is a different breakfast.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 1/4 cups milk
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter, plus more for the pan
  • Pinch of salt
  • Lemon and sugar, to serve

Steps

  1. Whisk the batter until smooth and rest it 15 minutes.
  2. Heat a small skillet and film it with butter.
  3. Pour a thin layer and swirl.
  4. Cook until the edge lifts, then flip for half a minute.
  5. Stack with lemon and sugar.
  6. The first pancake is the cook’s treat. It tells you the heat.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A thicker buttermilk drop scone is a different Irish cake, on its own page.
  • Orange instead of lemon is a fair home swap and tastes less sharp.

Pairings and uses

  • Holiday table
  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • lemon
  • sugar
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

Ireland · No chili heat · 4 farls

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ireland · Western Europe

Irish Soda Farls

Soft soda bread, griddled, with a tender crumb and a floury crust.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 farls

History

Soda farls are the griddled form of Irish soda bread, especially in Ulster. Baking soda and buttermilk became a fast way to leaven bread in the nineteenth century, when baking soda was a shop good and many houses cooked on a griddle rather than in an oven. The round is cut into quarters, farls, and cooked on the griddle.

Cultural background

They sit beside potato farls at breakfast. The dough is a soft soda-bread dough, not a pancake batter.

Why it tastes this way

Buttermilk and soda react at once, so the bread rises without yeast. That chemistry is why soda bread spread in Ireland: it was fast, and it used the sour milk farms already had. A griddle browns the outside while the inside stays steaming and mild. There is little sugar. It tastes of wheat and tang.

How it is made

Flour, baking soda, salt, and buttermilk make a soft dough that is patted into a round, quartered, and cooked on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Handle the dough lightly and cook it a bit slower than you think. A fierce heat burns the flour and leaves a raw stripe in the middle.

How it is used

Butter, jam, or with a breakfast fry. They are best the morning they are cooked.

Field notes

  • Soda farls are bread cooked on a griddle, included here as the pancake’s thick northern neighbor.
  • Buttermilk is the acid that makes the soda work. Sweet milk will not do the same job.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk

Steps

  1. Heat a griddle over medium-low.
  2. Stir flour, soda, and salt. Add buttermilk and mix to a soft dough.
  3. Pat into a round about 3/4 inch thick and cut into quarters.
  4. Cook on the griddle, turning once, until both sides are browned and the center is set, about 15 to 20 minutes.
  5. A low lid or a second pan helps the center cook.
  6. Cool slightly, then split and butter.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of sugar is a sweeter home version.
  • Oven soda bread is the same dough baked in a round loaf.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • butter
  • jam
  • eggs
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Japan · No chili heat · about 6 thick pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Japan · East Asia

Japanese Hotcakes

Thick, fluffy buttermilk-style pancakes, a kissaten breakfast.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 3 · about 6 thick pancakes

History

Hotcakes, hotto keki, are Japan’s thick diner pancakes, served in kissaten coffee shops with butter and syrup. They are an American-style pancake adapted in twentieth-century cafés, distinct from the later soufflé trend and from okonomiyaki. A little baking powder and a thick batter make the height.

Cultural background

They are a sweet breakfast or a café snack. Syrup and butter are the usual. Whipped cream is a dessert turn.

Why it tastes this way

A thick batter and a covered pan steam the center so the cake rises tall and tastes mild and milky. The style came with Western-style cafés and stayed because it photographs and eats like a treat. It is not a peasant grain cake. It is a café food, and the mild sweetness is the point.

How it is made

A thick milk batter is cooked covered in deep rounds.

Authentic touch. Cook covered on low heat. Flip once. A high griddle burns the outside of a thick cake and leaves a raw ring.

How it is used

With butter and syrup.

Field notes

  • Japanese hotcakes are the thick café pancakes, older in Japan than the soufflé fad.
  • Low heat and a cover are how the center cooks.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 egg
  • 3/4 cup milk
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir into a thick batter. Do not overmix.
  2. Cook on a buttered medium-low griddle, covered, in deep rounds.
  3. Flip when the tops are full of bubbles and the edges look set.
  4. Cook covered until the center springs back.
  5. Serve with butter and syrup.
  6. They should be tall, not crisp crackers.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Soufflé pancakes are the whipped-white café cousin.
  • A banana on top is a kissaten habit.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • butter
  • syrup
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Japan · No chili heat · 4 tall pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Japan · East Asia

Japanese Soufflé Pancakes

Very tall, jiggly pancakes of whipped egg white, a modern café style.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 2 · 4 tall pancakes

History

Soufflé pancakes became a Japanese café fashion in the 2010s: egg whites whipped and folded into a small amount of batter, cooked slowly in rings so the cakes stand high and shake. They are a modern style, not a Meiji-era household recipe, and they should be dated that way. They are a dessert breakfast.

Cultural background

They need time and a low heat. A cover traps steam. They deflate as they cool, which is expected. Powdered sugar and butter are the finish.

Why it tastes this way

Whipped whites are almost the entire structure, so the cake tastes of egg and steam rather than of a dense crumb. The café style chased height. That is a recent commercial aesthetic, and the science is still a soufflé cooked in a pan. Low heat keeps the outside from browning before the inside sets.

How it is made

Whipped egg whites are folded into a yolk batter and cooked covered, slowly, in tall rounds.

Authentic touch. Do not open the lid for the first several minutes. Rings or a deep spoon shape help them climb. If the heat is high they brown and then collapse into a sad omelette.

How it is used

Immediately, with butter and powdered sugar or fruit.

Field notes

  • This is a 2010s café style, not an old household pancake.
  • They are supposed to jiggle and then slowly deflate.

Ingredients

  • 2 eggs, separated
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 2 tablespoons milk
  • 1/4 cup flour
  • 1/4 teaspoon baking powder
  • Pinch of salt
  • Oil
  • Powdered sugar

Steps

  1. Whisk yolks, sugar, milk, flour, baking powder, and salt.
  2. Whip the whites to firm peaks and fold them in.
  3. Oil two rings on a medium-low griddle. Fill them with batter.
  4. Cover and cook about 6 minutes, until the tops look set.
  5. Flip carefully and cook covered until the centers are just done.
  6. Dust with powdered sugar and serve immediately.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A little cocoa makes a chocolate soufflé pancake and is still modern.
  • Ordinary hotcakes are the diner style on the next page.

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • powdered sugar
  • butter
  • berries
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

China · Mild · 2 large wraps

Flying Pancakes of the World

China · East Asia

Jianbing

A crisp savory crepe with egg, scallion, sauce, and a cracker.

15 min prep · 10 min cook · Serves 2 · 2 large wraps

History

Jianbing is a northern Chinese street breakfast. A thin batter, often mung-bean or wheat, is cooked on a round griddle, brushed with egg, and topped with scallion, cilantro, fermented bean sauce or hoisin, chile, and often a crispy cracker, the guozi, or a fried dough stick. Tianjin and Shandong both have strong claims. This is a home wheat-and-egg version with bean sauce and a store-bought cracker.

Cultural background

It is wrapped and eaten on the way to work. The sauce is savory and slightly sweet. The herbs are raw. The crepe is crisp at the edge.

Why it tastes this way

Mung-bean batter, where it is used, tastes cleaner and crisper than wheat. Wheat is the easier home flour and is what this recipe uses, so the page does not pretend otherwise. Egg fries onto the crepe. Fermented bean sauce is the deep savory note, a northern pantry flavor. Cilantro and scallion are fresh against that. The cracker is there for noise and texture. Street griddles run very hot, which is why the edge shatters.

How it is made

A thin batter is cooked, topped with egg, sauce, herbs, and a cracker, then folded.

Authentic touch. Spread the batter paper-thin on a hot pan and crack the egg on immediately so it bonds. Wrap it while it is still flexible enough to fold and crisp enough to crackle.

How it is used

Wrapped, hot, as breakfast.

Field notes

  • Jianbing is a northern street breakfast. Tianjin and Shandong are the places most often named.
  • The cracker is traditional texture. The crepe and egg are the meal if you cannot find one.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup flour
  • 1/2 cup water
  • 2 eggs
  • 2 tablespoons fermented bean sauce or hoisin
  • 2 scallions, sliced
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro
  • Chile sauce, to taste
  • 2 crackers or a piece of fried dough
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Whisk flour and water into a thin batter.
  2. Oil a hot skillet and pour a paper-thin crepe.
  3. Beat an egg and spread it on the crepe. Cook until just set.
  4. Spread sauce, scallion, cilantro, and a little chile. Press on a cracker.
  5. Fold and serve.
  6. Repeat.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A mung-bean flour batter is closer to some Tianjin stalls and can be gluten-free if the sauce is too.
  • Hoisin instead of plain bean sauce is sweeter.

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • fermented bean sauce
  • cilantro
  • scallion
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

Austria · No chili heat · one pan, about 4 servings

Flying Pancakes of the World

Austria · Western Europe

Kaiserschmarrn

A torn, soufflé-like pancake with raisins, dusted with sugar.

20 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · one pan, about 4 servings

History

Kaiserschmarrn is an Austrian torn pancake, associated with the Habsburg table and now a standard Alpine dessert and lunch. The name refers to the emperor. Stories about which emperor and why the pancake was torn are anecdotes, and they are not repeated here as fact. The method is solid: a thick, egg-rich pancake, torn in the pan, sugared, often with raisins, served with plum compote.

Cultural background

It is eaten as a meal in mountain huts as well as a dessert. Plum compote, Zwetschkenröster, is the classic side.

Why it tastes this way

Separated eggs make it puff, and tearing it creates more browned surface for powdered sugar. Raisins plump in the batter. Plum compote is tart, which a sweet torn pancake needs. The Alpine hut version is hearty because people eat it after being outside in the cold, not because the batter is different.

How it is made

A soufflé batter with raisins is cooked in a wide pan, torn, and sugared.

Authentic touch. Whip the whites and fold them in. When the underside is brown, tear the pancake with two forks and turn the pieces. Do not try to flip it whole.

How it is used

With powdered sugar and plum compote or another tart fruit.

Field notes

  • The imperial anecdotes are left out because they conflict. The dish is the torn pancake.
  • Plum compote is the usual fruit.

Ingredients

  • 4 large eggs, separated
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1 cup milk
  • 1 cup flour
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1/3 cup raisins
  • 2 tablespoons butter
  • Powdered sugar, to serve

Steps

  1. Beat yolks with sugar, milk, flour, and salt. Fold in raisins and the whipped whites.
  2. Melt butter in a wide skillet and pour in the batter.
  3. Cook covered on medium until the bottom is brown and the top is set.
  4. Tear into pieces with forks and turn them to brown.
  5. Dust with powdered sugar.
  6. Serve with plum compote.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Without raisins it is still Kaiserschmarrn.
  • Palatschinken are the thin Austrian crepes, a different dessert.

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • powdered sugar
  • plum compote
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

Turkey · No chili heat · one cake

Flying Pancakes of the World

Turkey · Middle East

Kaygana

A Black Sea herb pancake, more egg than flour, soft and green.

10 min prep · 12 min cook · Serves 2 · one cake

History

Kaygana is a herb pancake or thick omelette of Turkey’s Black Sea coast, made with eggs and a handful of flour or cornmeal, and a large quantity of scallions, leeks, or herbs. It is a regional breakfast. This version uses a spoon of cornmeal so it can stay gluten-free, and it is still a pancake rather than a plain omelette.

Cultural background

It is cut into wedges. The herbs are the body. Cheese on top is common and adds dairy. The written cake has none.

Why it tastes this way

The Black Sea kitchen puts greens into eggs because the coast is wet and green. A little cornmeal sets the eggs into a cake you can cut. The flavor is scallion and egg, not a sweet batter. It is a regional dish, not a national crepe, which is why it has its own page.

How it is made

Eggs, cornmeal, and a mass of scallions are cooked as one thick pancake.

Authentic touch. Cook it covered on medium-low so the egg sets without burning. The herbs should wilt. Do not stir it into scramble.

How it is used

In wedges, warm.

Field notes

  • Kaygana is a Black Sea herb and egg pancake.
  • This version uses cornmeal so it contains no wheat.

Ingredients

  • 4 eggs
  • 2 tablespoons cornmeal
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 6 scallions, chopped
  • 1 tablespoon oil

Steps

  1. Beat the eggs with cornmeal and salt. Stir in the scallions.
  2. Heat oil in a skillet and pour in the batter.
  3. Cook covered over medium-low until set.
  4. Brown the top briefly if you like.
  5. Cut into wedges.
  6. Serve warm.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A handful of grated cheese on top adds dairy.
  • Wheat flour instead of cornmeal is common and is not gluten-free.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • bread
