India · No chili heat · about 8 appams

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Kerala Appam

A bowl-shaped rice pancake, lacy at the rim and soft in the center.

40 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 appams

History

Appam, or palappam, is a Kerala rice pancake cooked in a small rounded pan, an appachatti. The edges are thin and lacy. The center is thick and soft. Fermented rice batter, often with coconut, is traditional. Yeast or toddy can start the ferment. This home version uses a pinch of yeast and coconut milk, and it notes that toddy is the older starter where it is used.

Cultural background

Appam is eaten with stew, egg roast, or sweetened coconut milk. The shape is the dish. A flat dosa pan will not make a bowl.

Why it tastes this way

A rounded pan sends the thin batter up the sides, where it crisps, and leaves a custardy center of rice and coconut. Coconut is Kerala’s fat and sweetness. Fermentation, whether from toddy or yeast, adds a light sourness and the lift. The result tastes of rice and coconut, not of wheat, which is why it matches a coconut-milk stew.

How it is made

Fermented rice and coconut batter is swirled in a rounded pan and cooked covered.

Authentic touch. Swirl the batter in a hot, oiled appam pan and let the excess sit in the middle. Cover it. Do not flip. A wok is a poor substitute that can work if you treat it gently.

How it is used

With vegetable stew or sweet coconut milk. An egg cooked in the center is a common variation and adds egg.

Field notes

  • The bowl shape comes from the pan and the swirl.
  • Toddy is traditional. Yeast is the home starter written here.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup rice, soaked 4 hours
  • 1/2 cup cooked rice
  • 1/2 cup coconut milk
  • 1/4 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1/2 teaspoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Grind soaked rice and cooked rice with a little water until smooth.
  2. Stir in coconut milk, yeast, sugar, and salt. Ferment 6 to 12 hours until bubbly.
  3. Heat an oiled appam pan.
  4. Pour a ladle, swirl to coat the sides, and let the rest pool in the center.
  5. Cover and cook until the center is set and the rim is lacy. Do not flip.
  6. Serve with stew.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Sri Lankan hoppers are the island cousin, often with an egg.
  • Kallappam is a toddy-fermented cousin.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • vegetable stew
  • coconut milk
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Ethiopia · No chili heat · about 6 rounds

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ethiopia · East Africa

Kita

A quick, unfermented teff flatbread, milder than injera.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 6 rounds

History

Kita is a quick Ethiopian flatbread, often teff or other flour mixed and cooked without the long ferment of injera. It is everyday bread, softer and less sour. Chechebsa, or kita firfir, is torn kita cooked with spices and is a breakfast. This page is the plain kita. It is not a failed injera.

Cultural background

When there is no time to ferment, kita is the bread. It can be torn and dressed with spiced butter for breakfast. This version is teff and salt, vegan as written.

Why it tastes this way

Without fermentation, teff tastes toasted and grainy rather than sour. That is the point of kita: the same grain, a different schedule. Injera’s sourness takes days. Kita takes minutes. The flavor is the grain itself, which is why a spiced butter breakfast exists, to dress a plain bread.

How it is made

Teff flour and water are mixed into a batter and cooked on both sides.

Authentic touch. Cook it on a medium pan like a thick crepe, and flip it, unlike injera. It will not grow eyes. Do not wait for them.

How it is used

Plain, or torn and finished with a spiced butter if you eat dairy. The bread itself is vegan.

Field notes

  • Kita is the quick bread. It is not supposed to taste like injera.
  • Teff keeps it gluten-free.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup teff flour
  • 1 cup water
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 teaspoon oil

Steps

  1. Stir flour, water, and salt into a thick batter.
  2. Oil a skillet lightly over medium.
  3. Spread a round and cook until the underside browns.
  4. Flip and cook through.
  5. Stack under a cloth.
  6. Serve warm.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chechebsa is torn kita warmed with spices.
  • Injera is the fermented bread.

Pairings and uses

  • Bread course
  • Breakfast
  • spiced butter
  • honey
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Lancashire Potato Cakes

Buttery mashed potato and flour, griddled in spots.

15 min prep · 12 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 cakes

History

Lancashire potato cakes are mashed potato, flour, and butter, rolled and cooked on a griddle. They are a northwest English breakfast bread, close to Irish potato farls and Scottish tattie scones. Butter in the dough is typical, which makes them richer than a lean farl.

Cultural background

They are eaten hot with more butter, or with bacon and eggs. Leftover mash is the usual reason to make them.

Why it tastes this way

Butter in the dough browns and tastes sweet against the potato. Flour makes a rollable bread rather than a fried shred cake. The northwest of England, like Ireland, put potatoes on the breakfast griddle because the tuber was the cheap staple and the griddle was already hot.

How it is made

Mashed potato, butter, and flour are rolled, cut, and cooked on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Use dry mash. Buttery, wet mash needs more flour and then tastes doughy. Cool it slightly so the butter does not melt into a slick before you roll.

How it is used

More butter, while they are hot.

Field notes

  • Leftover mash is the traditional start. Freshly boiled potatoes should be dried in the pan first.
  • They are a bread, not a syrup pancake.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups plain mashed potato
  • 2 tablespoons butter
  • 3/4 cup all-purpose flour, plus more to roll
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt

Steps

  1. Work the butter and salt into the mash, then add flour to a soft dough.
  2. Roll about 1/4 inch thick and cut rounds or squares.
  3. Cook on a medium dry griddle until spotted brown on both sides.
  4. The center should be hot.
  5. Serve with butter.
  6. They are best the same day.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A pinch of thyme is a savory home addition.
  • Irish potato farls are the close relative, often with less butter in the dough.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • butter
  • bacon
  • eggs
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

China · No chili heat · 2 breads

Flying Pancakes of the World

China · East Asia

Laobing

A thick, layered northern griddle bread, chewy and flaky.

30 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 2 breads

History

Laobing is a northern Chinese layered griddle bread, thicker than a scallion pancake, coiled with oil and cooked until the outside is spotted and the inside is chewy. Beijing breakfasts include it. It can wrap eggs or be eaten plain. It is a bread, and it is in this journal as the thick griddled bing beside the thin duck pancake.

Cultural background

The coil is simple. The thickness is greater than cong you bing. It is torn, not stacked with syrup.

Why it tastes this way

Oil and a coil make layers, and a thicker round stays chewy rather than shattering. Northern wheat eating is why the breakfast is a bing and not a rice cake. A moderate griddle cooks the center. Salt is the seasoning. Scallion is optional. The plain bread tastes of fried wheat.

How it is made

A dough is coiled with oil, flattened thick, and cooked on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Roll it thicker than a scallion pancake, about a third of an inch after the coil, and cook it covered on medium-low so the center is not raw dough.

How it is used

Torn, warm, plain or with a fried egg.

Field notes

  • Laobing is a thick layered griddle bread of the north.
  • Cook it slowly enough that the center finishes.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups flour
  • 3/4 cup warm water
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons oil

Steps

  1. Make a dough and rest it 15 minutes.
  2. Roll it out, oil and salt it, coil it, and flatten to about 1/3 inch.
  3. Cook covered on a medium-low oiled griddle, turning once, until both sides are spotted and the center is cooked.
  4. Cool 2 minutes and tear.
  5. Serve warm.
  6. A fried egg on the side makes a breakfast.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Scallions in the coil move it toward cong you bing.
  • Duck pancakes are the thin wrappers.

Pairings and uses

  • Bread course
  • Breakfast
  • fried egg
  • pickles
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Norway · No chili heat · about 12 small cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Norway · Northern Europe

Lapper

Small sour-milk pancakes, simpler than svele.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 12 small cakes

History

Lapper are Norwegian pancakes, often smaller and a bit tangier than svele, made with sour milk or buttermilk. The name covers a homey pancake rather than the ferry cake. They are breakfast and coffee food, served with butter and sugar or jam.

Cultural background

A sour milk batter was a way to use milk that had turned, which is how many northern pancakes began. Buttermilk is the reliable modern stand-in.

Why it tastes this way

Acid in the milk reacts with a pinch of soda and tastes clean and slightly tart, so the cakes want jam rather than a salty cheese. They are thinner than svele and closer to a small buttermilk pancake. The flavor is the sour milk, not a spice blend.

How it is made

Buttermilk batter is cooked as small cakes.

Authentic touch. Do not sweeten them like cake. Sugar on top is the sweet. The batter should stay mildly tangy.

How it is used

Butter and jam or sugar.

Field notes

  • Lapper and svele are both Norwegian and are not identical.
  • Buttermilk stands in for the older sour milk.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/4 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir into a smooth batter and rest 5 minutes.
  2. Cook small rounds on a buttered griddle.
  3. Flip when the bubbles hold.
  4. Serve with butter and jam.
  5. Keep the heat at medium.
  6. They are best warm.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Svele are thicker and often cardamom-scented.
  • Sour cream thinned with milk can stand in for buttermilk.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • jam
  • butter
  • sugar
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Somalia · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Somalia · East Africa

Laxoox

A spongy, lightly sour flatbread, softer and often smaller than injera.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Laxoox, also called canjeero, is the fermented Somali pancake, a relative of injera and of Yemeni lahoh. It is breakfast food, eaten with honey, ghee, or a stew. Sorghum, teff, or a mix of flours appear in different houses. This version uses teff or sorghum flour, both gluten-free, and says to pick one and name it.

Cultural background

It is cooked in a pan on one side until holes form. Diaspora kitchens keep the ferment going with a spoon of yesterday’s batter. That spoon is a starter, not a packaged yeast.

Why it tastes this way

Fermentation sours the grain and lifts the holes, the same science as injera, with a Somali breakfast habit of honey or ghee on a smaller cake. Sorghum is a dryland grain, which fits the climate better than wheat. The sourness should be gentle. A harsh ferment means it went too long.

How it is made

A fermented sorghum or teff batter is cooked as small spongy pancakes on one side.

Authentic touch. Keep a spoon of batter in the refrigerator to start the next batch. Cook on medium, covered, without flipping, until the top is dry.

How it is used

With honey or ghee, or with a stew. Honey is not vegan. The bread is.

Field notes

  • Laxoox and canjeero are names for the Somali fermented pancake.
  • A saved spoon of batter starts the next batch.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup teff flour or sorghum flour
  • 1 1/2 cups water
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon of a previous batter, if you have it

Steps

  1. Mix flour, water, and starter if you have it. Ferment 1 to 2 days until bubbly.
  2. Stir in the salt.
  3. Cook small rounds in a covered nonstick pan until holes form and the top sets. Do not flip.
  4. Serve warm.
  5. Save a spoon of batter, refrigerated, for next time.
  6. Discard moldy batter.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Yemeni lahoh is the related pancake across the water.
  • Wheat flour versions exist and are not gluten-free.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • honey
  • ghee
  • stew
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Norway · No chili heat · 8 rounds

Flying Pancakes of the World

Norway · Northern Europe

Lefse

A soft potato flatbread, lightly browned in spots.

40 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 8 · 8 rounds

History

Lefse is a Norwegian potato flatbread cooked on a griddle. It traveled with Norwegian immigrants to the Upper Midwest of the United States, where church basements still roll it for holidays. There are several lefse types in Norway. This is the potato lefse most Norwegian-Americans mean: mashed potato, flour, a little fat, rolled thin.

Cultural background

It is served with butter and sugar, or butter and cinnamon sugar, folded or rolled. It is a bread and a holiday food, not a syrup stack. The Midwest page of the journal is this same bread, cooked far from Norway.

Why it tastes this way

Potato keeps the bread moist and mild, and a dry griddle spots it brown without frying it hard. Butter and sugar on a warm lefse melt into the cloth-soft crumb. Immigrants kept the bread because it marked Christmas and church, and because potatoes and flour were available on the prairie. The taste is potato, wheat, and browned spots, then whatever you spread.

How it is made

Cold mashed potato is mixed with butter, cream, and flour, rolled into rounds, and cooked on a dry griddle.

Authentic touch. Chill the mashed potato before you add flour, and roll it thin enough to see the board almost through it. Thick lefse tastes like dough. A sock-covered rolling pin is the traditional anti-stick tool. Parchment and cold dough also work.

How it is used

Butter and sugar, folded, while still flexible.

Field notes

  • Upper Midwest church lefse is this Norwegian bread in a new country.
  • Roll it thin. Thickness is the usual failure.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups riced cold mashed potato
  • 2 tablespoons butter
  • 2 tablespoons cream
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour, plus more for rolling

Steps

  1. Mix potato, butter, cream, and salt. Work in enough flour to make a soft dough.
  2. Divide into 8 pieces and roll very thin on a floured cloth or board.
  3. Cook on a dry medium-hot griddle until spotted, about a minute a side.
  4. Stack under a towel so they stay flexible.
  5. Spread with butter and sugar and fold.
  6. Unused rounds can be frozen.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Lompe is a smaller potato lefse often wrapped around a hot dog.
  • Hardanger lefse and other regional Norwegian lefses differ. This is the soft potato one.

Pairings and uses

  • Bread course
  • Holiday table
  • Breakfast
  • butter
  • sugar
  • cinnamon
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Lithuania · No chili heat · about 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Lithuania · Eastern Europe

Lithuanian Blynai

Yeasted pancakes, soft and slightly sour, served with jam or sour cream.

1 hr 10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 12 pancakes

History

Blynai are Lithuanian pancakes, often yeasted, served sweet or with savory toppings. They belong to the same Baltic and Slavic pancake world as blini. Potato blynai are a separate cake. These are the wheat yeast pancakes.

Cultural background

They are a home dessert with jam, or a savory bite with herring. The batter rises. That is the difference from a quick crepe.

Why it tastes this way

Yeast and milk make a soft, faintly tangy cake that holds butter. Jam is the sweet path. The method is the eastern European yeasted pancake, adapted in a Baltic kitchen. A short rise is enough. A long sourdough is a different project.

How it is made

A yeasted milk batter is risen and cooked as small pancakes.

Authentic touch. Wait until the batter is bubbly. Cooking it flat and unleavened makes a crepe and wastes the yeast.

How it is used

Jam, sour cream, or butter.

Field notes

  • Wheat blynai and potato blynai are both Lithuanian and are different batters.
  • The yeast rise is the texture.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup warm milk
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 cup flour
  • 1 egg
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter

Steps

  1. Mix all ingredients and rest 45 minutes.
  2. Cook small pancakes on a buttered griddle.
  3. Flip when the tops are set.
  4. Serve with jam or sour cream.
  5. They should look slightly puffed.
  6. Butter the pan lightly between batches.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Bulviniai blynai are the potato ones.
  • Russian blini are the close technique.

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • jam
  • sour cream
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Maine Blueberry Pancakes

Tart wild blueberry bursting through a simple batter.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Lowbush wild blueberries are a Maine crop, managed in barrens and gathered by Wabanaki peoples long before they were a commercial can and a tourist breakfast. Folding the berries into wheat pancakes is a modern New England breakfast that depends on that fruit. The cake is not a Wabanaki recipe. The berry is the local fact.

Cultural background

In August the berries are fresh. The rest of the year, Mainers use frozen wild berries, which are smaller and tarter than cultivated highbush blueberries.

Why it tastes this way

Wild lowbush berries are smaller and more acidic than a large cultivated blueberry, so the cake tastes tart rather than jammy. Their skins stain the batter purple if you stir hard. Acid in the fruit also softens the crumb around each berry. The barrens and the short northern summer are why this fruit, and not peach, defines the plate.

How it is made

A buttermilk batter is cooked with wild blueberries folded in or dotted on top.

Authentic touch. Fold the berries in at the end, or drop them onto the poured batter. Stirring breaks them and turns the whole batch gray.

How it is used

Butter and maple syrup. Extra berries on the side if you have them.

Field notes

  • Wild Maine blueberries and large grocery blueberries are different plants in practice: smaller, tarter, and often sold frozen.
  • The pancake is a modern use of a much older Indigenous food.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup wild blueberries, fresh or frozen
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir the dry ingredients together, then stir in the wet ingredients until the batter is combined.
  2. Fold the berries in gently after the batter is mixed.
  3. Heat a griddle or heavy skillet over medium heat and film it with butter.
  4. Spoon on the batter. Cook until the underside is browned and the top looks set.
  5. Flip once and cook the second side through.
  6. Flip when the berries have set into the surface so they do not stick to the griddle.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Frozen berries should be used frozen or barely thawed, or they bleed.
  • Cultivated blueberries are milder. They still make a pancake. They are not the Maine berry.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • maple syrup
  • wild blueberries
  • butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Yemen · No chili heat · 6 breads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Yemen · Middle East

Malawach

A flaky pan-fried layered bread, crisp outside and soft within.

40 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 breads

History

Malawach is a Yemeni layered pan bread, brought by Yemeni Jews to Israel, where it became a popular breakfast. The dough is spread with fat, folded, and fried. Tomato grating and a hard-boiled egg are a common Israeli-Yemeni plate. The bread itself is the layers. Jachnun is the overnight baked cousin and is not this recipe.

Cultural background

It is cooked from disks of laminated dough that have rested in the refrigerator. The fry is quick. The lamination is the work.

Why it tastes this way

Fat between thin layers of dough fries into flakes, the same idea as msemen and paratha, in a Yemeni Jewish kitchen. Butter or margarine is the usual fat. A hot pan makes the outside crisp while the inside stays stretchy. Tomato and egg on the side are sharp and savory against the rich bread. The journey from Yemen to Israeli breakfast tables is part of the food’s modern life.

How it is made

A soft dough is laminated with butter, chilled in disks, and pan-fried.

Authentic touch. Chill the folded disks before you fry them, so the layers do not melt together. Fry in a dry or barely oiled pan from cold fat already in the dough. Press gently so they cook through.

How it is used

Hot, with grated tomato, zhug if you have it, or a boiled egg.

Field notes

  • Malawach is a Yemeni layered bread and an Israeli breakfast associated with Yemeni Jews.
  • Chilling the disks keeps the layers distinct.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups flour
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 4 tablespoons soft butter

Steps

  1. Mix flour, salt, and water into a soft dough. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Roll it out, spread with butter, fold into a packet, and divide into 6 disks. Chill 30 minutes.
  3. Fry each disk in a covered skillet over medium until both sides are golden and the center is cooked.
  4. Serve hot.
  5. Press lightly so the inside heats.
  6. The layers should separate when torn.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Jachnun is baked overnight and is a different bread.
  • Oil instead of butter makes it dairy-free and less traditional for many kitchens.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • grated tomato
  • boiled egg
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Somalia · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Somalia · East Africa

Malawah

A soft, sweet pan-fried bread, often with egg, cardamom, and a little sugar.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Malawah, or malawax, is a sweet Somali pancake, different from the sour laxoox. It is a softer, richer breakfast or snack, often with egg, sugar, and cardamom, pan-fried in oil or ghee. It is closer to a sweet griddle cake than to injera.

Cultural background

It is eaten with honey or on its own. Cardamom is the perfume. Street vendors and home cooks both make it.

Why it tastes this way

Sugar, egg, and cardamom move this cake into the sweet breakfast category, while laxoox stays sour and bready. Cardamom is a trade spice of the Indian Ocean coast, which is why it shows up in Somali sweets. Oil or ghee gives a fried tenderness a dry sour bread does not have.

How it is made

A sweet egg batter with cardamom is fried as small thick pancakes.

Authentic touch. Cook on medium and flip once. It should be golden, not as pale as laxoox and not as holey. A cover helps the center set.

How it is used

Warm, plain or with honey.

Field notes

  • Malawah is the sweet Somali pancake. Laxoox is the sour one.
  • Cardamom is a typical spice in the sweet batter.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 1 large egg
  • 3/4 cup milk
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon cardamom
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • Oil or ghee for the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk the batter until smooth.
  2. Fry small cakes in a little oil over medium.
  3. Flip when the underside is golden.
  4. Cook until the center is set.
  5. Serve warm.
  6. Honey on top is optional.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Laxoox is the sour fermented bread. Do not swap the names.
  • Ghee instead of oil is richer.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • honey
  • tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Lebanon · No chili heat · 4 breads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Lebanon · Middle East

Manakish

A flatbread topped with za'atar and olive oil, baked or cooked on a griddle.

1 hr 10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 breads

History

Manakish, or manaqish, are Levantine flatbreads topped with za'atar and olive oil, a breakfast bread across Lebanon, Syria, Palestine, and Jordan. Bakeries bake them. A covered skillet is a home method and is the one written here, so the bread sits honestly among griddled foods. Za'atar is thyme, sesame, and sumac.

Cultural background

They are eaten warm, folded, with tea, labneh, or vegetables. Sesame in the za'atar is an allergen. This recipe assumes a za'atar blend that contains sesame and says so.

Why it tastes this way

Olive oil carries the thyme and sumac so the bread tastes herbal, sour, and rich rather than plain. Sumac’s lemony acidity is the Levantine note. The dough is a simple yeast dough, softer than a cracker. Breakfast bakeries made manakish a daily bread. The skillet version is supper or breakfast at home when the bakery is shut.

How it is made

Small yeasted rounds are topped with za'atar and oil and cooked in a covered skillet.

Authentic touch. Dimple the dough, spread oil and za'atar to the edge, and cook covered so the top sets while the bottom browns. Do not drown it in so much oil that it fries into a doughnut.

How it is used

Warm, folded.

Field notes

  • Za'atar and oil are the classic topping.
  • Many za'atar blends contain sesame.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups flour
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 1 tablespoon oil
  • 3 tablespoons za'atar
  • 3 tablespoons olive oil

Steps

  1. Mix flour, yeast, salt, water, and 1 tablespoon oil. Rise 1 hour.
  2. Divide into 4 and flatten.
  3. Mix za'atar and olive oil and spread it on the dough.
  4. Cook in a covered skillet over medium until the bottom is spotted and the top looks set.
  5. Serve warm.
  6. Fold, do not stack uncovered.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Cheese manakish add akkawi or similar and are then dairy.
  • A bakery oven gives a drier crust. The flavor is the same topping.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • tea
  • vegetables
  • labneh
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Nigeria · No chili heat · about 12 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Nigeria · West Africa

Masa

Sour rice cakes, cooked in small molds, crisp at the edge.

40 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 12 cakes

History

Masa, also called waina, is a Hausa fermented rice cake from northern Nigeria and the wider Sahel, including Niger. Rice or sometimes millet is soaked, ground, fermented, and cooked in small round molds or a pan. It is a street and home breakfast, eaten plain, with honey, or with a savory sauce. This version uses rice and a skillet if you do not have the clay molds.

Cultural background

The ferment is the sourness. Yeast can hurry a home batch, and a natural ferment is the older method. The recipe uses a pinch of yeast so the timing is reliable, and it says so.

Why it tastes this way

Rice, soaked and soured, tastes gently tart and a little sweet when the edges fry. The small molds, or a small skillet cake, give a crisp rim and a soft center. Masa is a Sahel food, where rice and millet are the grains, not wheat. Honey or pepper sauce are the two directions, sweet or hot, and both are served.

How it is made

Soaked rice is blended, fermented with a little yeast, and fried as small thick cakes.

Authentic touch. Grind or blend the soaked rice until smooth. Lumpy batter makes a raw center. Oil the pan well. The cakes stick when the surface is dry.

How it is used

Plain, with honey, or with a pepper sauce on the side.

Field notes

  • Masa and waina are names you will both meet for these rice cakes.
  • The yeast here is a home timing aid. A wild ferment of the same batter is traditional.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup raw rice, soaked 8 hours and drained
  • 1/2 cup water
  • 1/4 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1/2 teaspoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Blend the soaked rice with water until smooth.
  2. Stir in yeast, sugar, and salt. Rest 1 to 2 hours until bubbly.
  3. Oil a skillet or muffin pan over medium.
  4. Fry small mounds until set and browned on both sides.
  5. The center should be cooked, not gritty.
  6. Serve warm.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Millet masa is a grain variation.
  • Sinasir is the related Hausa cake often made from millet, on its own page.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Street food
  • honey
  • pepper sauce
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

India · Mild · 8 filled dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Masala Dosa

A crisp dosa wrapped around a spiced potato filling.

40 min prep · 40 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 filled dosas

History

Masala dosa is a dosa filled with a spiced potato mash, now served in tiffin rooms across India and in diaspora restaurants. The potato filling is a later, beloved addition to the older rice-dal crepe. Onions, mustard seeds, curry leaves, and turmeric are the usual seasoning. It is still South Indian food, even when a restaurant in another city makes it.

Cultural background

The dosa is cooked crisp, the potato is spooned on, and the crepe is folded. Coconut chutney and sambar come alongside. This page uses the plain dosa batter and writes the filling.

Why it tastes this way

Mustard seeds, curry leaves, and turmeric make the potato taste savory and slightly bitter-hot, which is why a mild crepe can carry a whole meal. The potato is a comfortable starch against the sour batter. Curry leaves are a South Indian aroma that does not survive as a dried jar very well. Fresh leaves are the difference. The mustard allergen is real: the seeds are in the filling.

How it is made

A fermented dosa is filled with potatoes tempered with mustard seed, onion, turmeric, and curry leaves, then folded.

Authentic touch. Cook the dosa crisp before you add the potato. A soft dosa collapses under the filling. The potato should be dry enough to sit, not a soup.

How it is used

With coconut chutney and sambar.

Field notes

  • The potato filling is what makes a dosa a masala dosa.
  • Mustard seeds are in the filling, which is why this page lists mustard.

Ingredients

  • 1 batch plain dosa batter, fermented
  • 4 medium potatoes, boiled and cubed
  • 1 onion, sliced
  • 1 teaspoon mustard seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon turmeric
  • 8 curry leaves
  • 1 green chile, sliced
  • 1 tablespoon oil
  • Salt

Steps

  1. Make and ferment the batter as for plain dosa.
  2. Temper mustard seeds in oil, then add onion, chile, curry leaves, and turmeric. Stir in the potatoes and salt. Cook until the potato is dry and seasoned.
  3. Cook a crisp dosa. Spoon potato in the center and fold.
  4. Serve with chutney and sambar.
  5. Keep the filling warm.
  6. Do not overload the crepe.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Mysore masala dosa adds a red chutney inside the crepe.
  • A cheese dosa is a restaurant invention and a different dish.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Stuffed or filled
  • Street food
  • coconut chutney
  • sambar
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free

Open the field-guide page

United States · No chili heat · about 4 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Matzo Brei

Soft scrambled egg and toasted matzo, salty or lightly sweet.

10 min prep · 8 min cook · Serves 2 · about 4 cakes

History

Matzo brei is broken matzo soaked, mixed with egg, and fried. It is Passover food, when leavened bread is out of the Ashkenazi kitchen, and it is also a year-round breakfast for people who simply like it. The name is Yiddish: matzo cooked with egg. American Jewish households, especially in the Northeast, treat the salty-versus-sweet argument as part of the dish.

Cultural background

Some cooks fry it as a pancake-like cake. Others scramble it. Both are matzo brei. This recipe makes small cakes, so it sits honestly among pancakes, with the scrambled way noted as a variation.

Why it tastes this way

Matzo is an unleavened cracker of flour and water, baked fast, which is why it tastes toasty and dry. A short soak gives it just enough water to turn custardy with egg. At Passover the method exists because leavening is set aside. Salt and pepper make it breakfast. Sugar or jam makes it the other family. The taste is cracker and egg, not batter and baking powder.

How it is made

Sheets of matzo are wetted, mixed with beaten egg, and fried in cakes in a lightly oiled pan.

Authentic touch. Soak the matzo until it bends, not until it is porridge. Thirty seconds in water is often enough for thin matzo. Squeeze gently.

How it is used

Salt and pepper, or sugar and jam. Sour cream if you eat dairy with it.

Field notes

  • Salty and sweet matzo brei are both traditional in different families.
  • The cake shape is one method. Scrambled matzo brei is the other.

Ingredients

  • 2 sheets matzo
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • Black pepper
  • 1 tablespoon neutral oil or chicken fat

Steps

  1. Break the matzo, rinse it under water until flexible, and squeeze out the extra water.
  2. Beat the eggs with salt and pepper and stir in the matzo.
  3. Heat oil in a skillet.
  4. Fry as two or four small cakes, turning once, until the egg is set and the edges brown.
  5. Serve salty, or with sugar and jam.
  6. Eat it at once. It toughens as it sits.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Scramble it in the pan instead of shaping cakes if that is your house style.
  • A grated onion in the egg is a savory turn.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Holiday table
  • salt
  • jam
  • sour cream
  • applesauce
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

Paraguay · No chili heat · one 9-inch cake

Flying Pancakes of the World

Paraguay · South America

Mbeju

A crisp cassava-starch and cheese cake, rich and salty.

15 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 6 · one 9-inch cake

History

Mbeju, or mbeyú, is a Paraguayan cake of cassava starch and cheese, cooked on a griddle or in a pan until the outside is crisp. It is a traditional breakfast and snack, part of a cuisine that also includes chipa and sopa paraguaya. Cheese and starch are the body. An egg helps it hold.

Cultural background

It should be thin enough to crisp and rich enough to taste of cheese. It is eaten with coffee or cocido. Fresh farmer cheese or a dry queso is better than a wet mozzarella.

Why it tastes this way

Cassava starch crisps, and a salty cheese melts into it, which is the Paraguayan combination of a local starch and a dairy farm. The flavor is salty, toasted, and rich, not sweet. An egg binds. The griddle is the tool. It is a national breakfast cake, not a sweet pancake.

How it is made

Cassava starch, cheese, egg, and milk or cream are cooked as a flat cake in a skillet.

Authentic touch. Grate the cheese dry. Pat the dough thin in a buttered pan and cook it patiently until the bottom is a real crust. Wet cheese makes it steam.

How it is used

Warm, in wedges, with coffee.

Field notes

  • Mbeju is cassava starch and cheese, crisp on the griddle.
  • Use a dry cheese or the cake steams.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup cassava starch
  • 1 cup finely grated dry cheese
  • 1 egg
  • 1/4 cup milk
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon butter

Steps

  1. Mix starch, cheese, egg, milk, and salt into a soft dough.
  2. Pat it thin in a buttered skillet.
  3. Cook covered over medium-low until the bottom is a real crust.
  4. Brown the top.
  5. Rest 5 minutes and cut.
  6. The cheese should be dry.

Use commercial cassava flour or yuca sold ready to cook. Do not process bitter cassava at home from these pages.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Anise is sometimes in the dough.
  • Beiju is the plainer starch bread without cheese.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • coffee
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Morocco · No chili heat · 6 rounds

Flying Pancakes of the World

Morocco · North Africa

Meloui

A round, coiled flatbread, flaky and rich, cooked on a griddle.

40 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 rounds

History

Meloui, or rghaif in some naming, are round coiled Moroccan pan breads, a cousin of square msemen. The dough is stretched, oiled, and rolled into a spiral, then flattened and cooked. They are breakfast breads, served with honey. Naming varies by region. This page uses meloui for the coil, and msemen for the square, which is a useful distinction even where cooks blur it.

Cultural background

The spiral is the difference you can see. The taste is still oil, dough, and honey.

Why it tastes this way

A coil creates layers in a round instead of a square, so the bread puffs in a spiral and tastes flaky. The same soft dough and the same griddle as msemen are at work. Honey is the sweetener because it soaks the hot layers. Semolina keeps the coil from gluing shut before it cooks.

How it is made

Soft dough is stretched, oiled, coiled, flattened, and cooked on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Roll the oiled strip into a coil, then flatten gently so you can still imagine the spiral. If you mash it paper-thin, the layers vanish.

How it is used

Warm, with honey.

Field notes

  • The coil is what separates this bread from square msemen in this guide.
  • Regional names overlap. The shape is the clear difference.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups flour
  • 1/2 cup fine semolina
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 3/4 cup warm water
  • 1/4 cup oil, plus more for shaping

Steps

  1. Make a soft dough from flour, semolina, salt, water, and 1/4 cup oil. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Stretch each piece into a strip, oil it, and roll it into a coil.
  3. Flatten the coil into a round.
  4. Cook on a medium oiled griddle until both sides are spotted and the center is cooked.
  5. Serve with honey.
  6. Eat warm.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Msemen are the squares.
  • A savory coil with spicy filling is a different, stuffed bread.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • honey
  • butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Mexico · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Mexico · Mexico & Central America

Mexican Hotcakes

A diner-style pancake, often served with cajeta or condensed milk.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Hotcakes are a standard Mexican breakfast, the American-style pancake under a Spanish name, served in fondas and at home with butter, honey, cajeta, or fruit. They are not a pre-Hispanic cake. Corn tortillas are the older griddle bread. Hotcakes are the twentieth-century breakfast beside eggs and coffee.

Cultural background

Cajeta, goat’s-milk caramel, is a beloved pour and is dairy. Honey is the other. The batter is a simple baking-powder batter.

Why it tastes this way

The cake tastes like a mild wheat pancake because that is the café style that spread. Cajeta makes the plate Mexican: goat’s milk cooked with sugar until it is caramel, a colonial-era dairy sweet. Condensed milk is the humbler pour. The hotcake itself is not an ancient recipe, and the page does not pretend it is.

How it is made

A milk batter is cooked as pancakes and served with cajeta or honey.

Authentic touch. Cook them as ordinary pancakes. Warm the cajeta so it pours. Cold cajeta sits in a lump on a hot cake.

How it is used

With butter and cajeta, honey, or fruit.

Field notes

  • Hotcakes are a Mexican breakfast. They are not a pre-Hispanic corn cake.
  • Cajeta, goat’s-milk caramel, is a classic pour.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup milk
  • 1 egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter
  • Cajeta or honey, to serve

Steps

  1. Whisk a batter and rest 5 minutes.
  2. Cook on a buttered medium griddle until both sides are brown.
  3. Warm the cajeta.
  4. Serve with butter and cajeta or honey.
  5. Do not sweeten the batter like cake.
  6. Eat them hot.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Hotcakes de elote add corn.
  • Crepas de cajeta are the thin version.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • cajeta
  • honey
  • butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Michigan Cherry Pancakes

Tart dried or fresh cherries in a buttermilk cake.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Michigan, especially around Traverse City, grows a large share of the American tart cherry crop. Pancakes with those cherries are a modern regional breakfast served in cherry season and, with dried cherries, the rest of the year. They are not an old voyageur recipe. The orchard is the fact that makes the plate local.

Cultural background

Tart cherries, not sweet binge cherries, are the Michigan product. They need a little sugar so the cake does not taste like a sour candy.

Why it tastes this way

Montmorency and other tart cherries are acidic, which is why cherry pies from this region are sharp rather than floral. Dried cherries concentrate that acid. A buttermilk batter has its own tang, so the fruit should be chopped and sugared or the cake tastes harsh. The Great Lakes climate, with cold winters and a moderated shoreline, is what built the orchards.

How it is made

Chopped tart cherries are folded into a buttermilk batter.

Authentic touch. If you use dried cherries, soak them in hot water for 10 minutes and pat them dry. Unsoaked dried fruit stays leathery.

How it is used

Butter. Syrup can be light because the fruit is already sharp and sweet.

Field notes

  • Michigan’s tart cherry crop is the local reason for this breakfast.
  • The pancake is a modern use of that crop, stated plainly.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup dried tart cherries or 1 cup fresh tart cherries, pitted and chopped
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter

Steps

  1. Soak dried cherries 10 minutes if using them, then drain and chop. Toss fresh cherries with a pinch of the sugar.
  2. Mix the batter and fold in the fruit.
  3. Cook on a buttered medium griddle.
  4. Flip carefully. Cherries scorch.
  5. Cook until the center is set.
  6. Serve warm.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Fresh pitted tart cherries in July are the best version.
  • Sweet cherries are a different, milder fruit.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • butter
  • maple syrup
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United Kingdom · No chili heat · about 12 pikelets

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Midlands Pikelets

Thick, soft, lightly sweet griddle cakes.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 12 pikelets

History

Across much of the Midlands, a pikelet is a small thick pancake, close to a Scotch pancake or drop scone, not the holey Yorkshire kind. The shared name confuses visitors and is worth keeping straight. These are teatime cakes, buttered, sometimes with jam or syrup.

Cultural background

They are made from a thick egg batter and cooked in spoonfuls. They do not need yeast or rings.

Why it tastes this way

Egg and baking powder lift a soft crumb, and a spoon of sugar makes them tea cakes rather than bread. They taste milder and sweeter than a crumpet because they are not a salt sponge full of holes. The regional word is the cultural fact. The batter is the technical one.

How it is made

A thick sweetened batter is dropped onto a griddle and flipped once.

Authentic touch. Flip when the bubbles pop and stay open. They dry out if you cook them as long as a large pancake.

How it is used

Butter, jam, or a little golden syrup.

Field notes

  • Same word as the Yorkshire pikelet, different cake. The split is real.
  • They are the English drop-scone family.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 1/2 cup milk
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir the dry ingredients together, then stir in the wet ingredients until the batter is combined.
  2. The batter should drop from the spoon and hold a shape.
  3. Heat a griddle or heavy skillet over medium heat and film it with butter.
  4. Spoon on the batter. Cook until the underside is browned and the top looks set.
  5. Flip once and cook the second side through.
  6. Cook small rounds and serve warm with butter.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Yorkshire pikelets are the holey ones.
  • Currants in the batter are a home variation.

Pairings and uses

  • Snack
  • Breakfast
  • butter
  • jam
  • golden syrup
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Minnesota Wild Rice Pancakes

Nutty popped wild rice in a mild buttermilk cake.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Wild rice, manoomin, is a traditional food of Ojibwe and other Anishinaabe peoples of the Great Lakes, harvested from lakes and not a true rice. Minnesota still harvests and cultivates it. Stirring cooked wild rice into wheat pancakes is a modern Minnesota breakfast that uses the grain. It is not an Anishinaabe pancake recipe, and this page does not claim it as one.

Cultural background

The grain is cooked ahead, then folded into batter so it stays distinct. It is a north-woods pantry item that also became a state agricultural product.

Why it tastes this way

Wild rice tastes toasted and slightly grassy because the grains are parched. A wheat batter without it would taste like any diner pancake. The lakes and the cool northern climate are why the plant is here. Cooking it separately keeps that toasted flavor from turning to mush inside the cake.

How it is made

Cooked wild rice is folded into a buttermilk pancake batter.

Authentic touch. Cook the wild rice until it splits, drain it well, and cool it before it goes into the batter. Warm rice melts the batter and makes heavy cakes.

How it is used

Butter and maple syrup. The grain is savory enough that some people eat the cakes with salt instead.

Field notes

  • Wild rice is not rice. It is a native aquatic grain.
  • The pancake is a modern use. The grain’s older history belongs to Anishinaabe foodways.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup cooked wild rice, cooled
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter

Steps

  1. Cook wild rice in water until it splits, then drain and cool. You need 1/2 cup cooked.
  2. Mix the batter and fold in the rice.
  3. Rest 5 minutes.
  4. Cook on a buttered medium griddle until the centers set.
  5. Flip once.
  6. Serve with maple syrup or salt.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A purely savory version skips the sugar and is eaten with smoked fish. That plate adds a fish allergen.
  • Cultivated wild rice and hand-harvested wild rice differ in price and in how evenly they cook. Both work if they are fully cooked.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • maple syrup
  • butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Tunisia · No chili heat · 6 breads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Tunisia · North Africa

Mlewi

A soft, oiled Tunisian flatbread cooked on a griddle.

30 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 breads

History

Mlewi, also spelled mlawi, is a Tunisian pan flatbread related to msemen, oiled and folded or rolled, then cooked. It is street and home breakfast food, often eaten with harissa, tuna, or honey. Harissa adds chile. This recipe is the bread. Tuna would add fish.

Cultural background

It should be soft and layered. Vendors cook it to order. Honey and harissa are two different breakfasts, sweet and hot.

Why it tastes this way

Oil in the layers makes mlewi rich and pliable, a wrap for harissa or tuna rather than a dry cracker. Tunisia’s breakfast culture puts chile paste on bread, which is why the same dough can be sweet with honey or hot with harissa. The dough itself is mild. The condiment is the decision.

How it is made

A soft dough is oiled, flattened, and cooked on a griddle until supple and spotted.

Authentic touch. Keep the dough soft and the griddle moderate. A hard crust means the bread will not fold around a filling.

How it is used

Warm, with honey or with harissa. Harissa makes the plate spicy. The bread is not.

Field notes

  • Mlewi is the Tunisian oiled flatbread in this family.
  • Harissa is a condiment, not part of the dough.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups flour
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 3 tablespoons oil, plus more for the pan

Steps

  1. Mix flour, salt, water, and oil into a soft dough. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Divide, oil, and flatten into rounds.
  3. Cook on a medium griddle until spotted and cooked through.
  4. Keep them soft under a towel.
  5. Serve with honey or harissa.
  6. They are best warm.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A tuna and egg filling is a common street meal and adds fish and egg.
  • Msemen is the Moroccan square cousin.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • honey
  • harissa
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Madagascar · No chili heat · about 12 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Madagascar · East Africa

Mofo Gasy

Sweet, tangy rice cakes cooked in rounds, a street breakfast.

30 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 12 cakes

History

Mofo gasy means Malagasy bread. The common street version is a fermented rice cake, slightly sweet and sour, cooked in small molds or a pan and sold in the morning. Rice is the staple of Madagascar. The cake is what a rice country does for a portable breakfast.

Cultural background

Vendors cook them in ring molds. A home skillet works. They are eaten plain or with coffee. The sourness comes from a ferment or from a little yeast and time.

Why it tastes this way

Rice, soured, tastes mildly sweet and tangy, and sugar in the batter pushes it toward a cake rather than a bread. Madagascar’s daily rice is why the breakfast is not wheat. A ring mold gives a neat round that cooks through at the edge first. The ferment is the flavor that makes them more than plain rice flour pancakes.

How it is made

Soaked rice is blended, sweetened, fermented briefly with yeast, and cooked as small cakes.

Authentic touch. Blend soaked rice very smooth. Rest the batter until it is a little sour and bubbly. Oil the rings or the pan or the cakes will stick and tear.

How it is used

Warm, as breakfast, plain or with a little honey if you eat honey.

Field notes

  • Mofo gasy is a rice cake, a breakfast sold on the street.
  • Rice keeps it free of gluten.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup rice, soaked 8 hours and drained
  • 1/2 cup water
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • Oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Blend the rice and water until smooth.
  2. Stir in sugar, yeast, and salt. Rest 1 to 2 hours until bubbly.
  3. Oil a skillet or small rings over medium.
  4. Cook small cakes until set and browned on both sides.
  5. Serve warm.
  6. The center should be tender, not gritty.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A wild ferment without yeast takes longer and can be more sour.
  • Savory mofo exist. This is the sweet street style.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Snack
  • coffee
  • honey
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · Mild · about 6 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Moong Dal Cheela

A savory pancake of soaked yellow moong dal, ginger, and green chile.

20 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 3 · about 6 pancakes

History

Moong dal cheela is a North Indian breakfast pancake made from soaked split yellow moong, ground with ginger and chile. It is a home and diet-conscious tiffin food, high in the lentil and free of wheat when no flour is added. It is related to pesarattu and milder than adai.

Cultural background

It is cooked like a thick crepe and eaten with green chutney. A spoon of rice flour can help it hold. This version uses only the dal.

Why it tastes this way

Yellow moong tastes sweeter and lighter than besan, and grinding it fresh keeps that sweetness. Ginger and chile are the seasoning because the dal itself is gentle. No ferment means it tastes clean. It is a lentil breakfast from a pantry that already soaked dals for other dishes.

How it is made

Soaked moong dal is ground with ginger and chile and cooked as pancakes.

Authentic touch. Grind until almost smooth, with enough water to spread. A coarse grind stays gritty. Cook covered for a minute so the dal sets.

How it is used

With green chutney.

Field notes

  • Split yellow moong is the dal for this cheela.
  • It is an unfermented lentil pancake.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup split yellow moong dal, soaked 3 hours and drained
  • 1 inch ginger
  • 1 green chile
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water, to grind
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Grind the dal, ginger, chile, cumin, salt, and a little water to a smooth spreadable batter.
  2. Cook pancakes on a medium oiled tawa, covered briefly.
  3. Flip and cook until both sides are spotted and the center is set.
  4. Serve with chutney.
  5. Thin the batter if it will not spread.
  6. Eat them hot.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Pesarattu is the Andhra whole-green-gram crepe, a close cousin.
  • A spoon of rice flour makes the batter easier and is still gluten-free.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • green chutney
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Mountain Buttermilk Pancakes

Plain, tangy, and a little less sweet than a diner stack.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Once roller mills and baking powder reached mountain stores, wheat pancakes joined corn cakes at breakfast. Buttermilk from the family cow was the liquid. The cake is deliberately plain. Sweetness arrived at the table as sorghum or honey, not as a cup of sugar in the bowl.

Cultural background

This is the everyday wheat cake against which buckwheat cakes and corn cakes were judged. It is still what many mountain cooks mean by pancakes.

Why it tastes this way

Low sugar lets the toasted flour and the sour milk show. That balance comes from a kitchen that treated syrup as the sweet course and the cake as bread. Baking soda is here for the acid in the milk. Without it the crumb is heavy and the tang tastes harsh rather than lifted.

How it is made

A lightly sweetened buttermilk batter is cooked on a griddle in a moderate stack.

Authentic touch. Keep the sugar to a spoon. If you miss the diner taste, add syrup on the plate instead of turning the batter into cake.

How it is used

Butter and sorghum, honey, or fruit butter.

Field notes

  • The plainness is intentional. Syrup was the sweetener.
  • Apple butter on top is a mountain pantry match.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter or lard

Steps

  1. Stir the dry ingredients together, then stir in the wet ingredients until the batter is combined.
  2. Rest 5 minutes. The batter should fall from the spoon in a thick ribbon.
  3. Heat a griddle or heavy skillet over medium heat and film it with butter.
  4. Spoon on the batter. Cook until the underside is browned and the top looks set.
  5. Flip once and cook the second side through.
  6. Serve with syrup on the side, not cooked into the cake.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Half buckwheat flour moves this toward Appalachian buckwheat cakes.
  • A mashed banana is a later addition, not the old cake.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • sorghum
  • honey
  • apple butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

Morocco · No chili heat · 8 squares

Flying Pancakes of the World

Morocco · North Africa

Msemen

Square, layered pan-fried flatbread, flaky with oil.

40 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 8 · 8 squares

History

Msemen are Moroccan layered flatbreads, folded into squares and cooked on a griddle with oil. They are a breakfast and street bread, richer than a plain pancake because of the layers. They are often eaten with honey, cheese, or alongside a tagine. They are a griddled bread, included here as a pancake relative, not as a crepe.

Cultural background

The dough is soft. Each piece is oiled, folded, and flattened again so the layers stay. A good msemen peels a little when you tear it.

Why it tastes this way

Oil between thin layers of dough fries into flakes, which is why msemen taste rich without a sweet batter. Semolina on the board keeps them from sticking and adds a sandy crunch. Honey on a hot square melts into the layers. The technique is North African pan bread, closer to a laminated dough than to a poured pancake, and honest about that.

How it is made

A soft dough is divided, oiled, folded into squares, and cooked on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Oil your hands. If the dough fights you, it needs another spoon of water. Cook on medium until both sides are spotted and the center is not raw dough.

How it is used

Warm, with honey or soft cheese.

Field notes

  • Msemen are folded squares. The layers are the point.
  • They are a griddled bread, not a batter pancake.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups flour
  • 1/2 cup fine semolina
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 3/4 cup warm water
  • 1/4 cup oil, plus more for folding

Steps

  1. Mix flour, semolina, salt, water, and 1/4 cup oil into a soft dough. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Divide into 8. Oil each, flatten, fold into a square, and flatten again.
  3. Cook on a medium oiled griddle until spotted and cooked through, turning once.
  4. Serve warm.
  5. Honey is the usual sweet finish.
  6. They are best the hour they are cooked.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Meloui is the coiled round cousin.
  • Stuffed msemen with peppers or meat are a meal and a different recipe.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Bread course
  • honey
  • butter
  • cheese
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · Medium · 6 dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Mysore Masala Dosa

A dosa spread with a red chile-garlic chutney, then filled with potato.

30 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 3 · 6 dosas

History

Mysore masala dosa is a restaurant style associated with Mysore: a dosa spread inside with a red chutney of chile, garlic, and often chana dal, then filled with potato masala. It is spicier than a plain masala dosa. The red paste is the difference, not a different batter.

Cultural background

Tiffin rooms made the style famous beyond Mysore. The chutney is spread on the cooking dosa so it fries onto the crepe.

Why it tastes this way

Dried red chile and garlic, fried onto the crepe, taste hotter and deeper than potato alone. Chana dal, if you use a spoon of it, adds body to the paste. The potato stays mild, so each bite has heat and comfort. The style is a city restaurant habit built on the older dosa. Chile is why this page is marked medium.

How it is made

A crisp dosa is painted with a red chile chutney and filled with the same potato masala as a masala dosa.

Authentic touch. Spread the red chutney on the dosa while it is still on the pan, then add potato. If you spread it after, it tastes raw.

How it is used

Folded, with coconut chutney. The red chutney is already inside.

Field notes

  • The red chutney on the crepe is what makes it Mysore masala.
  • It is hotter than a plain masala dosa.

Ingredients

  • 1 batch dosa batter
  • 1 batch potato masala
  • 4 dried red chiles, stemmed
  • 2 garlic cloves
  • 1 tablespoon chana dal, soaked 30 minutes
  • 1 tablespoon oil
  • Salt

Steps

  1. Blend the soaked dal, chiles, garlic, oil, and salt into a thick paste. Add a spoon of water if needed.
  2. Cook a dosa. Spread a thin layer of the red paste on it while it cooks.
  3. Add potato masala and fold.
  4. Serve with coconut chutney.
  5. The paste should fry, not stay raw.
  6. Seed the chiles if you want less heat.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Without the red chutney it is a masala dosa, not a Mysore one.
  • Less chile makes a milder paste. The style is supposed to be noticeably hot.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Stuffed or filled
  • coconut chutney
  • sambar
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free

Open the field-guide page

India · No chili heat · about 10 dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Neer Dosa

A delicate, snowy-white rice crepe, thin and unfermented.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 10 dosas

History

Neer dosa is a coastal Karnataka rice crepe. Neer means water. The batter is freshly ground rice and water, thin and unfermented, cooked into a soft white crepe. It is associated with Mangalorean cooking and is often served with coconut chutney or a chicken or fish curry. The crepe itself is plain.

Cultural background

It should be soft enough to fold, not crackly like a restaurant ghee roast. The lack of dal and ferment is the difference from a classic dosa.

Why it tastes this way

Freshly ground rice and water taste sweet and clean, almost milky, because there is no sour dal ferment. Coastal kitchens that wanted a quick rice crepe did not wait overnight. The softness comes from a thin batter and a short cook. Coconut chutney echoes the coast. A crisp fermented dosa is a different grain balance.

How it is made

Soaked rice is ground with water to a very thin batter and cooked immediately as soft crepes.

Authentic touch. Grind the rice with plenty of water until the batter is smooth and watery. Pour a thin layer and cook it only until it lifts. Browning it hard dries it out.

How it is used

With coconut chutney. A curry alongside is a meal. The dosa is vegan and plain.

Field notes

  • Neer dosa is unfermented. Do not wait overnight and expect the classic taste.
  • It should be soft and pale, not deep brown.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup rice, soaked 4 hours and drained
  • 1 cup water, plus more if needed
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • Oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Grind the rice with water to a very smooth, thin batter. Add salt.
  2. Heat a lightly oiled nonstick pan.
  3. Pour a thin layer, swirl, and cook until the crepe lifts. Flip only if the top is wet.
  4. Fold and serve soft.
  5. Do not stack them uncovered or they dry.
  6. Coconut chutney is the partner.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A fermented dosa is sourer and usually contains dal.
  • Jaggery neer dosa is a sweet home variation.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • coconut chutney
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United States · No chili heat · 6 popovers

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

New England Popovers

Crisp shell, eggy steam, and a hollow center.

10 min prep · 35 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 popovers

History

Popovers are an American baked batter related to English Yorkshire pudding and to earlier popped breads. New England cookbooks have carried them since the nineteenth century as a breakfast bread and a partner for roast beef. They are not flipped. High egg and a very hot oven puff the batter into a hollow roll.

Cultural background

They come to the table in a basket, buttered, beside eggs at breakfast or a roast at dinner. The drama is the puff, which collapses if you open the oven early.

Why it tastes this way

Steam, not baking powder, lifts a popover. A hot oven turns the water in milk and egg into steam before the gluten and egg set, so the shell inflates. That is the same principle as Yorkshire pudding. New England’s version is usually a breakfast bread, served with butter and jam rather than roast drippings, because the region’s famous use of it moved from the meat course to the morning table.

How it is made

A thin batter of egg, milk, and flour is baked in a preheated hot pan until puffed and browned.

Authentic touch. Do not open the oven for the first 20 minutes. Fill the cups no more than half. A crowded cup cannot rise.

How it is used

Butter and jam at breakfast, or with a roast as you would Yorkshire pudding.

Field notes

  • Popover batter is close to Yorkshire pudding batter. The New England habit is often breakfast.
  • They are leavened by steam. Baking powder is not the engine.

Ingredients

  • 2 large eggs
  • 1 cup whole milk
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter, plus more for the pan

Steps

  1. Heat the oven to 425°F. Butter a popover or muffin pan and put it in the oven for 5 minutes.
  2. Whisk eggs, milk, flour, salt, and butter into a smooth thin batter.
  3. Pour the batter into the hot cups, filling them halfway.
  4. Bake 20 minutes without opening the door.
  5. Lower the heat to 375°F and bake about 10 minutes more, until deep brown.
  6. Serve at once. They settle as they cool.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A little grated cheese makes a savory popover.
  • Whole wheat flour works but rises less.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • Side dish
  • butter
  • jam
  • roast beef
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United States · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

New Mexico Blue Corn Pancakes

Slate-blue corn, a little sweet, with a toasted smell.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Blue corn is a Southwestern crop tended for centuries by Pueblo peoples, including Hopi farmers, and by other Indigenous growers. Hopi piki is a paper-thin blue-corn bread made with culinary ash on a hot stone. This guide does not give a piki recipe, because the ash and the stone are a specific practice and an improvised ash ratio would be a guess. Blue corn pancakes are a modern New Mexican breakfast that uses the regional meal in a simple griddle batter.

Cultural background

Cafés in Santa Fe and Albuquerque serve them with piñon, honey, or red chile on the side. Piñon is a tree nut and is optional here. The batter itself is meal, milk, and egg.

Why it tastes this way

Blue corn tastes earthier and a little sweeter than yellow meal because of the variety, not because of food coloring. It is an Indigenous crop adapted to high, dry fields. The pancake format is new. Honey or a side of red chile, sweet or hot, is how New Mexican plates frame corn. The chile is a regional sauce, not part of the batter, so this cake is marked as no chili heat.

How it is made

Blue cornmeal is mixed with milk, egg, and a little leavener and cooked as small cakes.

Authentic touch. Blue cornmeal can be coarse. Rest the batter 10 minutes. Cook on moderate heat so the natural sugars do not scorch into black spots.

How it is used

Honey or butter. A side of red chile is a New Mexican option and adds heat the batter does not have. Piñon nuts add a tree-nut allergen.

Field notes

  • Piki is a different Hopi bread. It is acknowledged here and not copied.
  • Blue corn pancakes are a modern café and home use of an older crop.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup blue cornmeal
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/4 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup milk
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir all ingredients and rest 10 minutes.
  2. Cook small cakes on a buttered medium-low griddle.
  3. Flip when the underside is browned.
  4. The center should be set, not chalky.
  5. Serve with honey or butter.
  6. Red chile on the side is optional and is a separate condiment.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Piñon on top is local and is a tree nut.
  • Yellow cornmeal makes a different cake. Do not dye it blue.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • honey
  • butter
  • red chile
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United States · No chili heat · 4 slices

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

New Orleans Pain Perdu

Custard-soaked bread, browned in butter, with brandy or orange in some pots.

15 min prep · 10 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 slices

History

Pain perdu, “lost bread,” is the French way of frying stale bread that has been soaked in milk and egg. New Orleans inherited the method through French and Creole cooking. On Creole tables it is a breakfast and a holiday dish, richer than plain American French toast, sometimes with orange zest, vanilla, or a splash of brandy.

Cultural background

It is what a bakery city does with day-old French bread. The name says the bread would have been lost. The soak saves it.

Why it tastes this way

Stale bread has lost water, so it drinks the custard instead of falling apart. French bread’s open crumb takes more custard than a tight sandwich loaf, which is why New Orleans pain perdu tastes softer and richer. Butter in the pan browns the milk sugars. Citrus and brandy, when used, are port flavors in a city built on trade.

How it is made

Stale bread is soaked in a sweetened milk-and-egg custard and fried in butter.

Authentic touch. Soak the slices long enough that the center is wet, then let the excess drip off. A dripping slice fries into a greasy puddle.

How it is used

Powdered sugar, cane syrup, or fruit. It can be breakfast or the end of a holiday meal.

Field notes

  • Pain perdu and American French toast are the same idea. The Creole version is usually richer and uses French bread.
  • The bread should be dry. Fresh, soft bread turns to mush.

Ingredients

  • 4 thick slices day-old French bread
  • 2 large eggs
  • 3/4 cup whole milk
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon vanilla
  • 1 teaspoon orange zest
  • Pinch of salt
  • 2 tablespoons butter

Steps

  1. Whisk eggs, milk, sugar, vanilla, zest, and salt.
  2. Lay the bread in the custard and turn it until the centers soften, a few minutes.
  3. Melt butter in a skillet over medium.
  4. Fry the bread until deep golden on both sides.
  5. The center should be set, not raw custard.
  6. Serve at once with powdered sugar or syrup.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Skip the brandy and it is still pain perdu.
  • Thick-cut bread needs a longer soak than thin sandwich bread.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Holiday table
  • powdered sugar
  • cane syrup
  • orange
  • berries
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United States · No chili heat · about 8 blintzes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

New York Blintzes

Thin egg crepes folded around sweet cheese, browned in butter.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 blintzes

History

Blintzes are the Ashkenazi filled pancake, descended from Slavic blini and crepes. Jewish immigrants from the Russian Empire and eastern Europe brought them to New York in the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries, and dairy restaurants and home kitchens made cheese blintzes a city staple. They are a Shavuot dairy food in Jewish practice and an everyday deli plate the rest of the year.

Cultural background

A blintz is cooked on one side, filled, folded like an envelope, and browned. The New York cheese filling is farmer cheese or pot cheese, lightly sweet. Fruit sauce on the side is common in restaurants.

Why it tastes this way

The wrapper is almost a crepe: egg-rich and thin, so the cheese is the flavor. Eastern European Jewish cooks used the soft cheeses they had, lightly sweetened, because Shavuot is a dairy holiday and because those cheeses were the pantry. Browning in butter, a second cook, gives the outside a diner crust that a pale crepe does not have. The New York plate often adds a tart fruit sauce because the filling is mild.

How it is made

Thin egg pancakes are filled with sweetened farmer cheese, folded, and fried in butter.

Authentic touch. Cook the wrapper on one side only before filling. If you brown both sides first, it cracks when you fold it.

How it is used

Sour cream and a spoon of blueberry or cherry sauce, or simply sour cream.

Field notes

  • Blintzes and blini are relatives. A blintz is filled and folded. A blin is usually the pancake itself.
  • Dairy restaurants helped make cheese blintzes a New York dish, not only a holiday one.

Ingredients

  • 3/4 cup all-purpose flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1 cup milk
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter, plus more for frying
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup farmer cheese or drained ricotta
  • 1 egg yolk
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon vanilla

Steps

  1. Blend flour, eggs, milk, 2 tablespoons butter, and salt into a thin batter. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Cook thin crepes on one side in a buttered skillet. Stack them browned side up.
  3. Mix cheese, yolk, sugar, and vanilla.
  4. Fill each crepe, fold the sides in, and roll.
  5. Brown the packets in butter over medium heat.
  6. Serve with sour cream.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A potato and onion filling is the savory cousin and is closer to a knish in spirit.
  • Ricotta can stand in for farmer cheese. It is wetter, so drain it.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Holiday table
  • Stuffed or filled
  • sour cream
  • blueberry sauce
  • cherry preserves
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

New Zealand · No chili heat · about 12 pikelets

Flying Pancakes of the World

New Zealand · Oceania

New Zealand Pikelets

Small drop scones, a close cousin of the Australian pikelet, served with butter and golden syrup.

10 min prep · 12 min cook · Serves 4 · about 12 pikelets

History

New Zealand pikelets are the same British drop-scone family as Australian pikelets, a standard in home baking and school cooking. Golden syrup and butter are the usual plate. They are not Māori fry bread. Treating them as a separate page keeps the shared recipe from erasing either country’s afternoon-tea habit.

Cultural background

They are small, thick, and best warm. Raspberry jam is a common second topping. The batter matches the Australian one closely, with a spoon more sugar in some New Zealand tins.

Why it tastes this way

The cake tastes of egg, butter, and a little sugar because that is the British tea cake that traveled. Golden syrup is the shared colonial pantry sweet of Australia and New Zealand. A pikelet is not a crepe and not a fry bread. The small size cooks quickly and gives more crust than a large pancake.

How it is made

A thick batter is cooked as small rounds.

Authentic touch. Cook them on medium and serve them in a stack under a cloth so the last one is not cold. Butter goes on while they are warm.

How it is used

With butter and golden syrup or jam.

Field notes

  • New Zealand pikelets belong to the same drop-scone family as the Australian ones.
  • Golden syrup is the usual sweet.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 egg
  • 1/2 cup milk
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter

Steps

  1. Mix a thick batter.
  2. Cook small rounds on a buttered griddle.
  3. Serve warm with butter and golden syrup or jam.
  4. Keep them covered so the last one stays soft.
  5. They are tea cakes.
  6. Do not thin the batter into crepes.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Australian pikelets are the twin.
  • A large breakfast pancake is a different pour of a thinner batter.

Pairings and uses

  • Snack
  • Breakfast
  • butter
  • golden syrup
  • jam
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free
