Russia · No chili heat · about 12 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Russia · Eastern Europe

Oladyi

Small, thick, kefir pancakes, lightly sour.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 12 cakes

History

Oladyi are small thick Russian pancakes, usually raised with kefir or sour milk and a chemical leavener rather than a long yeast sponge. They are homier and faster than blini, served with sour cream, honey, or jam. They are not the Maslenitsa centerpiece, though a family might fry them in that week too.

Cultural background

Kefir is the cultured milk that gives the tang. Yogurt thinned with milk is the workable substitute and should be called that.

Why it tastes this way

Kefir’s acid and baking soda make a quick lift and a sour taste that wants sour cream or a sweet jam, not both at once unless you like the contrast. They are thicker than blini because the batter is, so the inside stays soft. The speed is the cultural difference: oladyi are a morning cake, blini are a project.

How it is made

Kefir, egg, flour, and soda make a thick batter that is fried as small cakes.

Authentic touch. Fry them in a little oil, small and thick, and do not press them flat. They are supposed to puff.

How it is used

Sour cream, honey, or jam.

Field notes

  • Oladyi are the quick thick pancakes. Blini are yeasted.
  • Kefir is traditional. Thin yogurt is a substitute.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup kefir or plain yogurt thinned with a splash of milk
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup flour
  • Oil for frying

Steps

  1. Stir kefir, egg, sugar, soda, and salt. Add flour to a thick batter.
  2. Heat a film of oil over medium.
  3. Fry small cakes until puffed and browned on both sides.
  4. Drain.
  5. Serve with sour cream or jam.
  6. Stir the soda in just before cooking.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Apple grated into the batter is a common home oladyi.
  • Yeast blini are the different, holey cake.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • sour cream
  • jam
  • honey
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Ozark Corn Cakes

Yellow corn, buttermilk, and a fried edge.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

The Ozarks of Missouri and Arkansas share the upland South’s corn kitchen. Corn cakes cooked on a griddle were a farm breakfast alongside biscuits when wheat was on hand, and instead of biscuits when it was not. They belong with Appalachian corn cakes and are listed separately because Ozark cooks talk about their own tables, not as a suburb of Kentucky.

Cultural background

A simple meal-and-milk cake, eaten with sorghum, honey, or a savory meal. German-speaking settlers in parts of the region also had potato cakes. This entry stays with corn.

Why it tastes this way

Dent corn meal, stone-ground when you can get it, tastes sweeter and rougher than a wheat pancake. Buttermilk is the tenderizer. The Ozark pattern is the same agricultural fact as the Appalachian one: corn would grow on ridges where a wheat field was a gamble.

How it is made

Cornmeal, buttermilk, and egg are cooked as small cakes on a greased griddle.

Authentic touch. If the meal is very coarse, give the batter fifteen minutes. Fine meal needs less time and burns faster.

How it is used

Sorghum and butter, or with beans and fried potatoes.

Field notes

  • Ozark corn cakes and Appalachian cornmeal cakes are relatives, not one recipe with two labels.
  • Stone-ground meal needs a longer rest than degerminated supermarket meal.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup plain yellow cornmeal
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tablespoon oil or melted butter

Steps

  1. Mix all the ingredients and rest 10 to 15 minutes.
  2. Heat a skillet with a thin film of oil.
  3. Cook small cakes until the bottoms brown.
  4. Flip and cook until set.
  5. Keep finished cakes warm in a low oven.
  6. Serve with sorghum or a savory plate.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of bacon drippings in the batter is a savory turn.
  • Adding wheat flour makes a lighter cake and removes the gluten-free label.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • sorghum
  • fried potatoes
  • beans
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

France · No chili heat · 4 slices

Flying Pancakes of the World

France · Western Europe

Pain Perdu

Stale bread soaked in milk and egg, fried in butter.

15 min prep · 10 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 slices

History

Pain perdu, lost bread, is the French kitchen’s way of frying stale bread that has been soaked in milk and egg. The name says the bread would have been wasted. It is the ancestor of American French toast and a relative of New Orleans pain perdu, which is richer and often orange-scented. The French home version is simpler: stale bread, custard, butter, and sugar.

Cultural background

It is breakfast or a last-minute dessert. Brioche makes it cake-like. A lean country loaf makes it more bread-like. Both are fair if the bread is dry.

Why it tastes this way

Stale bread drinks custard because it has lost moisture. Fresh bread turns to paste. Butter browns the milk sugars on the surface. The thrift is the cultural fact: a bakery culture had leftover loaves, and the pancake journal includes this because it is the fried, egg-soaked cousin of the crêpe, not because it is poured batter.

How it is made

Stale bread is dipped in sweetened milk and egg and fried in butter.

Authentic touch. Dry the slices overnight if they are fresh. Soak them until heavy, then let them drip. Fry in butter over medium so the center custard sets.

How it is used

Sugar, honey, or fruit.

Field notes

  • The name means lost bread, bread that would have been thrown out.
  • Dry bread is the requirement. Fresh bread will not do.

Ingredients

  • 4 slices stale bread
  • 1 cup milk
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon vanilla
  • Pinch of salt
  • 2 tablespoons butter

Steps

  1. Whisk milk, egg, sugar, vanilla, and salt.
  2. Soak the bread until the centers soften.
  3. Fry in butter over medium until deep golden on both sides.
  4. The center should be set.
  5. Sugar them lightly.
  6. Serve at once.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • The New Orleans page is the Creole version with orange zest.
  • Brioche needs less soaking than a tight crumb.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • sugar
  • honey
  • berries
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

India · Mild · about 6 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Palak Cheela

A chickpea pancake green with spinach, ginger, and chile.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 3 · about 6 pancakes

History

Palak cheela is a home breakfast pancake: besan batter with chopped spinach, the sort of weeknight or morning dish that shows up in modern Indian cooking without a single ancient charter. Spinach and chickpea flour are both ordinary. The combination is a real home recipe, and it is described here as that, not as a temple dish.

Cultural background

It is eaten with pickle or yogurt. The spinach should be visible, not a drop of coloring.

Why it tastes this way

Spinach adds moisture, iron-rich color, and a green flavor that besan’s nuttiness can carry. Chile and ginger keep it from tasting like a bland health cake. It is a contemporary home use of two pantry foods. The lack of a long documented pedigree is stated so the page does not invent one.

How it is made

Besan batter is mixed with chopped spinach and cooked as savory pancakes.

Authentic touch. Chop the spinach fine and dry it. Wet leaves make the batter too loose and the cake steam instead of brown.

How it is used

With pickle or chutney.

Field notes

  • This is a modern home cheela, not a claim of an ancient named rite.
  • Dry the spinach or the batter will be watery.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup besan
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 1 cup finely chopped spinach, patted dry
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 1/2 teaspoon grated ginger
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Whisk besan, water, spinach, chile, ginger, salt, and cumin. Rest 10 minutes.
  2. Cook on a medium oiled tawa until both sides are browned and the center is set.
  3. Serve with pickle.
  4. The spinach should still look green.
  5. Do not cover so long that it steams gray.
  6. Eat hot.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Grated carrot or beet are the same idea with a different vegetable.
  • Besan cheela without spinach is the plain page.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • pickle
  • green chutney
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

India · Mild · about 14 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Paniyaram

Small round fermented-rice cakes from a special pan, crisp outside.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 14 cakes

History

Paniyaram, also kuzhi paniyaram or gunta ponganalu, are small cakes cooked in a hemispherical pan from dosa or idli batter. Sweet and savory versions both exist. Savory ones carry mustard seed, curry leaf, and ginger. They are a way to use fermented batter, and the pan is the tool.

Cultural background

The cakes are turned in the wells so both sides brown. They are a snack or a breakfast with chutney.

Why it tastes this way

The same fermented rice-dal batter that makes dosa tastes different when it is fried in a little pool of oil in a well: crisper, rounder, and more like a dumpling. Mustard and curry leaf make the savory version smell like a tiffin snack. The pan is South India’s cousin of other well pans in this journal, arrived at for the same reason: a small mold fries the sides.

How it is made

Dosa batter is seasoned and cooked in a paniyaram pan until round and browned.

Authentic touch. Get the pan properly hot and oil each well, or the cakes stick and tear when you turn them. Fill the wells about two-thirds full.

How it is used

With coconut chutney.

Field notes

  • The well pan defines paniyaram.
  • Savory and sweet versions both exist. This one is savory.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups fermented dosa batter
  • 1 teaspoon mustard seeds
  • 8 curry leaves, chopped
  • 1 teaspoon grated ginger
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Stir mustard, curry leaf, ginger, and chile into the batter.
  2. Oil a hot paniyaram pan.
  3. Fill the wells two-thirds full and cook until the bottoms are brown.
  4. Turn and cook until set.
  5. Serve with chutney.
  6. They should be crisp outside and soft inside.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A sweet jaggery batter is a different paniyaram.
  • Without the special pan, a muffin tin in the oven is a different cake. Say so if you do it.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • coconut chutney
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · No chili heat · about 6 large dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Paper Dosa

An extra-thin, extra-crisp, extra-large dosa.

10 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 6 · about 6 large dosas

History

Paper dosa is a restaurant style of plain dosa spread very thin and large until it is crisp as a cracker. The batter is the same fermented rice and urad batter. The technique is the difference: more oil or ghee, a hotter tawa, and a wider spread. It is a showpiece of tiffin rooms, not a separate ancient recipe.

Cultural background

It is served upright or broken, with chutney and sambar. The center may be a little softer than the lace edge. Ghee makes it richer and adds dairy. Oil keeps this version vegan.

Why it tastes this way

Thin batter and high heat drive off moisture until the rice starch shatters. That is why it tastes toasty and not spongy. The ferment is still there, fainter because so much surface has browned. The size is theater and also a way to keep the crepe crisp until it reaches the table.

How it is made

Fermented dosa batter is spread extra thin and cooked until brittle.

Authentic touch. Spread in widening circles on a very hot tawa, thinner than you think you can. Let it crisp before you try to lift it. A flexible paper dosa is just a thin plain dosa.

How it is used

With chutney and sambar, broken into shards if that is easier.

Field notes

  • Paper dosa is a technique applied to plain dosa batter.
  • It should snap. If it bends, cook it longer or thinner.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups fermented dosa batter, thinned with a spoon of water if needed
  • 2 tablespoons oil
  • A pinch of salt if needed

Steps

  1. Heat a large tawa until very hot and oil it lightly.
  2. Ladle batter in the center and spread it in a thin, wide spiral.
  3. Drizzle oil around the edge.
  4. Cook without flipping until the dosa is crisp and lifts in a sheet.
  5. If it bends, cook it a minute longer.
  6. Serve at once with chutney and sambar.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Ghee roast dosa is the same idea finished with ghee and often a red chutney.
  • Set dosa is the soft opposite.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • coconut chutney
  • sambar
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Pecan Pancakes

Buttermilk cake studded with toasted pecan.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Pecans are native to the south-central United States and have long been gathered and, later, orchard-grown across the South. Folding toasted pecans into a buttermilk pancake is a modern Southern breakfast habit built on that nut, not an old plantation receipt with a documented date. The nut is the point of the cake.

Cultural background

Georgia, Texas, and neighboring pecan states all claim the breakfast. What they share is the nut, toasted so it stays fragrant in a soft batter.

Why it tastes this way

A pecan tastes buttery because of its oil. Toasting drives off raw tannin and makes that oil smell sweet. If the nuts go in raw, the cake tastes flat and the pieces steam. The South’s native pecan, rather than imported almond, is why this breakfast cake tastes the way it does.

How it is made

A buttermilk batter takes a handful of toasted chopped pecans and is cooked like any wheat pancake.

Authentic touch. Toast the pecans and salt them lightly before they meet the batter. Stir them in at the end so the batter does not turn gray from the nut skins.

How it is used

Butter and sorghum or cane syrup. More pecans on top if you like.

Field notes

  • Pecans are a tree nut. This recipe is not nut-free.
  • Toasting is the difference between a fragrant cake and a raw one.

Ingredients

  • 3/4 cup chopped pecans
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter

Steps

  1. Toast the pecans in a dry skillet until fragrant. Salt them lightly and cool.
  2. Mix the batter as for buttermilk pancakes and fold in the pecans.
  3. Rest 5 minutes.
  4. Cook on a buttered medium griddle until both sides are browned and the centers are set.
  5. Serve hot.
  6. Keep leftover nuts aside. They soften if they sit in batter overnight.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Leave the nuts out of a few cakes for anyone who cannot eat tree nuts.
  • Black walnuts are a different, sharper nut used in Appalachian baking. They are not a silent swap.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • sorghum
  • butter
  • coffee
  • Vegetarian

Open the field-guide page

United States · No chili heat · about 10 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Pennsylvania Dutch Potato Pancakes

Shredded potato and onion, bound with egg and a little flour.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 10 cakes

History

German-speaking immigrants, later called Pennsylvania Dutch, settled in Pennsylvania in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries and brought potato pancakes with them. The cakes are everyday farm food, served with applesauce or sour cream, and they are part of the same family as German Kartoffelpuffer. They are not the same dish as Jewish latkes, though a hungry person could mistake the plate. The communities and the holiday meanings differ.

Cultural background

They show up at breakfast with apple butter and at supper beside meat. Church suppers still fry them by the dozen.

Why it tastes this way

A potato pancake tastes of starch browned in fat. German-speaking farmers in Pennsylvania had the potatoes, the onions, and the skillets. Flour in this version is the binder those recipes often use, which is why it is not gluten-free. Apple butter and applesauce on the side are the region’s fruit, preserved for winter, cutting the fry with acid.

How it is made

Grated potato and onion are mixed with egg and flour and fried as flat cakes.

Authentic touch. Squeeze the potatoes dry. Fry in a generous film of fat, not a dry nonstick pan, or the shreds never lace.

How it is used

Applesauce, apple butter, or sour cream.

Field notes

  • Pennsylvania Dutch means German-speaking settlers, from Deutsch, not a separate Dutch nation in this name.
  • These are relatives of German potato pancakes and are a different community’s food from Hanukkah latkes.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 pounds russet potatoes
  • 1 small onion
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons all-purpose flour
  • 3/4 teaspoon salt
  • Black pepper
  • Oil or butter for frying

Steps

  1. Grate potatoes and onion and squeeze dry.
  2. Mix with egg, flour, salt, and pepper.
  3. Heat a thin layer of oil over medium.
  4. Fry flattened spoonfuls until brown and crisp on both sides.
  5. Drain and salt.
  6. Serve with applesauce.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Leftover mashed potato makes a denser cake, closer to some boxty methods, and is a different texture.
  • Bacon fat is a farmhouse fat and adds meat.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Side dish
  • Supper
  • applesauce
  • apple butter
  • sour cream
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

India · Mild · about 8 crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Pesarattu

A green-gram crepe, savory with ginger and green chile.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 crepes

History

Pesarattu is an Andhra crepe made from whole green gram, moong, often with ginger and green chile. It is frequently served with upma, a semolina porridge, as pesarattu-upma. The crepe itself is the page. Upma is a partner and contains wheat. The pesarattu batter does not.

Cultural background

It is a breakfast crepe, thinner than a cheela and more specific than a generic moong pancake. Ginger is not optional if you want it to taste like pesarattu.

Why it tastes this way

Green gram tastes earthy and slightly sweet, different from urad dal in a classic dosa. Ginger and green chile cut that earthiness. There is often no long ferment, or only a short one, so the flavor stays beany rather than sour. Andhra breakfasts pair it with upma for a soft, salty contrast. The crepe is the protein. The upma is the wheat side.

How it is made

Soaked green gram is ground with ginger and chile and cooked as thin crepes.

Authentic touch. Grind the soaked moong very smooth with ginger. Cook it thinner than a cheela and oil the edges so it crisps. Do not expect dosa sourness.

How it is used

With ginger chutney, or with upma if you want the classic Andhra plate. Upma is a separate wheat dish.

Field notes

  • Pesarattu is green gram, an Andhra breakfast crepe.
  • The crepe is gluten-free. Upma beside it is not.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup whole green gram (moong), soaked 4 hours and drained
  • 1 inch ginger
  • 1 green chile
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water, to grind
  • Oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Grind dal, ginger, chile, cumin, salt, and enough water to make a spreadable batter.
  2. Cook thin crepes on a hot oiled tawa until the edges lift and the underside browns.
  3. Flip briefly.
  4. Serve warm.
  5. The batter should be smooth.
  6. Ginger should be visible as a flavor, not a hint.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • MLA pesarattu is a local name for a version stuffed with upma.
  • A fermented dosa batter is a different crepe.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • ginger chutney
  • upma
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Albania · No chili heat · about 16 pieces

Flying Pancakes of the World

Albania · Eastern Europe

Petulla

Irregular fried dough pieces, crisp and puffy, with cheese or honey.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 16 pieces

History

Petulla are Albanian fried dough pieces, a yeast or baking-powder batter dropped into oil. They are eaten with white cheese, honey, or powdered sugar, especially at breakfast or for guests. They are fritters rather than flipped pancakes, and they are a real national snack.

Cultural background

The shape is ragged on purpose. Cheese on the side is the savory way. Honey is the sweet way. This batter is the plain one, leavened with baking powder for a same-morning fry.

Why it tastes this way

Hot oil puffs a wet dough into something crisp outside and soft inside, a texture a dry griddle cannot make. White cheese is salty and is the contrast. Honey is the sweet contrast. Albania’s table often puts cheese and honey in the same breakfast orbit, even if not always on the same bite. The irregular edge is what holds either topping.

How it is made

A thick batter is fried in spoonfuls until puffed and golden.

Authentic touch. The oil should be hot enough that a drop of batter rises at once. Too cool, and petulla drink oil. Drop the batter from a spoon so the edges stay ragged.

How it is used

With white cheese or honey, not necessarily both.

Field notes

  • Petulla are fried batter pieces, not round diner pancakes.
  • Cheese and honey are both served. They are different plates.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup water
  • Oil for frying

Steps

  1. Stir flour, baking powder, salt, and water into a thick batter.
  2. Heat 1 inch of oil over medium.
  3. Drop spoonfuls and fry until golden and cooked through, turning once.
  4. Drain.
  5. Serve with cheese or honey.
  6. The centers should not be doughy.

Fry over steady heat and do not leave the pot.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A yeast batter rested an hour is more traditional.
  • Flija is the layered baked pie, a different Albanian specialty.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • white cheese
  • honey
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · No chili heat · about 10 dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Plain Dosa

A thin, tangy rice-and-lentil crepe, crisp at the edge.

40 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 5 · about 10 dosas

History

Dosa is a South Indian fermented crepe of rice and urad dal, the black gram. It is a breakfast and tiffin food in Tamil Nadu, Karnataka, Andhra Pradesh, Kerala, and far beyond those states now. The batter ferments overnight. A plain dosa is the cake itself, served with chutney and sambar. Masala dosa, the potato-filled one, has its own page.

Cultural background

Homes and tiffin shops both cook it on a hot tawa. The ferment is what makes it tangy and light. A batter that does not ferment in a cold kitchen can be helped by a warm spot, not by skipping the wait.

Why it tastes this way

Rice keeps the crepe crisp. Urad dal gives protein and the fermentation that tastes sour and a little sweet. The overnight rest is lactic fermentation in a warm climate, which is why dosa batter in a hot kitchen moves faster than batter in a cold one. The tawa’s heat is what laces the edge. Chutney and sambar are the sour, hot, and salty partners. The crepe itself is mild and tangy.

How it is made

Soaked rice and urad dal are ground, fermented, and cooked as thin crepes on a hot griddle.

Authentic touch. Spread the batter thin in a spiral from the center of a hot, lightly oiled tawa. Wait until the edge lifts. A pale, limp dosa needed more heat or more time.

How it is used

With coconut chutney and sambar. Ghee on the surface is a common finish and adds dairy.

Field notes

  • Plain dosa is the crepe. The potato filling makes it a masala dosa.
  • Fermentation is the flavor. An unfermented rice crepe is a different bread.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup rice
  • 1/4 cup urad dal
  • 1/2 teaspoon fenugreek seeds
  • Water, for soaking and grinding
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Oil for the tawa

Steps

  1. Soak rice, dal, and fenugreek together or separately for 6 hours.
  2. Grind to a smooth batter, a little thicker than cream. Ferment in a warm place 8 to 16 hours, until risen and sour.
  3. Stir in the salt. Thin with water if needed.
  4. Spread thin on a hot, lightly oiled tawa.
  5. Drizzle a few drops of oil around the edge. Cook until the underside is golden and the dosa lifts. Do not flip if it is already crisp.
  6. Serve with chutney and sambar.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Masala dosa adds a potato filling.
  • A spoon of soaked fenugreek in the batter is a common ferment aid.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Supper
  • Street food
  • coconut chutney
  • sambar
  • ghee
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

India · No chili heat · about 8 dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Ragi Dosa

An earthy finger-millet dosa, darker than a rice dosa.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 dosas

History

Ragi, finger millet, is a traditional grain in parts of Karnataka and Tamil Nadu. Ragi dosa is a crepe that uses ragi flour with rice or with a rice-dal batter. It is both an older grain in a newer breakfast format and a home recipe people cook for the millet. This version mixes ragi flour into a fermented rice-dal batter.

Cultural background

The color is brown-gray. The taste is earthier than plain dosa. Chutney is still the partner.

Why it tastes this way

Finger millet tastes mineral and slightly bitter, a dryland grain that stores well. Mixed with fermented rice and dal, it keeps that earthiness and gains the sourness of the batter. The cake is a way millet enters the dosa breakfast without pretending ragi was always cooked as a crisp crepe in every century. The flavor is the grain.

How it is made

Ragi flour is stirred into fermented dosa batter and cooked as crepes.

Authentic touch. Ragi browns fast. Keep the heat at medium and cook the dosa through, because the millet can taste raw if it is only browned on the outside.

How it is used

With coconut chutney.

Field notes

  • Ragi is finger millet, a real South Indian grain.
  • The earthy taste is the millet, not a burnt pan, if you cook it moderately.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups fermented dosa batter
  • 1/2 cup ragi flour
  • 1/4 cup water
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Whisk ragi, water, and salt into the dosa batter. Rest 10 minutes.
  2. Cook thin dosas on a medium oiled tawa until the underside is browned and the center is cooked.
  3. Flip if needed.
  4. Serve with chutney.
  5. They will be darker than rice dosas.
  6. Do not use high heat.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • An instant ragi dosa with yogurt is a different, quicker batter and adds dairy.
  • Plain dosa is the rice-dal cake without millet.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • coconut chutney
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

India · No chili heat · about 8 dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Rava Dosa

A lacy, crisp instant dosa of semolina and rice flour, with onion and chile.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 dosas

History

Rava dosa is an instant dosa, made with semolina (rava), rice flour, and water, without an overnight ferment. It became a popular tiffin-room dosa because it is fast and crisp, with a lacy look. It is not a substitute story for fermented dosa. It is its own recipe. Semolina is wheat, so it is not gluten-free.

Cultural background

Onion, cumin, and green chile are scattered on the batter as it cooks. The batter is thin, almost like water, which is why the lace forms.

Why it tastes this way

Semolina browns and crisps, and rice flour keeps the lace from tasting like a wheat pancake. No ferment means no sourness, so cumin, chile, and onion do the flavoring. The thin batter hits a hot tawa and sets in holes. That lace is the texture people order it for. The wheat in semolina is the allergen.

How it is made

A thin unfermented batter of rava and rice flour is poured on a hot tawa and cooked until crisp.

Authentic touch. Stir the batter every time you pour, because the semolina settles. Pour from a height or in a loose spiral and do not spread it like a fermented dosa. Spreading kills the lace.

How it is used

With chutney. It is best immediately. It loses its crispness as it sits.

Field notes

  • Rava dosa is instant. It is not a failed fermented dosa.
  • Semolina means it contains wheat.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup semolina
  • 1/2 cup rice flour
  • 2 tablespoons all-purpose flour
  • 1 1/2 cups water
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 1 small onion, finely chopped
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Oil for the tawa

Steps

  1. Whisk the flours, water, cumin, chile, onion, and salt. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Stir again. The batter should be thin.
  3. Pour onto a hot oiled tawa in a lacy spiral. Do not spread hard.
  4. Drizzle oil and cook until the underside is crisp. A flip is optional and often breaks the lace.
  5. Serve at once with chutney.
  6. Stir before each pour.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Onion rava dosa is this cake with more onion pressed in.
  • It will never taste sour. That is not a flaw.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • coconut chutney
  • sambar
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free

Open the field-guide page

United States · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Rhode Island Thick Johnnycakes

Soft corn interior, browned face, and the sweetness of milk.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Thick johnnycakes are the other Rhode Island style: cornmeal stirred with cold milk rather than spread as a lace with boiling water. They are associated especially with cooks who want a substantial cake. The shared history with thin cakes is New England cornmeal on a griddle, built on an Indigenous crop and a colonial kitchen. The method is different enough that Rhode Island treats them as a separate cake.

Cultural background

They are breakfast, often with butter and maple syrup, and they are more filling than the thin South County cake. Milk makes them less lean.

Why it tastes this way

Cold milk does not scald the meal, so the texture stays softer and tastes of milk sugar as well as corn. A thicker pour browns outside and stays moist inside, closer to a pancake than to a cracker. Maple syrup on top is a northern New England habit that fits this thicker, milder cake better than it fits a lacy thin one.

How it is made

White cornmeal is stirred with cold milk and salt and cooked as thick, small cakes.

Authentic touch. Do not add boiling water to this version. Heat is what turns it into the thin cake. Keep the batter thick and the cakes small.

How it is used

Butter and maple syrup, or beside baked beans.

Field notes

  • Thick cakes use cold liquid. Thin cakes are scalded and spread.
  • Both are johnnycakes. Arguing about that is a Rhode Island pastime with a real basis in method.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup white cornmeal
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup cold whole milk
  • Butter or oil for the griddle

Steps

  1. Stir meal, salt, and milk into a thick batter. Rest 5 minutes.
  2. Add a spoon of milk only if it cannot leave the spoon.
  3. Cook small thick rounds on a greased medium-low griddle.
  4. Flip when the bottom is well browned.
  5. Cook until the center is no longer raw meal.
  6. Serve with butter.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A teaspoon of sugar is optional and modern.
  • Water instead of milk gives a leaner cake and removes the dairy allergen.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Side dish
  • maple syrup
  • butter
  • baked beans
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United States · No chili heat · about 8 thin cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Rhode Island Thin Johnnycakes

Crisp, lacy corn, salted, with a toasted smell.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 thin cakes

History

Johnnycakes are cornmeal griddle cakes of southern New England. Narragansett and other Algonquian peoples of the region grew and prepared corn before English settlement, and colonial cooks adopted cornmeal breads. The word’s origin is disputed: “journey cake” and a Narragansett word often given as jonikin are both proposed, and neither is settled. In Rhode Island, thin johnnycakes, especially in South County, are a local standard, spread thin and cooked slowly.

Cultural background

White flint cornmeal is the local pride when you can find it. The cakes are breakfast and a side for chowder. A state full of opinions treats the thin cake and the thick cake as different foods.

Why it tastes this way

Flint corn meal is drier and more aromatic than a soft yellow meal, which is why Rhode Island cakes taste toasted rather than sweet. A thin spread on a barely greased griddle crisps the starch. There is no wheat to soften it and no sugar to fake a dessert. The corn is the whole story, and the corn is an Indigenous crop that became colonial daily bread.

How it is made

White cornmeal and salt are scalded or stirred with water into a thin batter and cooked slowly on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Spread them thin with the back of a spoon and do not rush the flip. They tear if you turn them before the underside has actually crusted.

How it is used

Butter. Some people add maple syrup. Purists in the thin-cake camp often stop at butter.

Field notes

  • The etymology of johnnycake is not settled. This page does not pick a folk origin and call it fact.
  • Thin and thick Rhode Island cakes are local styles, not a single recipe.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup white cornmeal
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt
  • 1 cup boiling water
  • 1/4 to 1/2 cup cold water, as needed
  • Oil or bacon fat for the griddle

Steps

  1. Stir salt into the meal. Add boiling water and mix.
  2. Thin with cold water until the batter spreads.
  3. Heat a griddle over medium-low and grease it lightly.
  4. Spread thin rounds.
  5. Cook until the underside is browned and the cake releases, then flip.
  6. Serve with butter.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Thick johnnycakes, on their own page, use cold milk and a deeper cake.
  • Yellow meal works in a pinch and tastes sweeter and less flinty.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • Side dish
  • butter
  • maple syrup
  • clam chowder
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

Malaysia · No chili heat · 4 rotis

Flying Pancakes of the World

Malaysia · Southeast Asia

Roti Canai

A flaky griddled bread, stretched thin, for dal or curry.

40 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 rotis

History

Roti canai is the flaky Malaysian Indian flatbread, cousin to Indian parotta, cooked on a griddle and served with dal or curry. The dough is oiled, rested, stretched, and folded. It is a mamak-stall breakfast and a late-night food. This is the bread, not the curry.

Cultural background

A good roti canai shatters into flakes. The stretch is the skill. A home version that is merely rolled thin is honest if it does not claim circus tossing.

Why it tastes this way

Oil in a soft dough, stretched thin and folded, fries into layers on a hot griddle. That flake is why people pull the bread apart. The Indian Muslim food stalls of Malaysia made it a national breakfast. Dal on the side is savory and thin, a sauce for the bread. The bread itself is mild, salty, and rich.

How it is made

An oiled dough is rested, stretched, folded, and cooked on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Rest the dough until it is very soft. Oil the counter, stretch as thin as you can without theater, fold, and cook on a hot griddle with a little oil. Clap it so the layers open.

How it is used

With dal or a simple curry.

Field notes

  • Roti canai is defined by the stretch and the flake.
  • A home version can be rolled thin without being tossed in the air.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups flour
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons oil
  • 3/4 cup water
  • Oil for the griddle

Steps

  1. Mix a soft dough and rest 30 minutes.
  2. Oil the counter, stretch each piece thin, and fold it into a layered square.
  3. Cook on a hot oiled griddle until flaky and spotted.
  4. Clap the bread so the layers open.
  5. Serve with dal.
  6. Keep finished roti wrapped.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Roti telur has an egg broken on the bread as it cooks.
  • Trinidadian paratha is the Caribbean cousin of the same technique.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • dal
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Russia · No chili heat · about 16 blini

Flying Pancakes of the World

Russia · Eastern Europe

Russian Blini

Yeasted wheat pancakes, lightly sour, full of small holes.

1 hr 40 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 6 · about 16 blini

History

Blini are yeasted Russian pancakes. During Maslenitsa, the week before the Orthodox Great Lent, they are the festival food, tied in folk explanation to the sun and to using up butter and eggs before the fast. Maslenitsa is a real holiday on the Orthodox calendar. The sun symbolism is folk interpretation, so it is named here as that, not as doctrine. Blini are also eaten the rest of the year with butter, sour cream, herring, or caviar.

Cultural background

A risen batter makes them different from a baking-powder pancake. Toppings are rich because the week before Lent was for finishing dairy. Caviar is a famous topping and is not required.

Why it tastes this way

Yeast and a warm rest give a slight tang and a holey crumb that holds melted butter. Maslenitsa explains the butter: the fast ahead limited dairy, so the week used it. Wheat blini are milder than buckwheat ones. Sour cream and salt fish are the savory logic of a rich, mild cake. The holiday is religious and seasonal, which is why the same batter tastes like a feast in that week and like breakfast later.

How it is made

A yeasted batter of flour, milk, egg, and butter is risen twice and cooked as small pancakes.

Authentic touch. Let the batter rise until puffy. Cook the blini smaller than a dinner plate so they stay tender. Brush the cooked side with butter if you want the classic sheen.

How it is used

Butter, sour cream, smoked fish, or jam. Herring and caviar are traditional and add fish.

Field notes

  • Maslenitsa is the Orthodox pre-Lenten week, and blini are its pancake.
  • The sun story is folk symbolism, not a verified ancient rite this page can date.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups warm milk
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter, plus more for the pan

Steps

  1. Stir milk, yeast, sugar, and flour. Rest 45 minutes until bubbly.
  2. Beat in salt, egg, and melted butter. Rest 30 minutes more.
  3. Cook small pancakes on a buttered medium griddle.
  4. Flip when the surface is full of holes.
  5. Brush with butter.
  6. Serve warm with sour cream.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Buckwheat blini are the earthier Maslenitsa cousin.
  • A spoon of batter saved as a starter makes the next batch sourer.

Pairings and uses

  • Holiday table
  • Savory meal
  • Breakfast
  • butter
  • sour cream
  • jam
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · about 12 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Scotch Pancakes

Small, thick, lightly sweet cakes with a soft middle.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 12 pancakes

History

Scotch pancakes, also called drop scones, are the thick small pancakes of Scotland. They are not the thin English Shrove pancake and not a crumpet. Tea tables and breakfasts both claim them. A girdle, the Scottish griddle, is the traditional pan.

Cultural background

They are eaten warm with butter and jam or syrup. They are a batter cake, leavened with baking powder, and they cook in minutes.

Why it tastes this way

A thick spoonful on a moderate girdle sets a tender crumb and a light crust, sweeter than an oatcake and less rich than a singing hinny. Scotland’s tea table took to baking powder in the nineteenth century because it was faster than yeast for a small cake. The flavor is flour, egg, and a little sugar, with browning from the iron.

How it is made

A thick sweetened batter is dropped by the spoon onto a warm griddle.

Authentic touch. Use a girdle or a heavy skillet, not a screaming hot pancake top. Drop scones burn on the outside while the middle stays doughy if the pan is too hot.

How it is used

Butter and jam, or butter and golden syrup.

Field notes

  • Scotch pancakes and English drop scones are the same family.
  • They are thick. Shrove pancakes are the thin ones.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 large egg
  • 1/2 cup milk
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir the dry ingredients together, then stir in the wet ingredients until the batter is combined.
  2. Rest 5 minutes. The batter should hold a soft peak on the spoon.
  3. Heat a griddle or heavy skillet over medium heat and film it with butter.
  4. Spoon on the batter. Cook until the underside is browned and the top looks set.
  5. Flip once and cook the second side through.
  6. Cook until both sides are golden and the center springs back.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Irish buttermilk drop scones are the close cousin, sourer if you use buttermilk.
  • A few raisins make a fruit scone.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • butter
  • jam
  • golden syrup
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · one round, about 8 pieces

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Scottish Bannocks

A thick oat and wheat girdle bread, hearty and mild.

15 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 8 · one round, about 8 pieces

History

A bannock in Scotland is a larger girdle bread, historically oat or barley, later often wheat or a mix, cut into pieces from a round. The word traveled to Canada and the northern United States with traders, where bannock became a different baking-powder bread. This page is the Scottish girdle bread. The Canadian skillet bread has its own page and its own history.

Cultural background

It is a bread for butter, cheese, or soup. A sweet bannock with dried fruit is a festive version. This one is plain.

Why it tastes this way

Oats and a portion of wheat make a bread that is denser than a scone and moister than a thin oatcake. Cooking it as one round on a girdle is the old hearth method, from a kitchen where a pot and a griddle did the work of an oven. The flavor is grain and salt.

How it is made

Oat flour, wheat flour, baking powder, salt, fat, and water are patted into a round and cooked on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Cook it slower than oatcakes and turn it once. Thicker bread needs time. Press the top lightly. If it springs back, the center is set.

How it is used

Broken into pieces, with butter or cheese.

Field notes

  • Scottish bannock and Canadian bannock share a name and diverged.
  • A bannock is traditionally a round cut or broken to share.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup oat flour
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons oil
  • 2/3 cup water

Steps

  1. Mix the flours, baking powder, and salt. Add oil and water to a soft dough.
  2. Pat into a round about 3/4 inch thick.
  3. Cook on a medium-low griddle, turning once, until both sides are brown and the center is set.
  4. Cool slightly and break into pieces.
  5. Serve with butter.
  6. Keep the heat low so the center cooks.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Beremeal bannocks use Orkney bere barley and are on their own page.
  • Canadian bannock is a later wheat bread and should not be substituted into a Scottish story.

Pairings and uses

  • Bread course
  • Breakfast
  • butter
  • cheese
  • soup
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · one 10-inch pancake

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Seattle Dutch Baby

A puffed oven pancake, crisp at the rim, eggy in the center.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · one 10-inch pancake

History

The Dutch baby is a German oven pancake. The “Dutch” in the name is generally explained as a muddle of Deutsch, not a recipe from the Netherlands. In Seattle, Manca’s Cafe is often credited with serving it in the early twentieth century and with the name that stuck in American restaurants. David Eyre’s pancake, a related skillet pancake published later, is a cousin. The Netherlands has its own pannenkoeken, which are a different food.

Cultural background

It is baked in a hot buttered skillet and brought to the table puffed, then collapses. Lemon and powdered sugar are the usual finish. Fruit is a fair variation.

Why it tastes this way

Steam from a hot pan and a wet, eggy batter inflates the rim before the center sets. That is why it looks like a popover the size of the skillet. Butter browns the bottom. Lemon is there because the cake is rich and needs acid. The Seattle story is immigration and restaurants, not a medieval Dutch guild.

How it is made

A blender batter of egg, milk, and flour is poured into a preheated buttered skillet and baked until risen.

Authentic touch. The skillet and the oven must be hot before the batter goes in. A cold pan makes a flat custard. Serve it as soon as it leaves the oven if you want anyone to see the puff.

How it is used

Lemon juice and powdered sugar, or fruit.

Field notes

  • Dutch here is widely understood as a twist on Deutsch. The cake is German in origin.
  • Manca’s Cafe in Seattle is the restaurant usually credited with the American name. Treat that credit as the common account.

Ingredients

  • 3 large eggs
  • 2/3 cup whole milk
  • 2/3 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 3 tablespoons butter
  • Lemon and powdered sugar, to serve

Steps

  1. Heat the oven to 425°F with a 10-inch ovenproof skillet inside.
  2. Blend eggs, milk, flour, sugar, and salt until smooth.
  3. Add the butter to the hot skillet and let it melt.
  4. Pour in the batter and bake 16 to 20 minutes, until puffed and browned.
  5. Do not open the door early.
  6. Dust with sugar and lemon and serve at once.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A savory baby skips the sugar and is finished with herbs and cheese.
  • David Eyre’s version is the same family, often in a smaller skillet.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • lemon
  • powdered sugar
  • berries
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

India · No chili heat · about 12 small dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Set Dosa

Soft, thick, spongy dosas served in a stack of two or three.

40 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · about 12 small dosas

History

Set dosa is a Karnataka tiffin: small, thick, spongy dosas served as a set, usually with a vegetable curry and chutney. The batter is a dosa batter made a little thicker, sometimes with a spoon of flattened rice for softness. It is not meant to be a crisp paper dosa.

Cultural background

The set is the serving. One lonely crisp dosa is a different order. The center stays pale and tender.

Why it tastes this way

A thicker, wetter ferment steams rather than fries into a cracker, so the crumb is spongy and sour. Flattened rice, poha, if you add it, holds moisture. Karnataka tiffin rooms built a breakfast around that softness plus a coconut chutney and a potato palya. The sourness is still the ferment. The texture is the regional choice.

How it is made

A thick fermented rice-dal batter is cooked covered, as small soft dosas.

Authentic touch. Pour small thick rounds and cover the pan. Do not press them thin. They are done when the top is set and the underside is only lightly browned.

How it is used

In a stack, with potato curry and coconut chutney.

Field notes

  • Set dosa is served in a set and is supposed to be soft.
  • Covering the pan is what keeps them tender.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups fermented dosa batter, left thicker than crepe batter
  • 1 tablespoon oil
  • A pinch of salt if the batter is unsalted

Steps

  1. Ferment the batter as for plain dosa. Do not thin it as much.
  2. Pour small thick rounds on a medium-hot tawa.
  3. Cover and cook until the top sets. Flip briefly if the top is still wet.
  4. Serve two or three to a plate.
  5. They should be spongy.
  6. Chutney and a potato curry are the meal.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A handful of soaked poha ground into the batter makes them softer.
  • Paper dosa is the opposite texture.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • potato curry
  • coconut chutney
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · about 24 small pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Silver Dollar Pancakes

The same buttermilk cake as a large pancake, cooked small so the edges crisp.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 24 small pancakes

History

Silver-dollar pancakes are a size, not a separate batter. American diners, especially along the East Coast, used the name for pancakes about the diameter of the old silver dollar coin. The small round cooks faster and has more browned edge per bite. The style is twentieth-century diner cooking built on the standard buttermilk pancake.

Cultural background

Children’s plates and diner menus still use the name. The coin is the measure. A modern dollar coin is not the traditional reference.

Why it tastes this way

A small cake has more crust in proportion to crumb, so it tastes more browned and less cakey. That is the whole technical difference. The batter is the East Coast diner’s buttermilk batter: tangy, lightly sweet, cooked on a flat top that is already hot from eggs and bacon.

How it is made

Buttermilk pancake batter is dropped in small rounds on a hot griddle.

Authentic touch. Use a scant tablespoon of batter and do not spread it. If you spread it, you get a crepe, not a silver dollar.

How it is used

A pile of them with butter and syrup. They cool fast, so serve the plate as soon as the last cake flips.

Field notes

  • The name refers to the old silver dollar, a size guide, not to a different recipe.
  • Small cakes show whether the griddle is too hot. The centers should still set.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir the dry ingredients together, then stir in the wet ingredients until the batter is combined.
  2. Rest 5 minutes.
  3. Heat a griddle or heavy skillet over medium heat and film it with butter.
  4. Spoon on the batter. Cook until the underside is browned and the top looks set.
  5. Flip once and cook the second side through.
  6. Drop the batter by the tablespoon. Flip as soon as the bubbles set. They cook faster than a large cake.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Any of the fruit pancakes on this site can be cooked this size.
  • A large cake from the same batter is just a pancake. The name is the size.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • maple syrup
  • butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Nigeria · No chili heat · about 10 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Nigeria · West Africa

Sinasir

A fermented millet pancake, sour and soft, from the Hausa kitchen.

1 hr 10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 10 cakes

History

Sinasir is a Hausa fermented millet pancake, related to masa and often named separately. Millet is soaked, ground, and soured, then cooked as cakes. It is a northern Nigerian breakfast grain food. Recipes vary by town. This is a home version with millet flour and a short ferment.

Cultural background

It is eaten with sugar, honey, or a soup. The millet tastes earthier than rice masa.

Why it tastes this way

Millet is a Sahel staple that stores well in a hot, dry climate, which is why the cake tastes of millet rather than of wheat. Fermentation adds sourness and makes the flour easier to digest and more aromatic. A short ferment with a pinch of yeast is the reliable home path. A longer wild ferment is more sour.

How it is made

Millet flour is mixed with water and a little yeast, rested, and cooked as small pancakes.

Authentic touch. Rest the batter until it smells faintly sour and looks a little risen. Cook the cakes covered for a minute so the millet center sets.

How it is used

Warm, with honey or alongside a soup.

Field notes

  • Sinasir is the millet pancake. Masa is often the rice one. Households do blur the names.
  • Millet keeps the cake gluten-free.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup millet flour
  • 1 1/4 cups water
  • 1/4 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Stir millet, water, and yeast. Rest 1 hour, until slightly bubbly.
  2. Add salt.
  3. Fry small cakes in a lightly oiled pan, covered briefly, until set.
  4. Flip and brown.
  5. Serve warm.
  6. The center should not taste raw.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Masa is the rice cake in the same family.
  • Without yeast, ferment the batter 24 hours if your kitchen is warm, and watch for mold.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • honey
  • soup
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United Kingdom · No chili heat · one 8-inch round, 8 wedges

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Singing Hinnies

A rich griddle cake with currants, lard or butter, singing as it cooks.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 8 · one 8-inch round, 8 wedges

History

Singing hinnies are a Northumberland griddle cake. The “singing” is the sound of the fat on a hot griddle, and “hinny” is a local term of affection. They are a rich dough of flour, fat, and currants, cooked in a round and cut, not a poured batter. Nineteenth-century northern kitchens kept them as a tea cake.

Cultural background

They are split and buttered. The currants are the fruit. They are closer to a griddled scone than to a crepe.

Why it tastes this way

A high proportion of fat is why they sing and why they taste short, like a scone. Currants were a traded dried fruit that kept, which is how a northern tea table got sweetness without fresh fruit. The griddle, rather than an oven, browns the fat in spots and leaves the inside tender.

How it is made

Flour, baking powder, fat, currants, and a little milk are patted into a round and cooked on a griddle, then cut.

Authentic touch. Do not add liquid beyond a spoon of milk if the dough is cracking. A wet dough will not sing. It will stew. Cook over medium so the currants do not burn black.

How it is used

Split, with butter, while warm.

Field notes

  • The name comes from the sound of fat on the griddle and from “hinny,” a term of affection.
  • They are a dough cake, included because they are cooked on the griddle like the other northern breads.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1/4 cup sugar
  • 1/4 cup cold butter
  • 1/3 cup dried currants
  • 1/3 cup milk

Steps

  1. Stir flour, baking powder, salt, and sugar. Rub in the butter.
  2. Toss in the currants and add milk to a soft dough.
  3. Pat into a round about 1/2 inch thick.
  4. Cook on a medium griddle, turning once, until both sides are brown and the center is set.
  5. Cool slightly and cut into wedges.
  6. Split and butter.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • All butter instead of a mix with lard is the version written here.
  • A sweet scone baked in the oven is a relative, not a hinny.

Pairings and uses

  • Snack
  • Breakfast
  • butter
  • tea
  • Vegetarian

United States · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Sorghum Flapjacks

Mild wheat cake with the mineral sweetness of sorghum in the batter.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

In American English a flapjack is a pancake. Sorghum, pressed from sweet sorghum cane and boiled to syrup, was a common Appalachian and upper-South sweetener in the nineteenth and twentieth centuries, made on farms that could not rely on maple or imported sugar. Stirring a spoon of that syrup into the batter, and serving more at the table, is the local form of a wheat pancake.

Cultural background

The British flapjack, an oat traybake, is a different food and is not this recipe. Here the word means the griddle cake, which is how mountain cooks have long used it.

Why it tastes this way

Sorghum syrup tastes less purely sweet than maple and a little like the cane it was boiled from: mineral, faintly bitter, and dark. That flavor is the taste of a crop that ripens in a warm season and is processed in the fall. The cake itself stays plain so the syrup can be tasted twice, once inside and once on top.

How it is made

A buttermilk batter is sweetened with sorghum syrup and cooked as ordinary flapjacks, then served with more sorghum.

Authentic touch. Warm the sorghum so it blends into the milk. Cold syrup sits in a lump and burns in spots on the griddle.

How it is used

Butter and warm sorghum. They are a breakfast stack.

Field notes

  • American flapjacks are pancakes. British flapjacks are an oat bar and are not in this recipe.
  • Sorghum mills and stirring-off parties were a real part of upland farm life.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 2 tablespoons sorghum syrup, plus more to serve
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir the dry ingredients together, then stir in the wet ingredients until the batter is combined.
  2. Stir the sorghum into the buttermilk before it meets the flour, so the syrup dissolves.
  3. Heat a griddle or heavy skillet over medium heat and film it with butter.
  4. Spoon on the batter. Cook until the underside is browned and the top looks set.
  5. Flip once and cook the second side through.
  6. Serve with more warm sorghum. Do not cook the syrup on the bare griddle or it will scorch.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Molasses is darker and more bitter. It is a cousin, not the same pour.
  • Maple is a Northern swap and changes the story of the plate.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • sorghum syrup
  • butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United States · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Southern Buttermilk Pancakes

Tangy buttermilk, a soft crumb, and a browned butter edge.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Buttermilk pancakes became a Southern breakfast standard once chemical leaveners and commercial flour were ordinary pantry goods in the nineteenth and twentieth centuries. The sour milk itself is older: farms churned butter and cooked with the buttermilk that remained. A tall stack with butter is the diner and home version most people mean by a Southern pancake.

Cultural background

Sunday breakfast, church potlucks, and roadside diners all serve them. The cake is a canvas. Sorghum, cane syrup, or molasses often stands in for maple, which was never the local sugar.

Why it tastes this way

Heat and dairy explain the taste. Buttermilk’s acid reacts with baking soda, so the crumb lifts and tastes faintly sour instead of flat and sweet. Southern summers favored preserved sweetness, which is why sorghum and cane syrup, not maple, are the old pours. Wheat flour from a mill, not corn, is what makes this particular cake soft rather than gritty.

How it is made

A wheat batter of flour, egg, and buttermilk is leavened with baking powder and soda and cooked on a buttered griddle.

Authentic touch. Stop stirring when the flour disappears. A few lumps are better than a tough cake, and the batter should rest about five minutes so the soda starts working before it hits the griddle.

How it is used

Stack them with butter and sorghum, cane syrup, or honey. Bacon or sausage on the side is the usual plate.

Field notes

  • Maple syrup is a later pour in much of the South. Sorghum and cane syrup are the older local sweets.
  • The soda is there because buttermilk is acidic. Sweet milk needs a different balance.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/4 teaspoon fine salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Stir the dry ingredients together, then stir in the wet ingredients until the batter is combined.
  2. Let the batter rest 5 minutes. It should be thick enough to mound slightly.
  3. Heat a griddle or heavy skillet over medium heat and film it with butter.
  4. Spoon on the batter. Cook until the underside is browned and the top looks set.
  5. Flip once and cook the second side through.
  6. Serve at once with butter and syrup.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoonful of cornmeal in the batter leans toward a corn griddle cake.
  • Self-rising flour can replace the baking powder if you cut the salt and powder, but keep the soda for the buttermilk.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • sorghum syrup
  • cane syrup
  • butter
  • sausage
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Spain · No chili heat · about 10 tortitas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Spain · Southern Europe

Spanish Tortitas

Small, thick Spanish pancakes, milder than a carnival crepe.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 10 tortitas

History

Tortitas are the word many Spanish kitchens use for small thick pancakes, as opposed to filloas or the potato omelette called tortilla española. They are a breakfast or a children’s snack with sugar, honey, or chocolate. They are not the potato tortilla, which is an omelette and is not given a false pancake recipe here.

Cultural background

Baking powder makes them thick. They are closer to drop scones than to filloas. Chocolate a la taza is a fair partner and is a separate drink.

Why it tastes this way

A thicker batter tastes more of egg and sugar than a carnival crepe, a modern home breakfast rather than a Lenten custom. The word tortita is the clue. Tortilla, in Spain, usually means the potato omelette. Keeping the names apart avoids a real confusion.

How it is made

A thick sweetened batter is cooked as small pancakes.

Authentic touch. Drop them small and thick. Flip when the bubbles hold. They are not meant to be paper-thin.

How it is used

With honey, sugar, or jam.

Field notes

  • Tortitas are thick pancakes. Tortilla española is a potato omelette.
  • The names are kept apart on purpose.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 egg
  • 3/4 cup milk
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter

Steps

  1. Mix a thick batter.
  2. Cook small thick rounds on a buttered griddle.
  3. Flip when the bubbles hold.
  4. Serve with honey or jam.
  5. They are not crepes.
  6. Keep the heat at medium.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Filloas are the thin carnival crepes.
  • Tortilla española is a potato omelette, not this recipe.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • honey
  • jam
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Netherlands · No chili heat · one large cake

Flying Pancakes of the World

Netherlands · Western Europe

Spekdik

A thick bacon pancake, salty and substantial.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · one large cake

History

Spekdik is a bacon pancake from the northeast of the Netherlands, especially Drenthe and Groningen. Pieces of spek, pork belly or bacon, are cooked in the batter so the fat flavors the cake. It is a regional farm dish, heartier than a city crêpe, and it is still served at local events and at home.

Cultural background

It is a meal, not a dessert. Syrup on a corner of the plate is a Dutch habit even with bacon, and some people do that. Others keep it savory.

Why it tastes this way

Pork fat from the bacon bastes the batter as it cooks, which is why the crumb tastes salty and rich without a lot of butter. The northeast’s farming table had pork and flour and a pan big enough for one thick cake. The thickness is the difference from a thin pannenkoek: spekdik is meant to be filling in cold, flat country.

How it is made

Bacon is set in a pan, a thick batter is poured over, and the cake is cooked slowly, then flipped.

Authentic touch. Start the bacon in the pan so some fat renders, then pour the batter over it. If the bacon goes in raw on top of a thick batter, it stays pale.

How it is used

Cut into wedges. Mustard or syrup, depending on the house.

Field notes

  • Spekdik belongs to Drenthe and Groningen, not to every Dutch pancake menu.
  • It is a bacon dish. The plain pannenkoek page is the meatless cake.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 cup milk
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 4 ounces bacon, cut into pieces

Steps

  1. Whisk flour, egg, milk, and salt into a thick batter.
  2. Cook the bacon in a skillet until it starts to brown.
  3. Pour the batter over the bacon and spread it.
  4. Cook covered over medium-low until the top is set.
  5. Flip carefully and cook until the center is done.
  6. Cut into wedges.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A plain pannenkoek with bacon on the side is not quite spekdik. The meat should be in the cake.
  • Thick-cut bacon works better than paper-thin slices.

Pairings and uses

  • Supper
  • Savory meal
  • Breakfast
  • mustard
  • syrup
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

Sri Lanka · No chili heat · about 8 hoppers

Flying Pancakes of the World

Sri Lanka · South Asia

Sri Lankan Hoppers

A bowl-shaped rice and coconut pancake with a lacy edge.

40 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 hoppers

History

Hoppers, aappa, are Sri Lanka’s bowl-shaped fermented rice pancakes, close kin to Kerala appam. Coconut milk is usual. An egg hopper, with an egg broken into the center, is a common breakfast and adds egg. This recipe is the plain hopper, vegan, with the egg noted as a variation. They are cooked in a small curved pan.

Cultural background

Hoppers are eaten with sambol and curries. The center is soft. The rim is crisp. Yeast or a ferment starts the batter.

Why it tastes this way

Coconut milk and a curved pan are the flavor and the shape. The rim crisps because it is thin. The center stays creamy because the batter pools. Rice fermentation gives a slight sourness that matches a spicy sambol. The island and Kerala share the technique across the water. Egg in the middle is optional richness, not required for the bread to be a hopper.

How it is made

Fermented rice and coconut batter is swirled in a hopper pan and cooked covered.

Authentic touch. Swirl and cover, as with appam. Wipe the pan with oil between hoppers. If the rim burns, the pan was empty of batter at the top or the heat was too high.

How it is used

With sambol or curry. An egg in the center is the variation.

Field notes

  • Plain hoppers and egg hoppers are both common. This batter is the plain one.
  • The curved pan makes the bowl. A flat tawa will not.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup rice, soaked 4 hours
  • 1/2 cup cooked rice
  • 3/4 cup coconut milk
  • 1/4 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt

Steps

  1. Grind soaked rice and cooked rice smooth. Stir in coconut milk, yeast, sugar, and salt.
  2. Ferment 8 to 12 hours until bubbly.
  3. Oil a hot hopper pan or appachatti.
  4. Swirl a ladle of batter and cover until the center is set and the rim is crisp.
  5. Do not flip.
  6. Serve with sambol or curry.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Crack an egg into the center for an egg hopper and cover until the white sets.
  • Kerala appam is the mainland cousin.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • coconut sambol
  • curry
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · about 6 large oatcakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Staffordshire Oatcakes

Soft, savory oat pancakes, meant to be filled and rolled.

1 hr 10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 6 · about 6 large oatcakes

History

Staffordshire oatcakes are yeasted oat pancakes from the Potteries, especially Stoke-on-Trent. Oatcake shops still cook them on a griddle and sell them filled with bacon and cheese. They are a local daily bread, not a sweet Scottish biscuit, though both are called oatcakes. The yeast and the size, large and flexible, are the Staffordshire traits.

Cultural background

A typical shop filling is bacon, sausage, or cheese. The oatcake itself is the wrapper. This recipe is the pancake, with cheese as a meatless way to serve it.

Why it tastes this way

Oats, water, and a little wheat flour, fermented briefly, taste savory and slightly sour, which is why they want cheese or something salty rather than lemon sugar. The Potteries were an industrial district where a cheap, filling griddle bread sold from a shop window made sense. The yeast keeps them pliable enough to roll, unlike a crisp Highland oatcake.

How it is made

A yeast batter of oat flour, wheat flour, and water is rested, then cooked as large thin pancakes.

Authentic touch. Cook them thin and pale-gold, not dark. A flexible oatcake rolls. A crisp one cracks and becomes the other British oatcake.

How it is used

Warm, with cheese, or with bacon if you eat meat. Roll them while they are flexible.

Field notes

  • Staffordshire oatcakes are soft and usually savory. Scottish oatcakes are often a crisp bread.
  • Oatcake shops in Stoke-on-Trent are still the local way to buy them.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup oat flour
  • 1/2 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/2 cups warm water
  • Oil for the griddle

Steps

  1. Stir the flours, yeast, salt, and water into a thin batter. Rest 1 hour.
  2. Heat a lightly oiled griddle over medium.
  3. Pour a thin round and cook until the top sets.
  4. Flip and cook briefly.
  5. Keep them soft under a towel.
  6. Fill with cheese and roll while warm.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Derbyshire oatcakes are the neighboring county’s version.
  • A fully oat batter without wheat is more fragile and is gluten-free only if the oats are certified.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • cheddar
  • bacon
  • eggs
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Norway · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Norway · Northern Europe

Svele

A thick, lightly sweet cake, split and spread with brown cheese or jam.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Svele are thick Norwegian pancakes, especially associated with western Norway and with ferry kiosks, where they are sold as a snack. They are thicker than a crêpe and are often split and filled with brunost, the brown whey cheese, or with jam. The ferry svele is a real part of modern Norwegian travel food.

Cultural background

Brunost is salty-sweet and tastes of caramelized whey. Jam is the other filling. The cake is plain so the filling can be strong.

Why it tastes this way

A thick batter with a little sugar and spice, often cardamom, tastes like a soft bun that was cooked on a griddle instead of baked. Brown cheese is made by boiling whey until the milk sugar caramelizes, which is why it tastes sweet and savory together. On a ferry, a cake that can be wrapped and eaten one-handed makes sense. Cardamom is the Norwegian baking spice.

How it is made

A thick batter is cooked as palm-sized cakes and split for a filling.

Authentic touch. Cook them fully. A thick svele is gummy in the center if you flip by color alone. Split them while warm if you are filling them.

How it is used

With brunost or jam. Butter is enough if you have neither.

Field notes

  • Ferry kiosks helped keep svele in everyday Norwegian life.
  • Brunost is brown whey cheese, not a dyed cheddar.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1/2 teaspoon cardamom
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 cup milk
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter

Steps

  1. Whisk a thick batter.
  2. Cook cakes on a buttered medium griddle until both sides are brown and the center is set.
  3. Cool slightly and split.
  4. Fill with butter and brunost or jam.
  5. Serve warm.
  6. They dry out if you cook them too long after the center is done.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Cardamom is traditional in many batters. Leave it out and they are plainer.
  • Lefse is a different Norwegian potato bread.

Pairings and uses

  • Snack
  • Breakfast
  • brunost
  • jam
  • butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free
