United States · No chili heat · about 10 thin pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Swedish American Pancakes

Thin, eggy pancakes, often rolled, with lingonberry or butter and sugar.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 10 thin pancakes

History

Swedish immigrants in Minnesota, Wisconsin, and neighboring states kept the thin pancake, pannkaka, and the even smaller plättar. Church suppers and lodge breakfasts made “Swedish pancakes” a Midwestern event: stacks of thin cakes, lingonberry or strawberry preserves, and coffee. The batter is the Swedish one. The scale is American community cooking.

Cultural background

A special pan with round wells makes plättar. A ordinary skillet makes larger thin pancakes that are rolled. Both appear at the same suppers.

Why it tastes this way

More egg and a thinner batter than a fluffy American pancake give a crepe-like bite that rolls without breaking. Lingonberries are the tart northern fruit Swedes preserved, and imported preserves kept that taste on the prairie when the berry itself did not grow in the church kitchen. Butter and sugar, the other topping, is the plain home version.

How it is made

A thin egg batter is cooked like crepes, then rolled or stacked with preserves.

Authentic touch. The first cake will tell you if the pan is hot enough. It should set within a minute and come free in one piece. Thin the batter with a splash of milk if it will not spread.

How it is used

Lingonberry preserves, or butter and sugar. Whipped cream is a festive addition.

Field notes

  • Church-basement Swedish pancakes are a real Upper Midwest institution.
  • Lingonberry is traditional. Strawberry jam is the common substitute and should be called that.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1 1/2 cups milk
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • Lingonberry preserves, to serve

Steps

  1. Whisk everything except the preserves into a thin batter. Rest 10 minutes.
  2. Butter a skillet over medium-high.
  3. Pour a thin layer, swirl, and cook until the top looks dry.
  4. Flip briefly.
  5. Roll with preserves or stack with butter and sugar.
  6. Serve warm.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A plättar pan makes the smaller cakes. The batter is the same.
  • Swedish pancakes in Sweden are on their own page. This one is the Upper Midwest supper version.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • Holiday table
  • lingonberry preserves
  • butter
  • sugar
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

Sweden · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Sweden · Northern Europe

Swedish Pannkakor

Thin oven or skillet pancakes, soft enough to roll.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Pannkakor are Sweden’s thin pancakes, cooked in a skillet or baked as a sheet. Rolled with jam, they are everyday dessert, the meal after pea soup on Thursdays in a tradition many Swedes still know. Lingonberry is the jam most often named. The Thursday pea-soup custom is a real Swedish weekly pattern, and the pancake is its usual ending.

Cultural background

A skillet makes individual cakes. A buttered oven pan makes one sheet to cut. Both are pannkakor. This recipe is the skillet cake.

Why it tastes this way

Egg and milk outweigh the flour, so the cake bends around jam instead of breaking. The Thursday habit ties the taste of pancake to pea soup and mustard, a savory meal finished with something mild and sweet. Lingonberry’s tartness is the forest. Sugar alone is the plainer home finish.

How it is made

A thin batter is cooked like crêpes in a buttered skillet.

Authentic touch. Roll them while they are warm. A cold pancake cracks along the jam.

How it is used

Rolled with lingonberry jam, or with whipped cream on a Thursday.

Field notes

  • Thursday pea soup followed by pancakes is a familiar Swedish weekly meal.
  • The cakes are thin and meant to roll.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 2 cups milk
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter
  • Lingonberry jam, to serve

Steps

  1. Whisk the batter smooth.
  2. Cook thin pancakes in a buttered skillet over medium.
  3. Flip once.
  4. Spread with jam and roll.
  5. Serve warm.
  6. A spoon of cream is optional.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Ugnspannkaka is the baked sheet.
  • Plättar are the tiny ones.

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • lingonberry jam
  • whipped cream
  • pea soup
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Sweden · No chili heat · about 30 small pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Sweden · Northern Europe

Swedish Plättar

Tiny round pancakes from a special iron, with jam or berries.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 30 small pancakes

History

Plättar are small Swedish pancakes cooked in a plättpanna, a pan with several round wells. They are the miniature form of the Swedish pancake and a cousin of the larger pannkakor. Lingonberry jam is the classic partner. The Upper Midwest church-supper pancake is the emigrant version of this batter.

Cultural background

They are a weeknight dessert or a weekend breakfast. Without the special pan, a spoon of batter in a small skillet makes one cake at a time and is less charming and still the recipe.

Why it tastes this way

A thin, eggy batter cooks into a tender coin because the well is small and hot. Lingonberries are the tart forest fruit preserved as jam, and their acid is why a rich little cake does not taste cloying. Butter in the wells browns the edges. The pan, not a different flour, is what separates plättar from a plate-sized pancake.

How it is made

A thin egg batter is cooked in a plättpanna or æbleskiver-style pan and served with jam.

Authentic touch. Butter each well, not just the first one. The cakes stick the moment the fat is gone. Flip them with a skewer when the tops look dry.

How it is used

Lingonberry jam, or butter and sugar.

Field notes

  • The plättpanna is the traditional tool.
  • Lingonberry jam is the usual topping. Cranberry is a cousin, not the same berry.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1 1/2 cups milk
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter
  • Lingonberry jam, to serve

Steps

  1. Whisk a thin batter and rest 10 minutes.
  2. Butter the wells of a plättpanna over medium heat.
  3. Fill each well with a thin layer.
  4. Flip when the tops set.
  5. Serve with lingonberry jam.
  6. Keep them warm in a low oven if you are cooking many.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Swedish pannkakor are the larger thin pancakes.
  • Swedish American pancakes are the same idea at a Midwestern supper.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • lingonberry jam
  • butter
  • sugar
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United States · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Sweet Potato Pancakes

Roasted sweet potato, warm spice, and a soft wheat crumb.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Sweet potatoes have been a Southern and Appalachian staple for generations, grown in gardens where they stored well through winter. Pancakes that mash the cooked potato into a wheat batter are a home breakfast built on that pantry, not a single famous inventor’s recipe. The potato’s natural sweetness means the cake needs little sugar.

Cultural background

Families use leftover baked sweet potatoes. Cinnamon or nutmeg is common. The cake sits between a vegetable pancake and a wheat breakfast cake.

Why it tastes this way

Sweet potatoes store through a cool Appalachian winter and a humid Southern one, which is why they show up in breads as well as on the plate. Their moisture loosens the batter and their sugars brown fast, so the griddle has to stay moderate. Spice is optional. The potato is the flavor.

How it is made

Mashed roasted sweet potato is folded into a buttermilk pancake batter and cooked on a moderate griddle.

Authentic touch. Use a cooked, dry sweet potato, not a can of pie filling. Pie filling is already sweetened and spiced and will scorch.

How it is used

Butter and a light pour of sorghum are enough. Pecans on top are a Southern option and add a tree-nut allergen.

Field notes

  • A baked potato that has sat uncovered mashes in more cleanly than a steaming wet one.
  • This is a breakfast use of a staple crop, not a claim of one county’s invention.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup mashed roasted sweet potato
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon ground cinnamon
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir the dry ingredients together, then stir in the wet ingredients until the batter is combined.
  2. Fold the mashed sweet potato into the wet ingredients before they meet the flour. Rest 5 minutes.
  3. Heat a griddle or heavy skillet over medium heat and film it with butter.
  4. Spoon on the batter. Cook until the underside is browned and the top looks set.
  5. Flip once and cook the second side through.
  6. Serve warm. The centers should be set, not wet.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Skip the cinnamon if you want the potato to taste like itself.
  • Orange zest is a later idea. It is pleasant and not traditional.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • sorghum
  • butter
  • pecans
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

Russia · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Russia · Eastern Europe

Syrniki

Pan-fried farmer-cheese cakes, lightly sweet.

15 min prep · 12 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Syrniki are fried cakes of tvorog, the farmer cheese of Russia and Ukraine, bound with egg and a little flour. The name comes from syr, for the cheese. They are a breakfast across the region, served with sour cream, jam, or honey. They are not blintzes. The cheese is the cake, not a filling.

Cultural background

Tvorog is drier than many supermarket ricottas. If you use ricotta, drain it overnight or the cakes will not hold. That is a substitute, and it should be drained.

Why it tastes this way

Tvorog tastes mildly sour and milky, which is the flavor under the browned crust. A little sugar makes them breakfast-sweet without turning them into dessert pastries. Flour is only the binder. The cheese is there because dairy regions kept tvorog as a way to use milk. Sour cream on top doubles the dairy and softens the crust.

How it is made

Farmer cheese, egg, sugar, and a little flour are shaped into thick patties and fried.

Authentic touch. Drain the cheese until it is crumbly, not wet. Flour your hands. Fry on medium-low so the center heats before the crust blackens.

How it is used

Sour cream and jam or honey.

Field notes

  • Syrniki are cheese cakes, in the literal sense, not a New York cheesecake.
  • Wet ricotta must be drained or the patties collapse.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup well-drained tvorog or farmer cheese
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/4 cup flour, plus more for shaping
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • Oil or butter for frying

Steps

  1. Mash the cheese with egg, sugar, salt, and flour.
  2. Shape thick patties with floured hands.
  3. Fry over medium-low until deep golden on both sides and hot in the center.
  4. Serve with sour cream.
  5. They should not ooze liquid. If they do, the cheese was too wet.
  6. A few raisins can be mixed in.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Raisins in the cheese are common.
  • Ukrainian and Russian breakfasts both claim syrniki. The cake is shared.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Dessert
  • sour cream
  • jam
  • honey
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Taiwan · No chili heat · 2 rolls

Flying Pancakes of the World

Taiwan · East Asia

Taiwanese Danbing

A thin breakfast crepe with egg, scallion, and a salty-sweet sauce.

10 min prep · 10 min cook · Serves 2 · 2 rolls

History

Danbing is a Taiwanese breakfast crepe: a thin batter cooked on a griddle with egg, often a cruller or youtiao inside, and a savory sauce. Breakfast shops cook them to order. It is related to northern jianbing and is its own breakfast-shop food. This version uses a wheat crepe, egg, and sauce, with the cruller as an optional variation.

Cultural background

It is wrapped and eaten with soy milk at a breakfast shop. The crepe is soft-crisp. The egg is fully cooked.

Why it tastes this way

Egg on a thin crepe is the protein of a fast breakfast, and the sauce, often a sweet soy or a miso-like bean paste, makes it salty-sweet. Scallion is the fresh note. Taiwanese breakfast shops standardized the wrap. Wheat batter is what those shops usually pour. The speed of the griddle is why the edge crisps before the egg dries out.

How it is made

A thin batter is cooked with egg and scallion, sauced, and rolled.

Authentic touch. Spread the batter thin, add the egg at once, and roll the crepe as soon as the egg sets. Overcooking makes a stiff tube.

How it is used

Hot, as breakfast.

Field notes

  • Danbing is a Taiwanese breakfast-shop crepe.
  • The egg should be cooked through in the roll.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup flour
  • 1/2 cup water
  • 2 eggs
  • 2 scallions, sliced
  • 1 tablespoon soy sauce
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Whisk flour and water. Stir soy and sugar together.
  2. Cook a thin crepe in an oiled pan.
  3. Pour a beaten egg on it with scallion and cook until the egg sets.
  4. Brush with the soy-sugar mix and roll.
  5. Repeat.
  6. Serve immediately.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A fried cruller inside is the fuller shop version.
  • Jianbing is the northern cousin, often with a cracker and cilantro.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • soy milk
  • scallion
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 4 scones

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Tattie Scones

Mild potato flatbread, girdled in spots.

15 min prep · 12 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 scones

History

Tattie scones are Scottish potato farls: mashed potato and flour, rolled, cut, and cooked on a girdle. They are a breakfast bread in a Scottish fry and a close cousin of Irish potato farls. “Tattie” is potato. They became common once potatoes were a Scottish staple.

Cultural background

They are best hot, with butter. In a fry they sit beside eggs and sausage. The scone itself need not include meat or egg.

Why it tastes this way

Potato makes the bread moist and mildly sweet. Flour makes it hold. A girdle dries the surface in brown spots without an oven. Scotland’s adoption of the potato, as in Ireland, turned a staple into a morning bread rather than only a boiled side.

How it is made

Plain mashed potato and flour are rolled, quartered, and cooked on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Mash the potatoes without milk or butter if you want them vegan, and keep them dry. Roll just thick enough that they do not tear, and cook them through so the flour does not taste raw.

How it is used

Butter, or as part of a breakfast fry.

Field notes

  • Tattie means potato.
  • They are a flatbread, not a fluffy oven scone, despite the word scone.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups plain warm mashed potato
  • 1/2 cup all-purpose flour, plus more for rolling
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt

Steps

  1. Mix mash, flour, and salt.
  2. Roll into a round and cut into quarters.
  3. Cook on a medium dry girdle until spotted on both sides.
  4. Eat hot with butter.
  5. Leftovers can be fried in a little fat the next morning.
  6. Wet mash makes them stick. Dry the potatoes in the pan first.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A little butter in the dough is richer and adds dairy.
  • Irish potato farls are the sister bread.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • butter
  • eggs
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

India · Mild · 4 rounds

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Thalipeeth

A multigrain savory pancake with onion, cilantro, and spice, pan-roasted.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 rounds

History

Thalipeeth is a Maharashtrian savory flatbread made from bhajani, a roasted multigrain flour that often includes wheat, sorghum, chickpea, and other grains. It is mixed with onion, cilantro, and spices and roasted on a tawa. Because bhajani usually contains wheat, this version is not gluten-free. It is a breakfast with yogurt or pickle.

Cultural background

A hole poked in the center helps it cook. It is thicker than a cheela and more breadlike. House flours vary. This recipe uses a workable mix you can assemble.

Why it tastes this way

Roasted flours taste nutty, which is why thalipeeth is more aromatic than a single-flour pancake. Onion and cilantro keep it savory. The multigrain mix is the Maharashtrian pantry, a way to use several local grains in one bread. Yogurt on the side is cool and tangy. Wheat in the mix is why it holds together and why it is not a dosa.

How it is made

A soft dough of mixed flours, onion, and spice is patted into rounds and cooked on a tawa with oil.

Authentic touch. Pat the dough onto a wet cloth or parchment if it sticks to your hands, then invert it onto the tawa. Poke a hole in the middle and add a few drops of oil there.

How it is used

With yogurt or pickle. Yogurt adds dairy. The bread is vegan.

Field notes

  • Thalipeeth is a multigrain Maharashtrian bread, not a crepe.
  • The center hole and the oil are how cooks help it roast through.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup whole wheat flour
  • 1/4 cup besan
  • 1/4 cup sorghum flour or additional wheat flour
  • 1 small onion, finely chopped
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water, to bind
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Mix the flours, onion, cilantro, chile, cumin, and salt. Add water to a soft dough.
  2. Pat four rounds, each with a small hole in the center.
  3. Cook on a medium oiled tawa, adding a few drops of oil in the hole and around the edge.
  4. Turn and cook until both sides are spotted and the center is done.
  5. Serve with yogurt or pickle.
  6. Keep the dough soft, not stiff.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Using only sorghum and besan can reduce wheat. Read the flours. This written mix includes wheat.
  • A store-bought bhajani flour is the traditional shortcut if you can get it.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • yogurt
  • pickle
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Ecuador · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ecuador · South America

Tortilla de Verde

Green plantain cakes, griddled or pan-fried, salty and starchy.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Tortillas de verde are Ecuadorian cakes of mashed green plantain, cooked on a griddle, a coastal staple different from the highland potato llapingacho. Green plantain is starchy, not sweet. The cakes are a bread beside fish or eggs.

Cultural background

The plantain is boiled, mashed, salted, and shaped. Some versions add cheese. This one does not, so it stays vegan.

Why it tastes this way

Green plantain is starch, which is why the cake tastes savory and filling rather than like a ripe-plantain tatale. The coast grows the plantain. A griddle toasts it. Salt is the seasoning. It is a bread in a plantain country, the way a potato cake is a bread in the highlands.

How it is made

Boiled green plantains are mashed with salt and pan-cooked as small cakes.

Authentic touch. Boil the plantains until they are truly soft, mash them while warm, and salt them. A stiff mash fries cleanly. A wet one sticks.

How it is used

Warm, as a side.

Field notes

  • Green plantain is starch. Ripe plantain would be a different, sweet cake.
  • These are a coastal bread, not the highland potato cakes.

Ingredients

  • 3 green plantains, boiled until soft
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Mash the warm plantains with salt until smooth.
  2. Shape small cakes.
  3. Pan-cook in a little oil until both sides are browned and the center is hot.
  4. Serve as a side.
  5. Green plantains only.
  6. A wet mash will stick. Dry it in the pan first.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Cheese in the mash adds dairy.
  • Ripe plantain would make a sweet cake and is not this recipe.

Pairings and uses

  • Side dish
  • Breakfast
  • eggs
  • fried fish
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Sweden · No chili heat · one 9-by-13-inch pan

Flying Pancakes of the World

Sweden · Northern Europe

Ugnspannkaka

A baked pancake slab, custardy and browned at the rim.

10 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 6 · one 9-by-13-inch pan

History

Ugnspannkaka means oven pancake. It is the Swedish batter baked in a pan instead of fried one cake at a time, close to Finnish pannukakku. It is weeknight food, cut into squares and served with jam or berries.

Cultural background

One dish feeds a household without anyone standing at the stove flipping. That practicality is the point.

Why it tastes this way

Milk and egg set in the oven into a soft center, and the edges brown where they meet the buttered pan. It tastes less fried than a skillet pancake and more like a custard bread. Jam on top supplies the acid. The method suits a cold evening: the oven heats the kitchen and the meal at once.

How it is made

A thin batter is poured into a hot buttered pan and baked until set.

Authentic touch. Bake it in a pan you have heated with butter, so the bottom fries a little. A cold glass dish makes a pale, steamed cake.

How it is used

Warm, with lingonberry or strawberry jam.

Field notes

  • The name means oven pancake.
  • It is the same batter family as skillet pannkakor, cooked in one pan.

Ingredients

  • 3 large eggs
  • 2 cups milk
  • 1 cup flour
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 3 tablespoons butter

Steps

  1. Heat the oven to 425°F. Melt the butter in a baking pan in the oven.
  2. Whisk eggs, milk, flour, sugar, and salt.
  3. Pour into the hot pan.
  4. Bake about 25 minutes until set and browned at the edges.
  5. Cut into squares.
  6. Serve with jam.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Finnish pannukakku is the close cousin, often with cardamom.
  • A handful of berries in the batter is a summer change.

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • lingonberry jam
  • berries
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 8 oatcakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Ulster Oatcakes

Toasted oats, salt, and a dry crisp or a chewy round, depending on thickness.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 oatcakes

History

Oatcakes are a shared tradition of Ireland and Scotland. In Ulster they are a griddle bread of oats, fat, and hot water, eaten with butter, cheese, or a fry. Oats suited a cool, wet climate better than wheat for a long time. The Ulster cake sits between a Scottish oatcake and the other breads of an Ulster breakfast.

Cultural background

They can be thin and crisp or a little thicker. This version is a griddled round, not a sweet biscuit.

Why it tastes this way

Oats taste of the grain’s own oil, which is why a good oatcake smells nutty without any nut in it. Hot water softens the meal so it binds. The climate of the north, cool and damp, is why oats were the reliable crop and why the bread tastes of them instead of wheat.

How it is made

Oatmeal, a little flour, salt, fat, and hot water are rolled and cooked on a dry griddle.

Authentic touch. Roll them thin if you want a cracker, thicker if you want a bread. Either way, cook them slowly. Oats burn before they taste toasted if the griddle is roaring.

How it is used

Butter, cheese, or alongside soup. Honey is a later sweet use.

Field notes

  • A little wheat flour helps them roll. All-oat versions exist and are more fragile.
  • Ulster oatcakes and Scottish oatcakes are relatives cooked on the same kind of griddle.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups oat flour or finely ground oats
  • 1/4 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons oil or melted butter
  • 1/3 cup hot water, plus more if needed

Steps

  1. Mix the oats, flour, and salt. Stir in the fat and hot water to a dough.
  2. Roll thin and cut rounds.
  3. Cook on a dry medium-low griddle until the edges lift and both sides are faintly brown.
  4. Cool on a rack if you want them crisp.
  5. Serve with butter or cheese.
  6. Keep them dry. They soften in a tin that is not sealed.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Scottish oatcakes on their own page are the close cousin.
  • Fine oat flour makes a smoother cake. Pinhead oats need a longer soak and a coarser bite.

Pairings and uses

  • Bread course
  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • butter
  • cheese
  • soup
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · one 9-by-13-inch pan

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Upper Peninsula Pannukakku

A baked custard pancake, browned at the edge and creamy in the center.

10 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 8 · one 9-by-13-inch pan

History

Finnish immigrants settled in Michigan’s Upper Peninsula and in northern Minnesota to work mines and logging. They brought pannukakku, an oven pancake closer to a custard than to a flipped stack. In the Upper Peninsula it is still a community and home breakfast, sometimes baked in a large pan for a crowd. The Finnish original has its own page. This is the Upper Midwest pan.

Cultural background

It is cut into squares and served with butter, jam, or cardamom sugar. Cardamom is a Finnish baking spice that survived the move.

Why it tastes this way

An oven pancake puffs at the edge and stays custardy because it is mostly milk and egg, with just enough flour to set. That texture fits a cold climate breakfast: one pan, no standing at a griddle, and a dish that feeds a household. Cardamom came into Finnish baking through trade and stayed in American Finnish kitchens as the smell of holiday bread and of this pancake.

How it is made

A thin batter of milk, egg, flour, and sugar is poured into a hot buttered pan and baked.

Authentic touch. Bake it in a preheated buttered pan so the edges fry. If the pan is cold, the bottom steams and turns gray.

How it is used

Butter and jam, or sugar and a pinch of cardamom.

Field notes

  • Finnish settlement in the Upper Peninsula is why this oven pancake is a regional American breakfast.
  • Cardamom is a traditional Finnish baking spice, not a random addition.

Ingredients

  • 3 large eggs
  • 2 cups milk
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1/4 cup sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1/2 teaspoon ground cardamom
  • 3 tablespoons butter

Steps

  1. Heat the oven to 425°F. Put the butter in a 9-by-13-inch pan and melt it in the oven.
  2. Whisk eggs, milk, flour, sugar, salt, and cardamom into a smooth batter.
  3. Pour the batter into the hot buttered pan.
  4. Bake about 25 to 30 minutes, until puffed and browned at the edges.
  5. It will settle when cut.
  6. Serve warm with jam.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • The Finnish pannukakku page is the same family baked at home in Finland.
  • A handful of berries in the pan is a summer change.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Holiday table
  • jam
  • butter
  • cardamom sugar
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

India · Mild · about 6 uttapams

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Uttapam

A thick, savory dosa pancake with onion and chile cooked into the top.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 3 · about 6 uttapams

History

Uttapam is a thick pancake made from dosa batter, topped with onion, tomato, chile, and coriander, and cooked softer than a crisp dosa. It is a South Indian breakfast and a way to use batter that has fermented a little longer than you meant. The toppings are pressed into the wet surface.

Cultural background

It is eaten with chutney. It is not folded around potato. The vegetables are the face of the cake.

Why it tastes this way

A thicker pour steams into a spongy, sour cake. Onion and green chile on top taste fresh and sharp against the fermented batter. The sourness of a well-fermented batter is an advantage here, which is why leftover dosa batter becomes uttapam. Oil around the edge crisps the rim while the center stays soft.

How it is made

Thick dosa batter is cooked covered with onion, chile, and tomato pressed into the top.

Authentic touch. Pour thick, sprinkle the toppings at once, and press them in gently. Cover the pan. Flip only if you must. Many cooks brown only the bottom.

How it is used

With coconut chutney.

Field notes

  • Uttapam is the thick savory pancake of dosa batter.
  • Leftover fermented batter is a traditional reason to cook it.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups fermented dosa batter
  • 1 small onion, chopped
  • 1 tomato, finely chopped and drained
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro
  • Oil
  • Salt

Steps

  1. Salt the vegetables lightly.
  2. Pour a thick round of batter on a medium-hot oiled tawa.
  3. Scatter onion, tomato, chile, and cilantro and press them in.
  4. Cover and cook until the top is set and the bottom is browned.
  5. Flip carefully if the top is still wet.
  6. Serve with chutney.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A plain thick uttapam without vegetables is still uttapam and less interesting.
  • Tomato makes it wetter. Pat the pieces dry.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • coconut chutney
  • sambar
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Venezuela · No chili heat · 6 arepas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Venezuela · South America

Venezuelan Arepas

Thick corn cakes of precooked maize flour, griddled, then split.

15 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 arepas

History

Arepas are a daily bread of Venezuela and Colombia, with roots in Indigenous maize cakes of the region. The modern Venezuelan arepa is usually made from precooked corn flour, masarepa, shaped into thick disks, griddled, and often finished in the oven, then split and filled. Fillings are a whole cuisine. This page is the corn cake. Reina pepiada and other meat fillings are variations, not the dough.

Cultural background

The dough should feel like clay. A griddle browns the face. A few minutes in a hot oven, if you have one, sets the center. They are split while warm.

Why it tastes this way

Precooked corn flour is a twentieth-century product that made a much older maize cake fast to cook at home. The taste is toasted corn, mild and a little sweet, a container for salty fillings. The thickness is why an arepa is not a tortilla. Venezuela’s habit of stuffing them turned the bread into breakfast and dinner.

How it is made

Masarepa and water are shaped into thick disks and cooked on a griddle, then split.

Authentic touch. Use masarepa, not masa harina for tortillas and not cornmeal. The dough should be moist and not crack. Cook the crust, then finish the center. A raw white line in the middle means they need more time.

How it is used

Split, with cheese, beans, or eggs. Meat fillings are a separate choice.

Field notes

  • Use masarepa, the precooked corn flour, not tortilla masa.
  • Stuffing them is the Venezuelan habit.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups masarepa
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 2 1/2 cups warm water
  • Oil

Steps

  1. Stir masarepa, salt, and water. Rest 5 minutes. The dough should be moist and not crack.
  2. Shape 6 thick disks.
  3. Cook on a medium griddle until a crust forms, then bake at 400°F for 10 minutes or continue on the griddle until the center is set.
  4. Split.
  5. Fill with beans, cheese, or eggs.
  6. Do not use tortilla masa harina.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Colombian arepas are often left unsplit and served beside the meal.
  • Andean arepas use wheat and are a different bread.

Pairings and uses

  • Bread course
  • Breakfast
  • Stuffed or filled
  • cheese
  • beans
  • eggs
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the field-guide page

United States · No chili heat · about 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Vermont Maple Pancakes

A plain cake whose point is real maple syrup.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 pancakes

History

Maple syrup is made by boiling the sap of maple trees, a practice of the Native nations of the northeastern woodlands long before Vermont’s sugarhouses. European-descended farmers turned sugaring into a late-winter farm season. The wheat pancake under the syrup is a later New England breakfast. Vermont’s reputation is the syrup, which is graded and sold as an agricultural product, not a flavoring.

Cultural background

Sugar-on-snow and sugarhouse breakfasts are the seasonal frame. At home, the cake is simple so the syrup can be tasted.

Why it tastes this way

Maple tastes like the tree and the boil: caramel, wood, and acid, stronger in darker grades. A very sweet batter would hide that. The climate of a cold late winter is why the sap runs at all, when nights freeze and days thaw. The pancake is only the warm bread underneath.

How it is made

A lightly sweetened buttermilk pancake is cooked plain and served with maple syrup.

Authentic touch. Warm the syrup. Cold syrup sits on a hot cake and tastes less aromatic. Use syrup you trust is maple, not pancake syrup made from corn syrup and flavoring.

How it is used

Butter and maple syrup. Sausage is common and is a separate meat dish.

Field notes

  • Pancake syrup and maple syrup are different products. This plate wants maple.
  • Indigenous sugaring in the Northeast is older than the Vermont wheat pancake.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tablespoons melted butter
  • Maple syrup, to serve

Steps

  1. Stir the dry ingredients together, then stir in the wet ingredients until the batter is combined.
  2. Rest the batter 5 minutes. Do not sweeten it like cake.
  3. Heat a griddle or heavy skillet over medium heat and film it with butter.
  4. Spoon on the batter. Cook until the underside is browned and the top looks set.
  5. Flip once and cook the second side through.
  6. Serve with warm maple syrup.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A teaspoon of maple syrup in the batter is enough if you want the flavor inside. Do not replace the table syrup with it.
  • Buckwheat cakes with maple are an older northern pairing.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • maple syrup
  • butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Virginia Corn Cakes

White cornmeal, a little sugar, and a soft crumb.

10 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Virginia cooks have used white cornmeal for breads and griddle cakes for generations. Corn was an Indigenous crop in the Chesapeake, and English colonists adopted it early. Later Virginia tables, Black and white, kept corn cakes as breakfast and as a bread beside salt-cured ham. A spoon of sugar in some house recipes marks them as breakfast rather than a purely savory hoe cake.

Cultural background

White meal is preferred in much of Virginia when it is available. The cake is thinner than a corn muffin and thicker than a cracker.

Why it tastes this way

White cornmeal tastes cleaner and less sweet than yellow meal, which is why a Virginia cake can take a little sugar and still taste like corn rather than cake. Ham on the same table is salty and smoky, so the bread stays mild. The Chesapeake’s long corn agriculture is the reason meal, not wheat, is the traditional starch.

How it is made

White cornmeal, milk, egg, and a little sugar and leavener are cooked on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Rest the batter. White meal can be fine, and a short rest keeps the center from tasting powdery.

How it is used

Butter, honey, or beside eggs and ham. The ham is a separate salty dish.

Field notes

  • White cornmeal is a Virginia preference, not a rule of chemistry.
  • A little sugar marks this as the breakfast cake rather than a hoe cake.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup white cornmeal
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking powder
  • 1/4 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup milk or buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tablespoon melted butter

Steps

  1. Stir everything together and rest 10 minutes.
  2. Cook on a buttered medium griddle.
  3. Flip when the underside browns and the top sets.
  4. Cook until the center is done.
  5. Serve hot.
  6. Leftovers split and toast well the next day.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Leave the sugar out for a bread to eat with beans.
  • Yellow meal makes a sweeter, deeper cake.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • Side dish
  • butter
  • honey
  • country ham
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Tanzania · No chili heat · about 12 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Tanzania · East Africa

Vitumbua

Coconut rice cakes, sweet and cardamom-scented, crisp outside.

1 hr 10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 6 · about 12 cakes

History

Vitumbua are Swahili coast rice-and-coconut cakes, cooked in a special pan with round wells, related in shape to appam pans and to æbleskiver pans. They are a Zanzibar and coastal Tanzanian breakfast and snack. Rice, coconut milk, sugar, and cardamom are the flavor. Yeast lifts them.

Cultural background

The coconut is the coast. Cardamom is the Indian Ocean trade. They are sweet, not savory, and they are eaten with tea.

Why it tastes this way

Coconut milk makes the crumb rich without dairy, and rice keeps it soft. Cardamom is a traded spice that became local in coastal sweets. The well pan fries the sides so the cake is crisp outside and tender inside, a shape that suits street selling. Sugar is moderate. The coconut is the sweetness people remember.

How it is made

A yeasted batter of rice flour and coconut milk is cooked in round wells.

Authentic touch. Use full-fat coconut milk. Light coconut milk makes them dry. Turn the cakes in the wells if your pan is deep, so both ends brown. An æbleskiver or appe pan is a fair tool.

How it is used

Warm, with tea. They are already sweet.

Field notes

  • Vitumbua are a Swahili coast coconut rice cake.
  • Cardamom points at Indian Ocean trade, not at a random spice.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup rice flour
  • 1 cup coconut milk
  • 2 tablespoons sugar
  • 1/4 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1/2 teaspoon cardamom
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • Oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Stir the batter and rest 1 hour until slightly risen.
  2. Oil an æbleskiver, appe, or similar well pan over medium.
  3. Cook the batter in the wells, turning, until golden and set.
  4. Serve warm.
  5. Do not use low-fat coconut milk if you can help it.
  6. They are best the day they are cooked.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A little mashed banana in the batter is a home variation.
  • Plain rice flour pancakes in a flat pan are not quite vitumbua. The wells matter.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Snack
  • tea
  • cardamom
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Yemen · No chili heat · about 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Yemen · Middle East

Yemeni Lahoh

A spongy fermented pancake, kin to Somali laxoox, eaten with ghee or honey.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · about 8 cakes

History

Lahoh is the Yemeni fermented pancake, a close relative of Somali laxoox across the Red Sea and the Gulf of Aden. Sorghum or a mix of flours is traditional. It is cooked on one side until holes form. Honey, ghee, or a stew are the partners. This version uses sorghum flour.

Cultural background

It is breakfast bread. A saved spoon of batter starts the next batch. The sourness should be gentle.

Why it tastes this way

Sorghum suits a dry climate and ferments into a mildly sour, spongy bread. The covered pan makes the holes. Trade and migration across the water explain the kinship with laxoox without making them one recipe. Ghee is the rich finish. Honey is the sweet one. The bread itself is the grain and the ferment.

How it is made

A fermented sorghum batter is cooked as small spongy pancakes on one side.

Authentic touch. Ferment until bubbly and pleasant, not harsh. Cook covered, one side only, on medium heat.

How it is used

With honey or ghee. The bread is vegan. Those toppings may not be.

Field notes

  • Lahoh and laxoox are relatives across the water.
  • One-sided cooking is the method.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup sorghum flour
  • 1 1/4 cups water
  • 1/4 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt

Steps

  1. Stir flour, water, and yeast. Ferment 8 to 24 hours until bubbly.
  2. Add salt.
  3. Cook small rounds in a covered nonstick pan until holes form. Do not flip.
  4. Serve warm.
  5. Save a spoon of batter for next time.
  6. Discard moldy batter.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Somali laxoox is the sister pancake.
  • A wheat blend is common in some kitchens and is not gluten-free.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Bread course
  • honey
  • ghee
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United Kingdom · No chili heat · about 10 pikelets

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Yorkshire Pikelets

A free-form crumpet, spongy and ready for butter.

1 hr 20 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 5 · about 10 pikelets

History

In Yorkshire a pikelet is close to a crumpet cooked without a ring, so it spreads a little and still aims for holes. In the Midlands the same word often means a thicker drop scone. The two are different cakes that share a name. This page is the Yorkshire, crumpet-like pikelet.

Cultural background

They are toasted and buttered, sometimes with jam or a slice of cheese. They are tea food and breakfast food.

Why it tastes this way

Without a ring the batter spreads, so you get more browned edge and a slightly thinner sponge. The holes still come from a wet yeast batter and a late pinch of soda. The regional confusion in the name is a reminder that British griddle words moved with bakers, not with a single law.

How it is made

A crumpet-style batter is cooked in small free-form rounds on a griddle.

Authentic touch. Pour small rounds. If you pour a large one, the center stays wet after the edge burns.

How it is used

Split or toasted whole, with butter.

Field notes

  • Yorkshire pikelet and Midlands pikelet are not the same cake.
  • Holes still matter. A solid disk means the batter was stiff.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 1 cup warm milk
  • 1/2 cup warm water
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda

Steps

  1. Mix all but the soda into a batter and rest 1 hour.
  2. Stir in the soda. The batter should flow.
  3. Cook small rounds on a medium-low greased griddle until the tops set and bubble.
  4. Flip and brown lightly.
  5. Cool, then toast.
  6. Butter generously.

Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Midlands pikelets are the thicker, drop-scone meaning of the word.
  • Rings turn these into crumpets.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • butter
  • jam
  • cheese
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free
